## PDF page 5
Preface

    he present volume is a modified version of my dissertation with
     updates and is presented in a manner for a general reader. This workToriginated prior to my enrollment in a Ph.D. program in 2019, at a time
when resources on Hilma af Klint were scarce. Over the ensuing years,
culminating in the completion of this dissertation in 2024, an increas­
ing number of publications and scholarly materials became available.
Shortly after finalizing my dissertation, I had the opportunity to connect
with members of the af Klint family, who granted me access to previously
unavailable archival documents, many of which have subtly shifted my
understanding of af Klint’s intentions and legacy.
   Among the most consequential of these materials is a 1967 letter writ­
ten by af Klint’s nephew, Erik af Klint (1901-1981), commonly referred to
now as “Erik Sr.” to distinguish him from his grandson, the current Erik
af Klint, who serves as Chairman of the Hilma af Klint Foundation and is
Hilma’s great grandnephew. This letter, titled Hilma af Klint and her work:
A short biography accompanied by a historical account of the creation and care of
the work (Appendix B), offers profound insight into the custodial respon­
sibilities Erik Sr. undertook and the spiritual and philosophical intentions
underlying the work. It has deeply informed my evolving perspective on
how af Klint’s work should be preserved, interpreted, and shared.
     Additionally, my encounter with an early article (1943) by Ragnar
Alcén, titled Sought by the Spiritual Masters and published in the Swedish
spiritual journal Sökaren (The Seeker) in 1974, revealed what may be one of
the first public attempts to investigate af Klint’s spiritual mission. Erik Sr.
mentions Ragnar, then a young researcher, as a figure whose early interest
in Hilma’s work was noteworthy.
    As is true with all scholarly inquiry, new resources and ideas continue
to emerge, offering expanded context and, at times, subtle shifts in interpre­
tation. This ongoing process is a testament to the living nature of research
and the importance of sustained engagement with primary sources.

                   Introduction
                   Precursor of a New Era
     his book explores advancing scientific knowledge and spiritual shifts
     in early modernity as artist, Hilma af Klint (1862-1944) pushed theTboundaries of ideology and technique by making the invisible, visible
in her series, Drawings to the Temple (1906-1915; see Appendix A). The
Tree of Knowledge series (1913-1915) is part of Drawings to the Temple.1
Although her Atom series was painted in 1917, after the creation of
Drawings to the Temple, af Klint believed that inspiration for the Atom
was created through spiritual channeling of the former and as an attempt
to further understand its meaning and significance.
    Her curiosity and involvement with Rosicrucianism, Theosophy,
and  later, Anthroposophy, greatly influenced her secret works that
brought together spiritualism and science. Until recently, in the twenty-
first century, Wassily Kandinsky (1866-1944) was widely considered the
first abstract artist. As curator of Hilma af Klint: Paintings for the Future,
Tracey Bashkoff of the Solomon R. Guggenheim Museum, states: “Hilma
af Klint’s abstract work predates the work by artists such as Kandinsky,
Mondrian, Kupka, Malevich, artists that we have long considered the
pioneers of abstraction. She begins working in an abstract mode as early
as 1906.”2 This book explores the parallels of science, esoteric mysticism,
and the creative process by analyzing the works of af Klint, advocating
for her long-delayed recognition as the trailblazer of abstraction.
    Although af Klint requested that her notebooks and abstract works
remain hidden until at least twenty years after her death because she
believed her audience did not yet exist, it is unfathomable for her to

                            1


## PDF page 8
2                      Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


be continuously excluded from art history texts even long after being
discovered.3 Unfortunately, several factors worked against af Klint dur­
ing her life: she was a woman, she had little contact with the art world,
and she was a medium who believed her work moved through her as a
conduit of a higher consciousness. While she was alive, she worked in
obscurity on a Swedish island. Today, her works are held by descendants
and therefore unrecognized by many collectors. Af Klint’s work is not
meant for commodity, but rather to teach humanity about the invisible
spiritual world. This fact still creates resistance by many in the art world.
While her spiritually focused male contemporaries were viewed as artis­
tic geniuses, she was and is often seen by many as disconnected from
both reality and the art world. Due to the hesitation of some historians
in adding af Klint to the art history narrative, this research is crucial in
arguing for her right of acknowledgment as her work has largely been
minimized or overlooked by many historians.
    The basis of this work is threefold: First, this study provides an
analysis of the artistic process of Hilma af Klint to understand better her
interpretation of the technological and scientific advances at the turn of
the twentieth century. Second, it explores the cultural and personal spiri­
tual shifts that influenced the purpose and overall aesthetic of her works
of art, visually connecting science and spiritualism. Finally, it examines
her work in the context of her artistic contemporaries—many of whom,
claimed to be the pioneers of abstraction. As a whole, this work discusses
how new scientific discoveries, in particular research regarding the atom,
opened a world of possibilities for scientists and artists alike.
    Af Klint drew inspiration from science but also from Spiritualism.
Modern occultism’s interaction with science can be linked to science’s
legitimating power and eminent status. The appropriation of notions
(the atom) or rhetoric (“observation,” “experiment”) was thought to
add an air of validity to occult practices. This engagement attempted to
counter the materialistic, stubbornly rationalist, positivist, and entirely
disenchanted worldview many believed science propagated. University
of Amsterdam art historian, Tessel M. Bauduin noted, “Many artists,
still influenced by late Romanticism and its inherent notions of artistic


## PDF page 9
Introduction                         3


Prometheanism and intuitive genius, also resisted science’s material­
ism and exclusive knowledge claim.”4 Both occult and artistic appro­
priations of science gave an alternative perspective from that which was
dominated by educated Caucasian men in which there was a perceived
reliance on technology and disembodied instruments, the separation of
materiality and consciousness, and a mechanistic and inanimate world­
view.5 In part, af Klint’s appropriation of these concepts and science
may be viewed as a critique, even though it also served her in investi­
gating and constructing alternative forms of knowledge regarding the
physical world and her beliefs in its spiritual foundation.6
    Hilma af Klint was working at a time when many of the new
scientific discoveries showed aspects of the world that are beyond
the observable physical world, such as electromagnetism, radioactive
decay, X-rays, subatomic particles, and atoms. This generated ques­
tions regarding an invisible and immaterial world, allowing for the
study and making of observations of another dimension of reality. In
an ironic sense, these scientific revelations sparked interest not only in
the scientific community but also in practitioners of Spiritualism and
the occult who were interested in how to document spiritual realms
through clairvoyant and mediumistic interaction. Thus, this book stud­
ies the parallel historical trajectories of science, spiritism, and the shift­
ing artistic perspectives.
        Overview of Hilma af Klint’s Life and Work
For the reader unfamiliar with af Klint, an overview of her life and work
may be beneficial. Though both are explored in greater detail later in
the book, an awareness of her remarkable life and endeavors may give a
framework for later chapters.
    Hilma af Klint was born at Karlberg Palace in Stockholm to
Mathilda Sontag and Captain Fredrik Victor af Klint, the fourth of five
children, on October 26, 1862. Her family moved to Norrtullsgatan in
Stockholm in 1872 and spent their summers at Hanmora, the family
estate located on the island of Adelsö. As a child, she attended a General
School for Girls, the Normalskola för Flickor, and later attended a


## PDF page 10
4                      Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


technical school, Tekniska Skolan, known today as University College of
Arts, Crafts and Design or Konstfack, where she took classes in portrait
painting.7 She was one of the first women to attend the Royal Academy
of Fine Arts in Stockholm, graduating with honors and acquiring a stu­
dio in the Academy’s “Atelier Building.” Enrolling at the age of twenty,
in 1882, she spent the next five years studying landscape and portrai­
ture painting.8 In the public eye she made a living as a modest tradi­
tional landscape, portrait artist, and illustrator. Up until the beginning of
the twentieth century, she painted naturalistic imagery that emulates a
young artist who “meticulously observed the world around her.”9
    Like many of her contemporaries, af Klint became interested in
investigating the invisible relationships that shape human existence. At
the turn of the century, extensive scientific discoveries forever changed
the customary understanding of space and matter. Around this time,
German physicist Heinrich Hertz (1857-1894) discovered the existence
of electromagnetic waves that move in a similar manner and speed as light
waves. In 1886, he successfully transmitted these waves from a sender to
a receiver, forming the groundwork for wireless telegraphy and eventu­
ally, the radio. In addition, in 1895, Wilhelm Röntgen (1845-1923), pro­
duced and detected electromagnetic radiation in a wavelength known as
X-rays, making it possible for scientists to see structures that exist below
the surface of the visible world. Although this may appear to be of separate
interest to that of spiritualism, these concepts greatly influenced af Klint’s
future works as she devoted her work and life to exploring the invisible
realm.10 It was not unusual for artists from various media to be involved
in spiritualism at this time, as they believed they could communicate with
energy or spirits beyond perceivable reality. Af Klint was involved with
several spiritual movements such as Theosophy and Anthroposophy. These
movements were intertwined in Western and Eastern religious and philo­
sophical beliefs and with occult beliefs that did not separate the realms of
science and religion.11
     Further influencing af Klint in her younger years, she grew up dur­
ing the crisis of the Swedish Lutheran Church when Darwin’s theory
of evolution, On the Origin of Species (1859) was published, questioning


## PDF page 11
Introduction                         5


some beliefs of the church in Sweden. The Darwinian theory challenged
the creation of the universe and all in it by God, confronting religious
beliefs with scientific explanation.12 At this time, people were very recep­
tive to new ideas in philosophy and science, becoming uncertain about
current religious structures. Religious beliefs and some doctrines began
to shift in society. Traditional values were no longer secure, alternative
religions, spiritualism, and occultism became more attractive to some
people. This gained wider acceptance in the First World War as the
notions of grief and religion played an integral part in the rise of spirit­
ism. The idea of life after death, ghosts, and spirits provided much com­
fort after suffering great loss.13 In the twentieth century, the interaction
of science and religion has taken on many forms. New scientific dis­
coveries, challenge classic religious beliefs, even though Darwin himself,
believed “the process of evolution (but not the details of particular spe­
cies) was designed by God.”14
    Af Klint’s involvement with spiritism greatly influenced the type of
work she created and the purposes behind the images. Historically, eso­
teric doctrines hold the belief that nature has a soul and that all things,
even inanimate matter, hold cosmic energy. Unique connections, real or
symbolic, are thought to exist between invisible and visible segments
of the universe and proponents contend that communication occurs
between the macrocosm of the universe and the microcosm of things
within it such as the smallest flower or atom using channeling and intu­
ition. Additionally, it was believed that this type of correspondence could
be achieved through rituals, symbols, or by speaking to spirits. The con­
cept of communication between belief systems and consciousness is to
be found in religions throughout the world and appears in a majority of
Western esoteric forms. As art historian, Marja Lahelma, who special­
izes in early twentieth-century Nordic art writes, “We must re-evaluate
art history in a more accurate way that should demand a wider range
of perspectives beyond the surface of consciousness.”15 This notion has
origins within the traditions of alchemy, which believes all types of mat­
ter, dead or alive can be transmuted (or transformed) to another form
of matter.16


## PDF page 12
6                      Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


    Af Klint  believed deeply  in these  spiritual dimensions and
became a part of an expanding movement of like-minded individuals
in Swedish society who were interested in alternative spiritual explora­
tion. In 1889, she joined the Theosophical Society, the same year the
organization was established in Sweden. She also spent many years
doing research and attending lectures at the Goetheanum Dornach, the
location of Rudolf Steiner’s Anthroposophical movement. Although af
Klint chose to keep her abstract paintings made after 1906 from the art
world, she was an active member of a group that included inventors,
scientists, doctors, journalists, and poets that were all proportionally
attended by women. There was little room for advancement in the art
world at the time, however, the adherents to leaders of Theosophy were
often women.17
    Understanding the etymology of Theosophy gives great insight into
the foundation of her creative process. The term Theosophy is derived
from Greek, in which theos means “god” and sophia means “wisdom.”
Theosophy is often recognized as meaning “divine wisdom.” Its roots as a
belief system are traced from Neoplatonism, and Gnosticism, and influ­
enced by Indian thought. The doctinres have differing forms of conjec­
ture, yet hold recurrent characteristics, such as the significance of mysti­
cal experience. Theosophical authors believe that direct contact can be
made with a “deeper spiritual reality” through revelation, meditation,
intuition, and other states of transcendence that move beyond normal
human consciousness. In Theosophy, there is an emphasis on esotericism
and fascination with gaining the ability of spiritual and psychic powers
through the supernatural.18
    The founder of the Theosophical Society, Helena Petrovna Blavatsky
(1831-1891), believed that channeling spirits was an otherworldly sci­
ence that has existed since prehistoric times.19 She writes in Isis Unveiled:
      Since the day the modern science gave what may be consid­
      ered the death-blow to dogmatic theology, by assuming the
     ground that religion was full of mystery, and mystery is unsci­
       entific, the mental state of the educated class has presented


## PDF page 13
Introduction                         7


      a curious aspect. Society seems from that time to have been
      ever balancing itself upon one leg, on an unseen tight-rope
      stretched from our visible universe into the invisible one;
      uncertain whether the end hooked on faith in the latter might
      not suddenly break, and hurl it into final annihilation.20
Blavatsky rejected the concept of conflict between science and religion.
    The Theosophical Society directly declared occupancy of the role
of mediating science and religion. Blavatsky merged belief in magic and
scientific studies, developing a religious movement that held scientific
impact.21 The basic message in Theosophy was that “life after death was
pretty much the same as life before it; it was the consolation gained by the
bereaved from contact with the dead that put an end… to mourning.”22
Blavatsky primarily distinguished Theosophy from magic in which she
taught that the Theosophists practiced their own powers, striving “to
realize his unity with the whole of humanity.”23
    These concepts proved to be impactful for af Klint. Likely, when
af Klint was a teenager, she began searching for spiritual insight and
participated in séances, after the death of her younger sister, Hermina in
1880.24 She stopped for a while as it was not taken seriously at the time
but later as an adult would perform these rituals shortly after becoming
a member of the Theosophical Society.
     In 1896, af Klint and four of her friends, including a close patron
and collaborator artist Anna Cassel (1860-1937), formed a group known
as “The Five” (De Fem). They were an independent circle of women who
participated in séances and engaged in religious studies, eventually
resulting in contact with spiritual guides.25 These five women met every
Friday for ritualistic spiritual meetings that included studying the New
Testament, meditation, and praying. Af Klint, who is often referred to
as a medium, practiced spontaneous automatic writing and drawings.
After several years, they increasingly made contact with various spirits,
whom they named, “The High Ones” (De Höga), from whom they took
meticulous notes of the messages conveyed by the spirits.26 In 1906, the
spirit “Amaliel” came through to them and commissioned af Klint to


## PDF page 14
8                      Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


create Drawings to the Temple, 27 an ambitious and visionary project that
was kept secret from the art world for decades.28
    Af Klint created her series Drawings to the Temple from 1906-1915,
comprised of various groups and series, totaling one hundred and ninety-
three abstract paintings. The idea behind the series conveyed a message
beyond the visible dualistic world, focusing on knowledge of how all is
one. The temple is metaphorically represented for spiritual evolution. As
af Klint describes the process of creating The Ten Largest, “The pictures
were painted directly through me, without any preliminary drawings,
and with great force. I had no idea what the paintings were supposed
to depict; nevertheless, I worked swiftly and surely, without changing
a single brush stroke.”29 She believed that the principle of oneness and
equilibrium was lost at the Earth’s creation and gave way to universal
polarities: man and woman, good and evil, religion and science, spirit
and matter, microcosm, and macrocosm, which she attempted to resolve
through her paintings. This concept of duality is expressed through her
use of composition, color, and symbols. The series was inspired by con­
tact with the spirit and spiritual world and was, for her, a representa­
tion of finding truth in the relationship between material and spiritual
worlds, genders, and science and religion.
    The project was paused in 1908 so that she could provide care for
her blind mother. When she returned to the project in 1912, she no lon­
ger described the process as coming from an external source, but rather
as receiving messages, then interpreting them—thus providing more
subjective control over the creative process. Af Klint later states, “It was
not the case that I was to blindly obey the High Lords of the mysteries
but that I was to imagine that they were always standing by my side.”30
The unfortunate consequence of discussing the commencing spiritual
process, is that her mysticism tarnished her reputation long after she
died in 1944.
     In the 1970s her nephew, Erik af Klint offered the work to
Sweden’s most prominent modern art museum, the Moderna Museet in
Stockholm. The director at the time declined the paintings. “When he
heard that she was a medium, there was no discussion. He didn’t even


## PDF page 15
Introduction                         9


look at the pictures.”31 It was not until 2013 that the museum retrospec­
tively redeemed itself, exhibiting af Klint with some 230 symbolic works,
for the first time with her series The Ten Largest. As curator Iris Müller-
Westermann states, “For some it’s provocative when someone says, ‘I
did this physically but it’s not by me. I was in contact with energies
greater than me.’”32 She argues that Wassily Kandinsky (1866-1944),
Kazimir Malevich (1879-1935), and Piet Mondrian (1872-1944) were
also influenced by contemporary spiritual movements such as Theosophy
and Anthroposophy as they sought to transcend the constraints of rep­
resentational art as well as the physical world. They, however, never sug­
gested, publicly, that the images they created were visual representations,
expressed by consciousness outside of themselves.33
      It also is interesting to note that in 1910, af Klint invited leading
Theosophical movement leader, Rudolf Steiner (1861-1925), to view her
work. However, his comments were discouraging, and he refused to be
her mentor claiming that she already had found a teacher with spir­
its. Although it is not known what was discussed in detail during this
meeting, some suggest that it caused her to claim authorship over her
paintings. This type of transition to command was common for women
who received acknowledgment as mediums—once gaining a certain level
of respect, they began to speak for themselves rather than for spirits.
Her work after 1912 moved away from techniques related to channel­
ing spirits such as the fluid lines of automatic drawings created during
meetings of De Fem. Slowly, her use of geometric forms and Christian
iconography increased, until she completely ceased to create art through
channeling the spirits in 1917.34
    During her four-year pause from Drawings to the Temple, she stud­
ied philosophy and continued painting portraits and landscapes to sur­
vive financially. Af Klint was aware of her unique style of art and worked
intensely on personal development to better understand the process
involved in creating her works. The overshadowing question for her was:
What is the message that the paintings convey? She sought answers in
religion, philosophy, and archives, but to no avail. She envisioned her work
as something that would contribute to society and human consciousness.


## PDF page 16
10                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


However, she was convinced that the world was not prepared to under­
stand her art, having received strict orders from De Höga, or the High
Ones, not to show her work to anyone. Thus, she believed her paintings
“belonged to the future, and only then would they be understood by the
public.”35 She resumed working on this series from 1912 until 1915, cre­
ating eighty-two additional pieces. Her increasingly more independent
works include groups The Swan and The Dove (see Appendix A).
    The last group in Drawings to the Temple consists of three large
paintings entitled: Group X: Altarpieces (1915). This work is related to
Theosophy’s teaching of evolutionary theory, in which it is believed evo­
lution occurred in two separate directions: the heightening from the
physical to the spiritual and descending to the material world from the
divine. Altarpieces, sum up many of the other pieces that she created,
showing geometrical shapes that demonstrate unity. This group depicts
the “development of the material world from unity into multiplicity,
experience in the world, and finally the return to Oneness. They are
described by af Klint as ‘a summary of the whole work.’”36 For af Klint,
evolution is not necessarily physically literal from a Darwinian sense but
rather, rooted in the spiritual. Steiner and fellow Theosophists believed
that humans began in a pure spirit form, however, became separated
from their spiritual selves because of the material world.
     In 1915, after af Klint completed her Drawings to the Temple series,
she devoted most of her life to trying to decipher it. She edited, anno­
tated, and added clues that could assist her in decoding the divine lan­
guage she believed to be embedded in the series. Although she created
the work, she believed that she did not fully understand its meaning.
She was also known to be a devoted member of the esoteric Christianity
movement, which was led by Rudolf Steiner. One of the most significant
of Steiner’s philosophies was that the spirit plane could be metaphysi­
cally corroborated through scientific investigation. In af Klint’s note­
books, she describes the new set of symbols that evolves as a main theme
in her work, representing energies from plants and atoms.37
    The Tree of Knowledge series is an integral part of af Klint’s broader
exploration within Drawings to the Temple series. It serves as a symbolic


## PDF page 17
Introduction                        11


representation of cosmic knowledge and spiritual evolution, intertwining
themes of enlightenment, interconnectedness, and the pursuit of higher
understanding. Concurrently, af Klint’s Atom series, painted later, delves
into the mysteries of the atomic realm, reflecting her deep engagement
with scientific inquiry. Both series showcase af Klint’s visionary approach
to art, bridging the realms of science and spirituality while transcending
conventional dichotomies. Through these works, af Klint invites viewers
to contemplate the profound interconnectedness of the universe and the
transcendent nature of human consciousness within the context of her
broader oeuvre.
    Even though af Klint’s previous abstract works are influenced by
the belief in the atom, later in her research, she began to directly study
atoms in general, and thereafter the atomic structures of flowers and
plants specifically. More specifically, she focused on what she believed to
be a dynamic relationship between an atom’s ethereal and physical forms.
For af Klint, the atom was a gateway to cosmic knowledge. She wrote:
“At this moment I have knowledge of, in the living reality, that I am an
atom in the universe that has access to infinite possibilities of develop­
ment. These possibilities I want, gradually, to reveal.”38 In af Klint’s, the
Atom series (1917), there are several depictions of the hypercube as also
seen in Drawings to the Temple, such as The Swan, No. 11 and No. 22. In
her atomic painting studies of flowers and plants, Violet Blossom with
Guidelines (1919), the plants are geometrically shaped and described as
the ethereal atomic form. The cube or square is a crucial, vital force in
many of af Klint’s diagrams.39 Within this book, the terms “abstract” and
“abstraction” are used not as fixed art-historial categories, but as flexible,
interpretive language tools to approach af Klint's symbolic, non-figura­
tive visual language without confining it to the conventional narratives
and movements that typically define “abstraction.”
    Hilma af Klint’s work and process holds a likeness to that of a modern-
day Leonardo da Vinci, as she documented her experiences in one hundred
and twenty-four annotated notebooks, totaling over twenty-six thousand
pages of writings. As an artist and researcher, she recorded her nature stud­
ies, spiritual experiences, creative processes, and scientific studies. From the


## PDF page 18
12                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


material she left behind, she broke many of the rules and restrictions society
had placed on her—as a woman at the turn of the twentieth century, as
an art student at the Royal Academy, and as a modern artist. It is slowly
becoming more widely accepted among art historians that before painters
such as Malevich or Kandinsky, who claimed to invent abstraction, she had
been painting in this style for several years specifically in series rather than
individual non-figurative works like other painters at the time.. When she
first started in 1906, at the age of forty-four, she wrote, “The experiments
I have undertaken, will astound humanity.”40 Af Klint was aware that the
world was not ready for her work; neither the aesthetic nor what had moti­
vated it. In 1932, she wrote that none of her drawings or paintings should
be shown until at least twenty years after her death.41
                Method of the Study
To accomplish the first purpose of this study (provide an analysis of the
artistic process of Hilma af Klint, understanding her interpretation of
the technological and scientific advances at the turn of the twentieth
century), Ian Barbour’s (1923-2013) definition of technology as “the
application of organized knowledge to practical tasks by ordered sys­
tems of people and machines”42 is used. Although broad, this definition
provides insight into how people think about technology and how it can
be applied to many areas, including the fine arts. “Organized knowledge”
allows readers and researchers to include technologies based on inven­
tion and experience in addition to scientific theories. The “practical tasks”
can include the provision of services by communications media, comput­
ers, and biotechnologies as well as the production of material goods such
as in agriculture and industry. Referencing “ordered systems of people
and machines” shifts attention to the use of the hardware of technology
in social institutions and environments.
     In the case of the maker, art is a tool for communication that can sur­
pass language barriers, expressing universal ideas or theories. Additionally,
this study explores the meaning of technology as technē. Explained in greater
depth, later in this chapter, technē, means skill, craft, art, or a way in which
something is gained. Although the term logos has a multifaceted history,


## PDF page 19
Introduction                        13


for the sake of this research, it is used as an expression of the way an
internal thought is expressed. Artists use tangible materials as among the
earliest “technologies” to record and study their surroundings, grasp new
concepts, research science, and express societal impacts.
     Furthermore, concepts from Charles Percy Snow’s (1905-1980) The
Two Cultures (1959), is utilized. In his book, he eloquently discusses the
cultural divide between two significant areas of human intellect, the arts
and science. He writes, “I believe the intellectual life of the whole of
Western society is increasingly being split into two polar groups. When
I say intellectual life, I mean to also include a large part of our practical
life, because I should be the last person to suggest the two can at the
deepest level be distinguished.”43 This comment is regarding his back­
ground as a trained scientist and literary writer. His insightful observa­
tions focus on the proponents of the humanities and scientists’ failure to
comprehend the nature, content, and implications of others’ intellectual
activities. The controversial text highlights the long history of division
between these two intellectual domains and emphasizes the importance
of mutual respect.
    Concerning the second purpose (exploring the resulting spiritual
shifts that influenced the purpose and overall aesthetic of her works of
art), there is an examination of how advances in science could potentially
result in shifts in spirituality and influence changes in artistic expression.
As previously discussed, artists of the time, such as af Klint, realized that
if science can make the “unseen, seen,” then why might it not be true for
other forces, such as in spirituality? This brings together a melding of
spiritual theories from Steiner and Blavatsky as well as artistic methods
of visual influences from Johann Wolfgang von Goethe (1749-1832).
    Goethe was a polymath, progenitor for abstract art, and color theo­
rist who disputed Newtonian views in his book, Theory of Colors in 1810,
formulating instead a philosophical and psychological account of the
phenomenon in the way we experience color. Af Klint has a complex
symbolic vocabulary that only in recent years has come into exami­
nation. Patterns such as yellow representing male and blue as female,
have been observed by historians and were also recorded by af Klint


## PDF page 20
14                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


in her notebooks. The notion of using these two colors composes a
dichotomy that she derived from Goethe’s color theories. His work was
widely dismissed and rejected at the time due to his polemic against
Newton’s view of color. Goethe did not object to Newton’s notion of
color being measured by a wavelength, but rather believed that Newton
made an error of assuming an approach, “that banished the spirit of liv­
ing contemplation.”44 Goethe believed that Newton’s theory was reduc­
tionist and did not make colors in themselves, intelligible—but instead
“reduced them to measurable wavelengths of colorless light.”45
    Af Klint takes a great deal from Goethe, more than a simple dichot­
omy. Her body of work is not necessarily a rebuke of the Newtonian uni­
verse; however, like Goethe, she proposes a colorful and lush world that is
shaped by the principles of patterning and color. As previously discussed,
she thought Steiner’s lectures and theories resonated with her own. His
color lectures, influenced by Goethe, discussed yellow as the “lustre of
the spirit” and blue as the “lustre of the soul.”46 He wrote, “Green rep­
resents the lifeless images of the living, peach-blossom represents the
living image of the soul, white or light represents the soul’s image of
the spirit and black represents the spiritual image of the lifeless.”47 His
involvement with spiritualism along with his theories of color are clear
influences on af Klint as she developed her artistic practice. Steiner
writes in, Colour: “If you realize that art always has a relation to the
spirit, you will understand that both in creating and appreciating it, art
is something through which one enters the spiritual world.”48 He further
applied Goethe’s color theories to artistic practices, that he believed sup­
port spiritual revelations.
     Lastly, for the final purpose of this study (an examination of her
visual contemporaries of the time—many of whom, claim to be the pio­
neers of abstraction), this book leans upon the esoteric influence in the
arts and the early development of fellow abstract artists that have been
acknowledged through decades of in-depth analysis. To better under­
stand the works of af Klint, the concept of “abstraction as a language”
that is capable of expressing deeper truths than what people can com­
municate with the five senses is explored. As curator Maurice Tuchman


## PDF page 21
Introduction                        15


wrote, “The genesis and development of abstract art were inextricably
tied to spiritual ideas current in Europe in the late nineteenth and twen­
tieth centuries.”49 It demonstrates from the past to the present that an
“astonishingly high proportion of visual artists in the 100 years have
been involved with these ideas and belief systems.”50 Thus, studying what
influenced af Klint is vital in attempting to decipher and interpret her
complex visual maps of the Tree of Knowledge and the Atom series.
    As an example, The Symbolist Aesthetic and the Impact of Occult and
Esoteric Ideologies on Modern Art, written by Marja Lahelma, studies the
impact esoteric ideas have had on artistic practices and theories. She
believes in the importance of re-evaluating and reassessing dominant
narratives that are seen in art history. The central purpose, studies the
background of this phenomenon, pointing to the growing symbolistic
nature art that arose near the end of the nineteenth century. This demon­
strates that symbolist methods such as intuition, dematerialization, and
inwardness are directly linked to esoteric beliefs and these early abstract
aesthetic theorizations have had significant influence on later artists.
Work created by artists in the late nineteenth century was dramatically
different from work created before. Artists in this new era were no longer
content with the old “window of the world paradigm.”51 Rather than
creating realistic, tangible objects based in the reality of how things were
seen in the physical world, they turned inwards and expressed fantasies,
dreams, nightmares, hallucinations, and visions as well as spirituality
and ancient myths. The intent was not to shock the bourgeoisie but to
instead become more innovative, to develop both practice and theory in
their art. Their new aesthetic attitude rests upon a spiritual and philo­
sophical basis, deeply rooted in esoteric and occult ideologies and their
pursuit for new forms of expression and new directions in art that were
paralleled by in-depth religious searching.52 A similar pattern of inten­
tion is reflected in af Klint’s oeuvre.
      Science, Technology, and Culture in a New Century
At the end of the nineteenth century, European cultural shifts allowed
more opportunities for women to become scientists. This frequently


## PDF page 22
16                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


came with much scrutiny as female intelligence was questioned by such
individuals as Otto Weininger (1880-1903), author of Sex and Character
(1903). His book presented the long-standing idea, claimed for centu­
ries, that women are inferior to men both mentally and physically. As
more and more women were beginning to enter universities and pur­
sue careers, Weininger attempted to warn men of the inevitable when
women pursue advanced education, conduct research, participate in poli­
tics, or even make art. He blamed the “masculinization of the female sex”
on women’s rights movements.53 As a summary, fueling these beliefs he
wrote, “the abstract male is the image of God, the absolute something;
the female, and the female element in male, is the symbol of nothing.”54
Weininger died of suicide before seeing the first female Nobel Prize for
a female scientist, Marie Curie (1867-1934), for her contributions to
physics and her discovery of Radium. At the end of 1903, the question
on female intelligence was a highly debated topic that made sense to
many educated men of the time.55
     In 1905, there was great political turmoil occurring in Scandinavia:
Norway declared independence from Sweden, one-hundred and fifty
thousand Russian workers went on strike and marched the Winter
Palace, and Finnish workers also went on strike and demanded universal
suffrage for both men and women. All of this was of great interest in the
af Klint household as there were ties of family history with the events
occurring in neighboring countries. Hilma’s older sister, Ida closely fol­
lowed what was happening—she worked for Sweden’s women’s rights
organization Fredrika Bremer Association, for fifteen years. At this time,
Australia and New Zealand were the only countries that allowed women
the right to vote. It was possible that Finland would follow their exam­
ple. The worker’s movement gained momentum in Finland as indus­
trialization in the nation swelled. Unrest grew as workers continued to
organize. The old political order of Europe suffered a significant blow,
however, hoped the turbulence could be stopped, even by way of vio­
lence, if necessary.56
     Indications of revolution in science were also swirling at this time—
unlike other revolutions, this one was not downplayed by anyone, but


## PDF page 23
Introduction                        17


rather embraced by most of society. Researchers in chemistry and phys­
ics were winning one prize after another. In particular, everyone was fas­
cinated by the mysterious powers that Marie and Pierre Curie (1859-
1906) discovered, termed “radioactivity.” Their discovery of radiation
opened the door to a previously deemed impossible phenomenon. In
June 1905, Pierre Curie explained to a large audience in a Nobel lecture,
the mutable atom, and the discovery that there was a “continuous and
irreversible disaggregation of the atoms of the radioactive elements.”57
He stressed that these consequences could not be dismissed. He warned
the listeners of forces that would be unleashed by splitting the atom and
asked the question, did “mankind benefit from knowing the secrets of
nature?” “Radium could be very dangerous in criminal hands,” adding, “I
am one of those who believe with Nobel that mankind will derive more
good than harm from new discoveries.”58 Unexpectedly, these scientific
advances tie back to the works of Charles Leadbetter (1854-1934) and
Theosophy, pointing out the mystics had known all along that physical
matter was mutable.59 These discoveries at the turn of the century over­
lap science and spiritualism, showing oneness rather than separateness,
sparking the creative research of Hilma af Klint and a group known as
The Five.
                    Structure of the Study
This books draws from many individuals, as well as artistic, spiritual, and
scientific movements, and philosophies. For example, studying the work
on esotericism from Helena Petrovna Blavatsky and Rudolf Steiner is
crucial in gaining a better understanding of the Spiritualism movement.
Furthermore, connecting with a network of scholars from the European
Society for the Study of Western Esotericism (ESSWE) assists in gain­
ing information regarding the history and rise of esotericism. In addi­
tion, there is study of other early abstract artists and patterns in simi­
lar artistic processes to support research and focus on the influences of
Renaissance art, Marie Curie, and her discovery of radioactivity, research
on the atom, and the similarities among the spiritual beliefs that were
practiced by af Klint.


## PDF page 24
18                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


     Utilizing these resources further contributes to the research on af
Klint and the parallels seen in the abstract art of her contemporaries
during the early nineteenth century. The experience of images from both
the creator and the audience is multi-dimensional and cannot be fully
explained solely from interpretations of the lifeworld. The consider­
ation of artistic imagination combined with pure phenomena transcends
human explanations of ordinary life and challenges people to open their
consciousness to more complex existential questions. By further exam­
ining fellow researchers, this study brings more knowledge into pub­
lic discourse and consideration regarding the life and work of af Klint.
Additionally, this research perhaps provides insight into conscious­
nesses through the study of atomic structures and modernistic visual
interpretations.
     This study includes a summary of the biography of Hilma af Klint
to better understand events in her life and how they influenced her stud­
ies as seen in her abstract paintings. Although Theosophy and spiritual­
ism are mentioned in her short biography, it isbe necessary to study these
influences in greater detail within Chapter Two. Additionally, to further
break down movements of the art world, European trends of the arts
and aesthetics (1880-1920) and its influences on the artist, the scientific
and religious changes occurring at the turn of the twentieth century is
examined in Chapter Three, explore impacts on worldview.
     In Chapter Four, there is a study and visual analysis of af Klint’s
Tree of Knowledge and the Atom series. While this in-depth description
focuses on paintings from these two series; there is an examination of
atomic influences with her other paintings, for instance, when imagery
of geometrical forms appears and how they relate to the atomic ethereal
forms. This chapter ties together events occurring at the time as well as
connect science and spiritualism as seen in her abstract works and what
she believed was its meaning to humanity.
    Although af Klint’s paintings are first viewed as beautiful, this was
not the intent of the work. The intent was to discover truth. She regarded
her symbolic works as sacred and believed they contained a spiritual mes­
sage to humanity from higher dimensions, even calling them “The Fifth


## PDF page 25
Introduction                        19


Gospel.”60 Her work was created through her mediumship and spiritism.
Her vibrant compositions of symbols and shapes presented spiritual and
philosophical concepts that were essentially her visual manifestations for
us to see and reflect upon. Her work conveyed transcendental messages
for all of humanity. A creative process that expands beyond anything that
she knew of within this plane of existence. Her curiosity in spiritualism
combined with her studies in scientific studies at the time, was her pro­
cess of seeking divine wisdom (truth). In her notebooks, she states, “My
mission, if it succeeds, is of great significance to humankind. For I am
able to describe the path of the soul from the beginning of the spectacle
of life to its end.”61
     Lastly, thoughit is said by many historians that af Klint’s work was
hidden from the public, there are many interesting parallels seen among
her contemporaries. However, she made these similar images five years
before artists such as Malevich and Kandinsky. There is much specula­
tion about how these similar images would occur. Her abstract work was
fearless; but many of her predecessors worked in a slower, timid manner.
Is it possible, they met af Klint and discussed their works connected
to Theosophy at some point? Chapter Six studies artists who indicate
similar parallels to her early abstract works. The final chapter is a con­
clusion that includes a summary of findings and recommendations for
further study.

2
   A Brief Biography of Hilma af Klint
     his chapter examines significant influences in Hilma af Klint’s life
     that contributed to shaping her views of spirituality, science andThow it bestowed the formation of her unique creative process. This is not
a detailed account of af Klint’s life, rather, it is a presentation of experi­
ences and events that art critics and af Klint herself have noted as criti­
cal in the development of her oeuvre that moved away from traditional
images of the academic art world.
                     Background
Hilma af Klint was born in northwest Stockholm, housed in a former
palace in the barracks of the Military Academy Karlberg, run by her
great-grandfather and the first seafarer of the family, Erik af Klint (1732-
1812). His descendants served as directors and officers of the academy:
her grandfather Gustaf af Klint (1771-1840) as well as her father Victor
(1822-1898). Her mother (1832-1920), born as Mathilda Sontag from a
Finnish family that belonged to the Swedish upper class.1 Mathilda was
an energetic, practical person and a dedicated mother who maintained a
tight helm on the children and home. Her father, Captain af Klint, was
an instructor at Karlberg for the naval cadets. Besides his knowledge of
maritime navigation, he was interested in music, and mathematics and
enjoyed the outdoors.2
    The fourth child born in her family, Hilma af Klint had two older
living siblings, Gustaf (1858-1927) and Ida (1860-1938), the firstborn,
Anna died before the age of two (1857).3 Eight years after Hilma4,
another girl, Hermina was born in 1870 and would later die at the age of
ten on October 17, 1880, by unconfirmed causes.5

                            25


## PDF page 32
26                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


    The first six years of Hilma’s life were spent in the masculine envi­
ronment of the military academy, where each day she and Ida watch
sailing ships, coming in and out of the harbor. The af Klints were imbued
in the terminology and knowledge of the nautical life since Erik joined
the navy in 1750 at the age of eighteen. The af Klint family has docu­
mentations back to 1523. They were working as civil servants, notaries,
pastors, tax collectors, magistrates, and farmers with little opportunity
to rise in status. This was, however, until 1790 when King Gustav III
(1746-1792) elevated the af Klints to nobility in recognition of Erik and
his son, Gustaf (Hilma’s grandfather and great-grandfather), for their
significant contributions fighting against Russia in the Battle of Vyborg
Bay July 3, 1790. Thereafter, the family was known as the af Klints.6
    The af Klint family was not only known for their military service,
but gained an admirable reputation as cartographers, another legacy
traced back to Erik and Gustaf, who at the behest of the king, surveyed
the Swedish coastline. Gustaf continued making maps independently
after the death of the king, without official support or payment. His life
works include The Sea Atlas of Sweden, the Norwegian coastline, charts
of the North and Baltic Seas, and most of the large Swedish lakes and
other various bodies of water.7 Hilma’s brother Gustaf, also a navy officer,
the most notable achievement was to perform an extensive survey of the
navigable waters in Sweden.8
                  Early Artistic Influences
Had Hilma been born male, she would have followed the traditional
trajectory of the men in her family by joining the Swedish Navy as an
officer. Although a naval career was not an option for Hilma, its influ­
ences can be seen in her abstract works. She documented the journey of
her work within her notebooks as small map illustrations.9 An example
of this can be seen in The Ten Largest (1906-1908) (Figure 1), where she
uses cartographic symbols of dashed white lines that outline hidden
forms as if revealing reefs underwater. These notable marks that resemble
nautical charts became a leitmotif in Hilma’s work and an ongoing
theme of revealing the invisible. Contrary to terrestrial maps, nautical


## PDF page 33
A Brief Biography of Hilma af Klint                 27


charts primarily illustrate underwater territories that are inaccessible to
humans. In a metaphorical sense, the nautical chart’s function is akin to
hydraulic lifts, elevating the ocean floor to the surface for visibility. They
reveal underwater features such as reefs, sandbars, rocks, and cliffs that
otherwise remain hidden beneath the water’s surface.10





    Figure 1. Hilma af Klint, The Ten Largest, No. 3, Youth (1907). Tempera on paper, 320.9 x 240 cm

## PDF page 34
28                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


When Hilma’s father, Captain af Klint, was sailing with the cadets in the
summers, the family moved west of Stockholm to Lake Mälaren, on the
island of Adelsö. They lived in a cottage in Tofta Manor; a property that
was purchased by Hilma’s grandfather. Hanmora was another nearby
estate owned by the family, where many relatives lived. Hilma spent a
great deal of time in nature as a little girl in these idyllic surroundings.
She spent summers at the lake throughout her life. It is here that she
began observing natural phenomena and plants early on in her life which
would later play a critical role in her work.11
    As a teen, Hilma was involved in many artistic circles. This may
have been how she met painter and photographer, Bertha Valerius
(1824-1895)12, a woman nearly forty years older than Hilma and
thought to be an early creative influence. As one of Sweden’s first inde­
pendent female photographers with a thriving studio in town, Valerius
would have been in the position to connect Hilma to a wide segment of
Stockholm society.13
     Valerius had to fight for her personal freedom and professional
success. She, alongside many other like-minded women, demanded
the right and won the opportunity to study art, as a young woman.
A predecessor of Hilma, when Bertha was in her mid-twenties, she
became one of the first few women to be accepted into the Royal
Swedish Academy of Fine Arts14, after going through an extraordi­
nary admissions process. It was not until 1864, a mere fifteen years
later, that the Academy modified its policy and established dedicated
facilities for female students, referred to as a ladies’ department called
Fruntimmersavdelningen. This decision was uncommon; most state
academies across Europe adhered to tradition and didn’t enroll women
until after the First World War.15
     After completing her studies, Valerius went abroad to Paris, Dresden,
and Düsseldorf for further advanced training. When she returned to
Stockholm, she opened her own photography studio and was quickly “a
royal portrait supplier in 1864.” Tremendously successful, her paintings
were also in great demand. She painted King Karl XV (1859-1872) and
his successor, King Oscar II (1829-1907), among other members of the


## PDF page 35
A Brief Biography of Hilma af Klint                 29


royal family. Valerius was happy to be unmarried and childless and was
said to not put a great deal of effort into her appearance.16
    The oldest document in the Hilma af Klint archive is a one-hun­
dred-and-seventy-page black notebook, dated 1879-1882. Inscribed
within the first page states, “Communications Through Bertha Valerius”.
In the notebook, there’s an account of a séance held on July 26, 1879,
where Valerius experienced her first instance of channeling a voice, pur­
portedly that of a spirit named Ulla. During this session, Ulla voiced cri­
tiques against the state church and pointed out perceived “falsehoods in
the theological doctrine”. Ulla also stated it was inappropriate to regard
the Bible as the sole foundation of faith. Moreover, she expressed that
Jesus, recognized as sent by God, had “returned to him when his mission
was completed.” Valerius explains in her notebook that this made her
joyful as it suggests Heaven was open rather than a one-way street and if
Jesus could leave then return, maybe he would not be the last.17
     Ulla was not the only spirit documented by Valerius in this note­
book. Another mentioned voice is that of Sweden’s renowned Physician
and Botanist Carl von Linné (1707-1778). He was considered a “trans­
lator of the plant world” during his lifetime. During this communication,
it is documented that he expressed happiness in the afterlife and that
his research was now respected. Even though Valerius does not provide
details on her methods of opening her consciousness to speak to spirits
from the beyond, the one consistent message was of joy, happiness, and
reconciliation.18
     This positive perspective from the beyond, written by a successful
female painter and photographer who dabbled in spiritism and was a
friend to Hilma would prove to later influence her interest to experi­
ment with the spiritual world. The notes indicate that by 1879, a silent
art revolution had reached Sweden. Although its specific forms were
unclear, supporters of this new art were certain it would derive power
from higher sources, rather than the material realm. The purpose was
to depict the spirit rather than matter. Like Hilma, Valerius kept these
works secret. Gifted to Hilma by Bertha Valerius, she kept this notebook
her entire life.19


## PDF page 36
30                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

                Academic Studies of Art
Around the age of ten, Hilma’s family moved to Stockholm from
Karlberg, first into a large house on Norrtullsgatan, then later to one of
the main areas of Stockholm in Badstugatan, now known as Sveavägen.20
Her parents found pleasure in hosting guests. Friends and relatives fre­
quently congregated in their household, creating an atmosphere that
was delightful and peaceful. After attending the Royal Normal School
for Girls, she then enrolled in the Technical School in Stockholm, now
known as Konstfack University of Arts, Crafts and Design in 1879, in
preparation for the Royal Academy. Around this time, she began study­
ing portrait painting with a Swedish painter, known as Kerstin Cardon,
born Mathilda Kristina Cardon (1843-1924).21 The Technical School
was a private painting school, run by Cardon, modeled after the ones she
experienced in Paris.
    Cardon also attended the Royal Academy where her father, Johan
Elias Cardon (1802-1878) was a member of the school as well as her
uncle, Carl Oskar Cardon (1812-1899); both men were skilled litho­
graph artists. Kerstin was initially trained by her father in basic art skills
before being accepted into the Royal Academy in 1864, becoming a
member of the first full cohort of eighteen women. The work she created
at the beginning of her formal training was of animals and still lifes.
After her training at the Royal Academy, she continued studying art
and traveled throughout Europe to Vienna, Italy, Paris, London, Paris,
Munich, and the Netherlands to further develop her portrait painting
skills. She primarily worked in oils and pastels.22 She appeared proper
and dignified in photographs, which was suiting since most of her com­
missions came from either officers of the military or the aristocracy. Her
paintings immortalized lieutenants, counts, and barons in oil on canvas
as well as members of the royal family including Oscar II.23
    Cardon shared many  similarities with Bertha  Valerius. Both
women completed their studies at the Royal Academy in Stockholm,
pursued further education abroad, and subsequently returned to Sweden
to establish themselves as professional artists in the 1870s. Although


## PDF page 37
A Brief Biography of Hilma af Klint                 31


Cardon was exposed to modernity and early experimental impressionist
works of Édouard Manet (1832-1883) and Claude Monet (1840-1926)
in Paris, which studied light and atmosphere, her work resembled tradi­
tional techniques of Romanticism and Realism.24
     In a time when art was taught exclusively by men and female pro­
fessors were not allowed at the Royal Academy, Cardon was a pioneer.
She was an entrepreneur who began her own art school, a member of
many artist organizations such as the Swedish Artists’ Federation (Svenska
Konstnärsförbundet) in 1890, a commissioner for the Swedish Female
Artists’ section of the Chicago Exhibition in 1893, and later a board
member of Handarbetets Vänner from 1905-1907.25 In a historic first for
Sweden, women were receiving art instruction from another woman.26
     In 1882, Hilma af Klint began her studies at the Royal Swedish
Academy of Fine Arts, suggesting an early indication of her talent. She
completed the standard five-year course in 1887, standing out as a skilled
illustrator, as noted by her nephew Erik af Klint (1901-1981). Her lec­
turers included Swedish painters Per Daniel Holm (1835-1903), Johan
August Malmström (1829-1901), and Georg von Rosen (1843-1923)
and the teaching staff acknowledged her abilities. Among her peers
were Elisabeth Warling (1858-1915), Lotten Rönquist (1864-1912),
Charlotte Wahlström (1849-1924), and Anna Cassel (1860-1937).
Hilma formed a strong friendship with Anna, until she left for good in
1918.27 Norwegian painter, Vilhelm E. Behm (1859-1934), one of her
lecturers, was slightly older than Hilma and attended the Academy for
a few years during her studies, according to the Academy’s archives.28
Behm also attended the Technical School before studying and teaching
at the Royal Academy.
     There is no documentation of Hilma actively engaging in opposi­
tion against traditional academic art during her tenure at the Academy.
The movement, led by Swedish poet and painter, Ernst Josephson
(1851-1906) and known as the Opponents, was prominent during that
period. It’s plausible that Hilma, being relatively young at the time, did
not directly participate. However, it’s unlikely that she remained unaf­
fected or unaware of these developments. Notably, she later joined the


## PDF page 38
32                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


Association of Swedish Women Artists in 1910 and embarked on study
trips to Norway, the Netherlands, Belgium, and Germany.29
    The  highly  progressive  (for  the  1880s), Royal Academy  in
Stockholm allowed female students to observe and draw nude male
models. At that time, the idea of a room filled with a dozen or more
fully clothed women, sketching a naked man was considered unnatu­
ral and indecent by male artists and professors across Europe. They
attempted to use this point to advance their argument for not allowing
women to study art at all.30
    Hilma appeared unconcerned with societal expectations for female
artists. Her early sketches depict fully nude men, even featuring frontal
views, showcasing remarkable ease and freedom in her strokes. Before
her enrollment at the Academy in September 1882, she already demon­
strated a notable talent for drawing. Among her early works is a pencil
drawing from 1880 portraying a spiral staircase with elegant shading
and curves. Her archive includes sketches of plaster ornaments, vases,
and lamps, created during preparatory studies at the Technical School
under Cardon. In later years, she looked back fondly on her art studies,
in stark contrast to her memories of primary education. In a 1943 reflec­
tion, a notebook entry expresses happiness in embarking on the artistic
path, emphasizing the task of reproduction and the absence of a need for
original ideas—a reflection of the limited expectations placed on women
studying at the Academy.31
    At the Academy, Hilma studied different parts of the human form
in pencil and charcoal. Along with her nude male drawings, there are
sketches of nuns, a monk as well as human hands. Documentation illus­
trates what would typically be seen by an artist-in-training; whereas she
drew everything around her: sailboats, sheep, insects, houses, landscapes,
and human figures. The most prominent techniques were studies in
drawing, oil painting, and watercolor. The curriculum at the Academy
also included anatomy lessons and the creation of plaster copies of tra­
ditional classical sculptures. Hilma drew the class of students studying
figures with varying perspectives and compositions. Students wrote their
name and date on the paper of these exercises and when completed,


## PDF page 39
A Brief Biography of Hilma af Klint                 33


assessed by the instructor, made any necessary corrections, and signed off
with their initials on the work once it was approved.32
    At the Academy, men and women received instruction separately,
following the unchanged tradition initiated in 1864 upon women’s initial
admission. The educational setup lacked coed courses, maintaining strict
segregation between male and female spaces. Notably, only male teach­
ers were employed. Recollections from painter Carl Larsson revealed the
extent of segregation, admitting his lack of precise knowledge regarding
the female students’ location, save for the vague hint of a “constant smell of
coffee” in their vicinity. Even after he departed from the Academy, Larsson’s
remarks about his female colleagues were often disparaging in nature.33
    During her third year of study, Hilma delved into life drawing.
The male models posed in classical stances, emulating archers, or spear
throwers, adopting relaxed or contemplative seated postures. For female
students, mastering the art of drawing the nude figure presented a sig­
nificant opportunity. It equipped them with the skill to create history
paintings, which stood as the most esteemed genre in art. Traditionally,
the most celebrated artworks depicted pivotal historical or religious
moments, such as King Gustav II Adolf’s death on the battlefield at
Lützen in 1632. Capturing such scenes demanded the ability to realisti­
cally portray the human body in movement. Academic circles highly
valued history paintings as evidence of artistic brilliance, granting them
prestigious recognition and the most substantial public attention.34
    During Hilma af Klint’s time, the notion that monumental paint­
ings of their era would fade into obscurity for future generations seemed
inconceivable. Nonetheless, numerous large-scale canvases gradually
vanished into museum storage shortly after the turn of the century. In
contrast, paintings devoid of kings, generals, warriors, or soldiers began
gaining prominence and caused ripples throughout Europe as the style
of art was slowly beginning to shift.35
           An Artist in the Public View
Publicly, Hilma af Klint was an artist who primarily painted landscapes,
portraitures, and illustrated veterinary publications. She had a studio

                                             at Hamngatan 9, Stockholm
                                  from the late 1880s to around

                                       the Academy had apartments
                                  and  art  studios to students
                             who completed their studies.
                                    Lotten Rönqvist and Anna
                                       Arnell shared a studio and
                                       they allowed af Klint to use
                                                           it. The  building where  she

                                             this time was located above
 Figure 2. Hilma af Klint in her studio, Stockholm (1895)  cosmopolitan  Blanch’s  Art
      Courtesy of The Hilma af Klint Foundation      Salon and Café, named after
Vienna’s cuisine prize-winning owner and operator, Theodor Blanch
(1835-1911).36 This building, since demolished, was one of the most
prestigious hubs of Stockholm’s art world. Archived newspaper show
that Hilma exhibited at least once in a group exhibition here in 1891.
Blanch’s was a site for food, art, and music and one of the city’s first cin­
emas at the turn of the twentieth century.37
    Although there is no mention of Norwegian artist Edvard Munch
(1863-1944) in Hilma’s notebooks, young Munch presented his early
works in Blanch’s in 1894. The French Impressionist’s light and airy
style, popular at the time, was not in any way shown in Munch’s dark and
heavy themes that included works such as The Scream (1893). Munch
exhibited an image of his sister shortly after her death in a painting
called The Sick Child (1885), which may have possibly resonated with
Hilma, as she too lost her sister at a young age around the same time.
In Summer Night’s Dream (the Voice) (1893) (Figure 3), a solitary figure
dressed in white roams through a forest, attentively listening to the voice
of an unseen entity. Hilma later recounted a strikingly similar occurrence

A Brief Biography of Hilma af Klint                 35

when she recorded the words spoken by an invisible presence in the
Adelsö woods.38
    The af Klint family spent the summer of 1898 on the island of Adelsö.
On August 9, Hilma’s father, Victor, passed away at the family estate of
Hanmora. He was laid to rest in Stockholm, at the same cemetery39 where
Hermina, Hilma’s sister, was already interred.40 As previously stated, Hilma
met Anna Cassel at the Academy, and the two women remained close
friends until 1918. According to The Hilma af Klint Foundation, from
1900-1901, she worked alongside Cassel to create illustrations for a book
about horse surgery authored by John Vennerholm (1858-1931), who
served as professor and director of the Veterinary Institute in Stockholm.
The money earned by them at the Institute, financed their subsequent
travel through Europe to Italy and Florence.41
     In 1901, Vennerholm’s Textbook of Specialised Operations on Horses
for Veterinarians and Students was published, comprising of one-hundred
