36                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


and sixty-eight illustrations and four colored plates. The book was pub­
lished by Norstedts, Sweden’s most popular publisher at the time, and
later translated into German, serving as a standard reference for sev­
eral years. Despite the first automobiles reaching Sweden’s streets, the
use of horses continued to surge after 1901, with the increased need for
transportation in industrialized nations. Interestingly, in the foreword
of his book, Vennerholm explicitly acknowledged Hilma and Cassel for
their contributions.42 However, the women did not perceive their illus­
trations as a significant achievement, despite the knowledge gained from
the project.
    The veterinary school was only a few blocks from Blanch’s Art Salon.
The crude conditions were cramped and lacked basic amenities such as
toilets and heating, and surgeries were conducted outdoors by instructors
who worked without assistants. Despite this, Vennerholm remained an
optimist who attempted to bring awareness of humane animal treatment
to fellow veterinarians. However, at the time, arguments on animal rights
or ethics were largely ignored. The public showed more interest in the
usefulness of animals and how their health affected humans and what
the economic risks and dangers were if veterinary medicine research was
neglected. The field of bacteriology was progressing rapidly, prompting
an urgent need for a new, modern veterinary establishment in Sweden
due to the rapid advancements in hygiene and microbiology.43
    Vennerholm strived for the Textbook of Specialised Operations on
Horses for Veterinarians and Students to be a typographical standard in cut­
ting-edge veterinary books, setting a new benchmark for the discipline.
In preparation, he imaged high-level texts and first-class illustrations on
horse surgery and the new instruments he invented. Hilma af Klint, rec­
ognized for her proficiency in anatomical drawings during her time at the
academy, created illustrations along with Anna Cassel that would replace
the rudimentary depictions prevalent in veterinary literature of the time.
Vastly different from what the two women were accustomed to; these
horses were to be rendered precisely: broken bones, rashes, ulcers, respi­
ratory illnesses, abrasions, and wounds. They were not the same perfect
animals ridden by heroes and kings from traditional artworks.44


## PDF page 43
A Brief Biography of Hilma af Klint                 37


    The sketches of the horses were turned into puzzles; each distinctive
part separated into individual pieces: muscles, tissues, tendons, bones,
skulls, vertebrae, eyes, hoofs, reproductive organs, and several other main
bodily organs. This experience allowed the two friends to see into a whole
different world much different from their previous aesthetic training.
They were exposed to the mucus, blood, and excretions of animals. They
learned about methods and tools of medical research with models, mea­
surements, specialized instruments, illustrations, and heightened obser­
vation. Vennerholm’s scientific writings with such titles as, Diseases of
the Female Sex Organs and Mammary Glands, and research on the repro­
ductive process from both male stallions and female mares left a last­
ing impression on Hilma as her future paintings often depict motifs of
reproductive organs as well as exploring gender duality.45 The Symbolist
style, the closest description to Hilma’s oeuvre, explored gender duality
and each “gender’s struggle for a dominant position and power.”46
    Hilma held onto three sketches from her time at the institute. One
of them depicts a surgical instrument, while another shows an infected
hoof. The third sketch portrays a dental procedure where holes were
drilled in a horse’s upper jaw and forehead to access its mouth. Together
with Cassel, she created several similar sketches, which were then sent
for printing to create plates. These original sketches now reside in the
Veterinary Museum in Skara, stored in boxes from Vennerholm’s estate.
They showcase cross-sections of horse legs and eyes, organ positions,
anal sores, and hoof adhesions. Vennerholm was reluctant to part with
these pencil drawings, some of which were colored, and cherished them
throughout his life.47
     After her time creating depictions for the Veterinary Institute,
Hilma was commissioned to create illustrations for different children’s
books as well as magazines. She took on such commissions, includ­
ing the illustration of books like Little Knut, another book, Maria the
Ladybug, authored by Anna Maria Roos (1862-1938) who was a critic,
songwriter, Theosophist, and outspoken critic of the Lutheran church.
“The watercolor by H. af Klint” was featured in the December 1902 edi­
tion of the magazine Julklappen. Additionally, she published a Christmas


## PDF page 44
38                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


illustration in the journal Friska Vindar. She also created ten illustrations
for Lille Lapp-Natti 48, a children’s book authored by the accomplished
writer Laura Fitinghoff (1848-1908).49
     Creating compositions and motifs came naturally to her. Her
works often featured the small protagonists of the stories as sur­
rounded by vast arrays of animals and flowers, creating lively scenes
that filled the pages. Within her estate, numerous watercolors of plants,
particularly wildflowers, were found, offering the artist an abundant
resource for her creations. This period marked the beginning of the Art
Nouveau movement in Sweden, where botanical designs adorned vari­
ous media, including paintings, magazines, books, furniture, and even
entire buildings.50
   Among Hilma’s circle of friends, Ottilia Adelborg (1855-1936),
became a renowned children’s book author, was celebrated for her illus­
trations, and had also studied at the Academy. Their paths crossed fre­
quently, as both artists received grants from the Academy in 1891, and
both provided renderings for fairytales written by Roos. Moreover, both
artists had sisters who were prominent women activists and collectively
managed the Stockholm office of the Fredrika Bremer Association.51
    Hilma does not have any of the children’s book illustrations pre­
served in her estate. She only kept a few works from the Veterinary
Institute. She switched to a vegetarian diet in the summer of 1902,
perhaps Vennerholm’s research on animals and their ability to feel
pain resonated with her or perhaps she was following Eastern philo­
sophical teaching of Ahimsa52 to avoid inflicting suffering onto sen­
tient beings.53
    While Hilma af Klint is often referred to as an “unknown” artist by
researchers and historians; but she was an active painter who exhibited her
work and won awards for her skills. From the years 1886 to 1914, Hilma
exhibited conventional work in more than two dozen art shows, orga­
nized by the Swedish General Art Association. The Academy awarded
her a prize in 1888 for Andromeda at the Sea, an oil painting created for
the school’s competition on the topic of mythology. Additionally, Hilma
traveled to Italy, Belgium, Holland, and Germany while participating in


## PDF page 45
A Brief Biography of Hilma af Klint                 39


group exhibitions from 1888-1908. In 1914, she exhibited an academic
painting in Malmö in the Baltic Exhibition right before the First World
War broke out.54
             An Unmarried Woman
Around the time that Hilma was working at the Veterinary Institute, it
is said that she had fallen in love with Dr. Helleday (full name and living
dates unknown). There is not a significant amount of information avail­
able about their relationship, however, Hilma chose a life of dedication
to her paintings.55 Dr. Helleday who was interested in marriage, came
from family, hundreds of years older than the af Klint family. Relatives of
Hilma seemed more interested in his academic title than passing down
his last name. His family background and education would have been a
perfect match for the young artist at this time. The Helleday family emi­
grated to Sweden from Scotland in the seventeenth century, eventually
becoming distant relatives as one of Hilma’s aunts married into the fam­
ily in the mid-nineteenth century. Their wedding took place on Adelsö,
making it likely that Hilma then met Dr. Helleday.56
     In her thirties, this was long after most women in Sweden would
have married, however, social expectations began to change in her circle.
Women like Kerstin Cardon and Bertha Valerius decided against mar­
riage. The law that bestowed legal majority upon single women offered
Swedish daughters newfound freedom they were unwilling to give up.
The legal dominance of a husband over his wife in matters of law was
considered too steep a price by many women.57
     Additionally, the perspective around what love meant was shifting
in many progressive social and artistic circles. Love, in its many forms, was
highly important to Stockholm’s spiritualist group, called the Edelweiss
Society, founded by Huldine Beamish (1859-1931). According to the
1890 charter, Purity and Love were framing concepts. This spiritualist
group became very popular in Sweden and gained the admiration of
Russia and England. Beamish, knowing several languages, allowed her to
travel to sacred sites in Jerusalem, Constantinople, and Egypt. She urged
her followers to seek a soulmate, creating unity like putting together two


## PDF page 46
40                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


halves of a puzzle. This soul duality was not necessarily referenced as a
spouse. Never marrying Dr. Helleday, Hilma set out to “investigate the
mystery of love in all its forms”.58
                    De Fem
As discussed in Chapter One, early spiritualism in Sweden as well as
trends in advancing science, influenced Hilma’s work. It is thought that
she was involved early on in spiritualist circles in Stockholm. According
to Erik af Klint, her nephew, she communicated with the spirit world
even as a child, showing a natural talent in mediumship. She organized
and participated in séances from 1879 to 1882 as a member of a group,
name unknown, with strong Christian influences. It was around this
time that Hilma’s younger sister died.59
      It was in the 1880s when tensions began to arise in these spiritualist
circles she was involved in. Making a clear distinction, Erik remarked,
“The dead who spoke to Valerius with cheerful speeches and descrip­
tions of the afterlife often seemed downright naïve compared with the
voices af Klint and her friends began to record a decade later. Those
spirits were more philosophical and sincere, and they were very often
higher spiritual beings rather than dead people.”60 The specifics of these
practices and how they influenced her work will be discussed in further
detail throughout this study.
    Hilma became a member of Edelweissförbundet (the Edelweiss
Society) in 1896. During one of their séances, on Easter Sunday, April
15, 1896, De Fem (“The Five)” was founded.61 The name of this spiritualist
group comes from the number of founding members who participated
regularly and recorded their automatic writings, drawings, and texts in
notebooks. These five women were: Hilma af Klint, Anna Cassel, Sigrid
Hedman (1855-1922), Mathilda Nilsson (1844-1923), and her younger
sister, Cornelia Cederberg (1854-1933). Af Klint believed the spirits also
wanted her to lead a new religious movement that would be a substi­
tute the Anthroposophical Society. In this connection she had a group of
twelve women around her—“Apostles”—with herself being the thirteenth
person.62 Other women also involved in some capacities were: Emilia


## PDF page 47
A Brief Biography of Hilma af Klint                 41


Giertta, Lotten Rönquist, and Lotten Cassel. In later years, Thomasine
Andersson (1870-1940), Elisabeth Wahring (1844-unknown), and Anna
Ljungberg (unknown) also joined the group.63
    The information known about The Five’s meetings is retrieved from
Hilma’s notebooks that contain written descriptions of their seances; the
oldest record dating back to 1892 but without af Klint’s handwriting.
Two of these older books, containing details from 1896-1902 of medi­
ated spirit messages, are entitled Gregor’s Book I and Gregor’s Book II. In
one of her entries, Hilma describes Gregory as “a priest who lived during
the time of Pope Gregory VII and shared his views.”64 In the eleventh
century, Pope Gregory’s policies sparked the Investiture Controversy, a
dispute regarding the appointment of high-ranking church officials. The
artist makes note that the spirit Gregor aimed to liberate the church
from what was perceived as “Catholic heresies.”65 Records from The Five
indicate that they were aligned with dissenters and those who diverged
from mainstream beliefs from the outset. From 1903-1905, Clemen’s
Book was another notebook that was kept. Additionally, there are five
notebooks entitled, D.F.’s Book, which stands for The Five’s Book, cover­
ing the period from 1897 to 1907. Despite detailed record keeping, it
is difficult to discern which women were members in which periods,
although it is possible it was always the same women.66 Author, histo­
rian, and researcher, Julia Voss confirms Sigrid Hedman and Matilda
Nilsson were two of the women.67
     In addition to notebooks, there are also photographs from the late
1890s that show where and how meetings were held. Note-keeping and
photographs indicate each session had an altar set up, decorated with
large feathers, candles, with a cross placed in the center, and an oleo­
graph depicting Christ in presumably, Gethsemane, suspended from
above. There was also a chaise longue or bed for the medium to lie down
on comfortably while in a trance. In addition, there is also often a step
for kneeling and altar rail and other stools placed around the altar.68
   Many of the messages the group received were transmitted through
the leader of the group, Hedman, a married woman and mother of five
children. Nilsson, the oldest member of The Five, married to a captain in


## PDF page 48
42                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


the navy, played an important role as she had many years of experience
in spiritual groups. She belonged to Valerius’s Cloverleaf Society in the
1880s. She published the Journal for Spiritism and Related Topics for five
years. The journal had a good reputation and ran until 1922. English
Theosophist Annie Besant (1847-1933) visited Stockholm several times
in the 1890s, becoming a local celebrity, especially among spiritualist
circles. Nilsson interviewed her for the magazine during one of her visits.
Supporting India’s independence movement; Newspapers photographed
Besant wearing a bright-colored sari, in honor of India. Besant advocated
for equality, speaking up for the rights of workers, women, and colonized
peoples. Hilma read her books, often marking them with underlines and
annotations.69
    The Five used different tools to channel spirits such as medita­
tion, psychographs, and later, utilizing automatic writing. They typi­
cally gathered weekly at one of their residences. Before each session,
the women commenced with prayers, as indicated in an earlier note­
book, specific Bible passages read at the beginning of the séance. They
would join hands to create a sort of “chain,” resembling an electrical
circuit. Over time, their studies evolved to include Theosophical litera­
ture like Helena Petrovna Blavatsky’s (1831-1891) Secret Doctrine and
Charles Leadbeater’s (1854-1934) Invisible Helpers.70 It was around this
time that Hilma began distancing herself from spiritualism and began
to focus on Theosophy.71
     Hilma’s  first  experience  in mediumship  occurred  before  her
involvement with The Five. In the fall of 1891, Hilma notes in one of
her notebooks:
   My first experience with mediumship occurred when the
       painter, Valborg Hallström was using a psychograph in her
      studio and my interest aroused. I asked if a few words could
     be said to me through the instrument. It was said immedi­
       ately: Go calmly on your way and when I wanted further
      explanations they continued through life.72
She underscored this spirit message in her notes.


## PDF page 49
A Brief Biography of Hilma af Klint                 43


    The psychograph, mentioned earlier and described here, was a
device widely used in spiritualist circles, including Valerius’s Cloverleaf
Society. This instrument was constructed with a circular wooden frame,
equipped with an indicator placed above a plate, comprising all the let­
ters of the alphabet. Although this device was different, the process of
using this instrument was like that of an Ouija board, except with one
person placing their hand on the frame; the hand unconsciously moves
the indicator, pointing to letters until it spelled out words.73 Hilma makes
another notation of a spirit message, again underscoring the sentence: “It
was said to me: Hilma will receive a great gift if she never forgets the
power of the Highest.”74
      Influential Historical Symbolism and Rosicrucianism
Although there is a lack of substantial details—Hilma often associated
herself with the Rosicrucians both in public and documents, despite there
being no evidence that she belonged to a lodge.75 She viewed herself and
her friends as part of a lineage of dissenters. The Five convened within
the symbols of a clandestine organization that saw itself as the dawning
of Christian reform movements. Based on the belief at the time, it was
said that the founder of the Rosicrucians, Frater Christian Rosenkreuz
(1378-1484) died the same year that Martin Luther (1483-1546) was
born. However, we now know that he was born a year before his passing.
Luther, according to the Rosicrucians, steered Christians back onto the
correct path: “Their task was to amplify the teachings with ancient wis­
dom, drawing on the Kabbala, Hermeticism, and alchemy.”76 This syncre­
tistic approach was followed by Hilma, however; she also combined the
religious principles of Hinduism and Buddhism with Christian ideas.77
    The Five viewed themselves as a fellowship, like that of the
Rosicrucians. April 5, 1898, Hedman had a vision that the women were
called upon to prepare for a new apprenticeship: she saw three steps that
led up to a dark painting, with a white cross gradually appearing. The
symbolism of three was later explained; “the training the women were to
undergo would last three times three months. Each phase was assigned
a concept: word, thought, and rosary. The last was understood in a double


## PDF page 50
44                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


sense, as a request to pray and as a reference to the rose.” 78 This vision is
what led the women to build an altar made of rose-engraved glass and a
wooden white cross.
     In 1903, shortly after Hilma af Klint and Anna Cassel’s time at
the Veterinary Institute, the women traveled to Italy. Their route from
Sweden to Italy brought the women through Germany. One of their
stops was at the Wartburg Castle in Thuringia; Hilma has surviving
drawings from the inside of the castle as proof of this trip.79 Her inter­
est in this particular location is fueled by its history: that was the place
where Martin Luther resided in protective custody following his ex-
communication in 1521 after he refused to recant at the Diet of Worms.
During his stay there, the Reformer utilized his time to translate the
New Testament into German. The artist remarked on the back of her
sketch, which was accentuated with watercolor, as “Luther’s bostad”—
Luther’s residence.80
     Hilma’s notebook labeled, “Italian Journey,” maps a visual journey of
traveling through Italy, sketching its historically influential architecture
and Renaissance style throughout. In Florence, she sketched Brunelleschi’s
fifteenth-century dome for the Basilica of San Lorenzo, traveling through
Northern Italy, she sketched the Grand Canal, noting “vacker” (beautiful)
in her notebooks. She and Cassel also visited the Brera Art Gallery, where
Leonardo da Vinci and Raphael were on display. Additionally, the women
were able to see Da Vinci’s famous Last Supper on the wall of the refectory
in the church of Santa Maria della Grazie.81
    The incursion of the divine seemed ever-present in daily life in
Italy: in museums, chapels, churches, and even at train stations and
souvenir stands. Everywhere she went in Italy, saints and miracles were
taken for granted. The density of the art Hilma saw in a single country
was staggering and quite different from the somberness of the Protestant
Churches in which she was raised. What she experienced in Italy was
not if miracles happened but how could they be best captured in mar­
ble, bronze, plaster, gold, chisel, paintbrush, frescoes, or oil on canvas.
Moreover, the artists of the Renaissance and Middle Ages believed that
creating art, especially painting was part of the higher spheres, to be


## PDF page 51
A Brief Biography of Hilma af Klint                 45


practiced to service higher powers. As Saint Augustine said, “per visibilia
ad invisibilia.” meaning “from the visible to the invisible.”82
                   The Expansion
As will be discussed in greater detail in later chapters, the subsections of
Chapter Three introduce events of Hilma af Klint’s life that informed
her future abstracted works beginning in 1906. This short biography is
only a part of her story that provides insight into her life as an artist and
clairvoyant. The subsequential chapters discuss in further detail religious,
technological, and artistic trends that shifted at the turn of the nine­
teenth century to the twentieth century, giving additional understanding
to the respective influences that played a significant role in Hilma’s shift
to abstract, spiritualist paintings.

## PDF page 57

Contextual Influences: Early Twentieth-Century
Changes In Science, Religion, and European Art
                        Introduction
    he early twentieth century was a period of profound transforma­
     tion marked by significant shifts in science, religion, and, conse­Tquently, the arts. These changes brought about a reevaluation of estab­
lished artistic norms and ushered in new aesthetic paradigms. Scientific
advancements during this era, notably in physics with the development
of quantum theory and Albert Einstein’s (1879-1955) theory of rela­
tivity, challenged conventional perceptions of the universe.1 These theo­
ries introduced concepts such as uncertainty and the relativity of time
and space, fundamentally altering humanity’s understanding of reality.
Artists, influenced by these scientific breakthroughs, sought to capture
the dynamic and ever-changing nature of existence in their works.
     Religious landscapes were also evolving during this time. The rise
of existentialist philosophy questioned traditional religious certainties,
emphasizing individual experience and choice. Additionally, World War
I and its devastating impact fostered disillusionment and led to a reeval­
uation of spiritual beliefs. This period saw a move away from institution­
alized religion towards more personal, mystical, and sometimes esoteric
spiritual practices.
    These shifts in science and religion had a profound impact on the
art world’s aesthetic sensibilities. The traditional representation of real­
ity in art gave way to more abstract and subjective expressions. Artists
began to explore new forms, experiment with color, and abandon strict

                            51

representational conventions. The emphasis shifted from depicting the
external world to expressing inner thoughts, emotions, and the com­
plexities of the human psyche. Everything that transpired in the latter
part of innovations during the preceding century.
     In  the  late  nineteenth  century, advancements  across  various
domains laid the groundwork for a new approach to abstraction in the
arts. Natural and social scientists uncovered abstract patterns in both
nature and human experiences. The advent of novel communication
technologies enabled the transmission of messages across vast distances
through wires or open space, with scientific breakthroughs unveiling
invisible phenomena, such as the energy distribution of these messages.
     Concurrently, modernist writers ventured into experimental uses of
abstraction in literature, albeit with limited success. Meanwhile, Hilma
af Klint’s oeuvre confronted a substantial challenge to Christian under­
pinnings of knowledge, morality, and history with the rise of nihilism
and spirituality. In response, she endeavored to reaffirm these founda­
tions during an era when many sought alternatives through diverse belief
systems such as Buddhism, Hinduism, spiritualism, Theosophy, and
Anthroposophy, all of which left an imprint on her artistic endeavors.
The wellspring for her groundbreaking abstract art drew from a synthesis
of these scientific and cultural currents unique to her time, coupled with
her emotions and pursuits.2
               Invisible Science in the Atomic Age
During the era of af Klint, the interconnection between science and
spiritual thought was evident. Conventional  scientists engaged in
experiments to test ideas rooted in spiritualism. Conversely, spiritual
thinkers integrated scientific principles into their practices, partially
to authenticate their concepts. Various mystical and occult movements
grappled with significant queries prompted by scientific discoveries.
For instance, the emergence of telephones that allowed voices to tra­
verse great distances raised questions about such movements, ponder­
ing why communication with the deceased couldn’t be possible. When
exploring scientific themes in her works, af Klint likely interpreted

of relativity and quantum mechanics portrayed the universe as character­

and waves that, on a subatomic scale, could not be proven with absolute
certainty.5
                   Electromagnetic Radiation
The electromagnetic (EM) spectrum encompasses a wide array of EM
radiation types. Radiation denotes energy that travels and disperses as
it progresses—examples include the visible light emanating from a
household lamp and the radio waves emitted by a radio station.
Additionally, the electromagnetic spectrum comprises microwaves,
infrared light, ultraviolet light, X-rays, and gamma-rays. In the middle
of this spectrum are rays of visible light, detected by the human eye.
The other sides of this spectrum include the highest energy and short­
est; gamma, X-ray, and ultraviolet to the lowest energy and longest

which are invisible to the human eye.

              The Atom and Quantum Mechanics
              “spooky action at a distance” -Einstein6
Advances in quantum physics are important because they help individu­
als to better understand our material world: it explains the interactions
and behaviors between particles in addition to their driving force fields.
Over a century ago, it was likely the most abstract and least intuitive
among the theories accessible to scientists for describing and compre­
hending the world. The understanding of quantum physics in the con­
temporary world, as Pierre Henriquet, a doctorate in nuclear physics
describes, “the universe of the infinitesimal, the most obvious concepts of
our everyday experience are shattered. A particle, for example, has both
particle and wave properties. Its location is not determined by a precise
position but by a “cloud of probabilities” that makes it exist almost every­
where at once, with greater or lesser chances of finding it if we finally try
to observe it.”7
    Quantum mechanics describes the exchange of energy particles.
An electron within an atom can only emit or absorb precise amounts
of energy, unlike the macroscopic world, where the energy of a physical
object such as a tennis ball, can take on any value. The quantum world
is full of unconventional and strange rules that may appear paradoxical.
As nuclear physicist, Dr. Pierre Henriquet says for example, “a quantum

56                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


object can exist in several states simultaneously, or that two so-called
‘entangled’ particles are so fundamentally linked that  if you make a
change to one, the other will instantly suffer the consequences, regard­
less of the distance separating them.” One of the most remarkable rev­
elations of quantum physics is the well-known “wave-particle duality.”
Throughout the nineteenth century, various experiments had indicated
the wave-like characteristics of light. However, it wasn’t until 1905 when
Albert Einstein illustrated a phenomenon known as the “photoelectric”
effect, demonstrating that light could impinge on electrons and displace
them like bocce balls.8 Previous advancements in the realm of quantum
mechanics have served as foundational stepping stones, with subsequent
scientists building upon the discoveries of their predecessors to further
unravel the mysteries of the quantum world.9
   Max Planck (1858-1947), born in Kiel, made significant contri­
butions to the German scientific community as a professor in Berlin.
Despite the challenges posed by World War I, he worked tirelessly to
maintain international research collaborations. Later, as the Nazi regime
ascended to power, Planck, though opposed to their policies, chose to
remain in his position to defend independent research. It has been a
century since Max Planck published a paper that is often credited with
giving birth to quantum mechanics. However, historical accounts reveal
that Planck did not immediately grasp the implications of his work and
found himself thrust into a revolutionary role against his intentions.
According to the conventional narrative, which persists in many physics
textbooks, the emergence of quantum theory stemmed from the recog­
nition that classical physics failed to accurately predict the energy distri­
bution of black-body radiation observed in experiments.10
     In the late 1890s, German physicist Wilhelm Wien (1893-1928)
developed an expression that matched experimental data reasonably
well but lacked a solid theoretical foundation. Subsequent analyses by
Lord Rayleigh (1842-1919) and James Jeans (1877-1946), using classi­
cal physics, resulted in a spectrum that diverged significantly from both
experimental observations and Wien’s law. Faced with this perplexing
anomaly, Planck sought a solution, ultimately introducing the concept


## PDF page 63
Contextual Influences: Early Twentieth-Century Changes In Science, Religion, and European Art 57


of “energy quanta.” This groundbreaking quantum hypothesis success­
fully reconciled theory with experiment, marking the birth of quan­
tum theory.11
     Planck’s discovery in 1900, concerned the radiation spectrum of
black bodies. He observed that this spectrum only occurs with discrete
energies, separated by a value represented as hν, where ν signifies fre­
quency and h represents the Planck constant, also known as the for­
mula for the energy of a photon of frequency ν. (chemistry), a symbol
meaning a photon in a chemical equation. Classical physics predicted
that the intensity of this radiation would increase infinitely with rising
frequencies, resulting in what was termed the “ultraviolet catastrophe.”
However, Planck proposed that if black bodies exchanged energy with
the radiation field in proportion to HV (high velocity), this issue would
be resolved. This suggestion of discontinuous absorption and emission
of energy contradicted the principles of classical physics. Subsequently,
Einstein utilized Planck’s discovery in his explanation of the photoelec­
tric effect, suggesting that light waves were quantized, and each quan­
tum of light could deliver energy to electrons at the cathode in precise
multiples of hν.12
    Through his study of Brownian motion, French scientist, Jean
Baptiste Perrin (1870-1942), provided visual evidence of the existence
of atoms in 1909. In his technique, he tracked the “irregular movement
of insoluble particles in a solution as they were buffeted by molecules”,
revealing atoms.13 Long theorized as existing since the ancient Greeks,
it was not until 1897 that Ernest Rutherford discovered the electron
in 1897 and radioactive substances from subatomic particles by 1898.
The concept of the atom was frequently depicted in popular literature
as resembling a miniature solar system, with electrons orbiting around
a central nucleus. After Rutherford proved the existence of an atom
nucleus in 1911, groups of scientists who worked with Rutherford,
including Niels Bohr, began pursuing more accurate models of the
atomic structure.14
     In 1911, Rutherford conducted experiments involving the bom­
bardment of alpha particles into a gold foil. Based on these findings, he


## PDF page 64
58                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


formulated a model of the atom wherein a heavy nucleus with a posi­
tive charge was surrounded by negatively charged electrons, resembling a
miniature solar system. However, this model also conflicted with classical
physics laws. Classical mechanics and electrodynamics suggest that elec­
trons orbiting around a positively charged nucleus should continuously
emit radiation, leading to the eventual swallowing of the electrons by
the nucleus. Despite initial skepticism, Planck’s quantum theory gradu­
ally gained acceptance as it successfully explained various phenomena
beyond the reach of classical physics. His contributions laid the founda­
tion for Einstein’s explanation of the photoelectric effect and propelled
advancements in quantum mechanics. However, Planck faced personal
and professional challenges during the Nazi regime. While he remained
in Germany to defend independent research, he openly opposed the
government’s policies, particularly their persecution of members of the
Jewish community. His home was destroyed during the war, and he
endured significant hardships in its final weeks.15
    Awarded the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1918, Planck laid the
groundwork for quantum theory, offering a detailed account of the the­
ory’s inception and evolution. Initial experiments attempted to under­
stand the distribution of energy in the normal spectrum of radiating
heat phenomenon using classical electrodynamics, particularly Heinrich
Hertz’s (1857-1894) linear oscillator. Despite initial setbacks and mis­
understandings, Planck’s inquiries eventually led to the establishment of
a general connection between the energy of a resonator and the energy
radiation of the corresponding spectral region. However, further chal­
lenges arise, particularly regarding the relationship between entropy and
energy. Planck’s efforts to reconcile these concepts, drawing on existing
experimental data, particularly Wien’s energy distribution law. Through
careful calculations and analysis, a new radiation formula was derived
that successfully explains observed deviations in energy distribution for
different wavelengths.16
     Further exploration of the implications of the quantum theory con­
tinued across various fields of physics, including thermal and chemical
processes, electron impacts, and spectroscopy. Significant contributions


## PDF page 65
Contextual Influences: Early Twentieth-Century Changes In Science, Religion, and European Art 59


from other researchers, such as Einstein’s theory of specific heat and
Bohr’s atom theory, further validated the quantum hypothesis. Despite
the progress made, Planck acknowledged that the introduction of the
quantum of action did not yet lead to a comprehensive quantum theory.
At this time, challenges remained in reconciling quantum principles
with classical physics. However, Planck emphasized the significance of
the quantum theory in advancing our understanding of the fundamental
principles governing the universe. While acknowledging ongoing chal­
lenges, Planck remained optimistic about resolving these issues and the
broader implications for physics as a whole.17
     Furthermore, the advancements in quantum theory pioneered by
Planck and further elucidated by Einstein marked a critical turning
point in the scientific landscape, profoundly influencing the realms of
philosophy, literature, and more notably, the art world. These ground­
breaking discoveries challenged traditional notions of reality and per­
ception, inspiring artists to explore new concepts of space, time, and
the nature of existence in their creative endeavors. The integration of
scientific principles into artistic expression became a hallmark of the
modernist movement, fostering a deeper understanding of the intercon­
nectedness between science and art.
             New Religious Trends
During the mid-1800s, the movement known as spiritualism, origi­
nating in the United States, gained significant popularity in Industrial
Revolution-era Britain. Spiritualism was based on the belief in a spirit
realm and the possibility of earthly interaction with it. As interest in
the scientific method grew within mainstream society, spiritualists
began to interpret their sensory experiences as empirical evidence of
the spirit world, revealing an intriguing intersection between the mate­
rial and mystical domains.18
     Additionally, the bloodiest conflict in the history of the United
States was the American Civil War (1861-1865), killing nearly seven-
hundred and fifty thousand soldiers. The number of American fatalities
equals nearly the total amount of deaths in the Revolutionary War, the


## PDF page 66
60                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


War of 1812, the Mexican-American War, the Spanish-American War,
World War I, World War II, and the Korean War combined.19 In peri­
ods of grief, bereaved individuals explore various coping strategies, and
the process of mourning has undergone significant evolution over time.
Frequently, individuals seek solace and a sense of connection with the
departed through organized religion or spirituality.
    During the mid-nineteenth century, as the United States under­
went significant expansion, a notable economic contrast arose between
its northern and southern regions. In the north, manufacturing and
industry thrived, with agriculture primarily consisting of small-scale
farms. In contrast, the South’s economy revolved around large-scale
farming, heavily reliant on the labor of Black enslaved individuals for
cultivating specific crops, particularly cotton and tobacco. The growing
opposition to slavery in the North led to the South fearing the aboli­
tion of what many Southerners at the time viewed as the backbone of
the economy. This inevitably stirred conflict that eventually turned into
the outbreak of the Civil War after Abraham Lincoln (1809-1865)
took office.20
     Spiritism rapidly expanded during and after the Civil War as
it provided grieving survivors and the loved ones of those lost, clo­
sure that the war had denied them.21 As historian Drew Gilpin Faust
states, “The particular circumstances of the Civil War often inhibited
mourning, rendering it difficult, if not impossible, for many bereaved
Americans to move through the stages of grief. In an environment
where information about deaths was often wrong or entirely unavail­
able, survivors found themselves both literally and figuratively unable
to ‘see clearly what… has been lost’.”22 During a period when grieving
individuals sought renewed assurance of continuity and justice after
death and traditional religious beliefs seemed less capable of providing
such reassurance, Spiritist activity surged in America.23 As an example,
séances were employed to connect with departed loved ones, facilitated
by a skilled medium who purported to possess the mystical capability
to communicate with the deceased.24

Contextual Influences: Early Twentieth-Century Changes In Science, Religion, and European Art 61

             From Spiritualism to Theosophy
Spiritualism exploded during the devastation of the Civil War, but the
movement began in 1848 with the enigmatic tapping noises emerged
from beneath the kitchen table of Leah (1813-1890), Margaretta
(1833-1893) and Catherine Fox (1837-1892), later known as the Fox
sisters, in Hydesville, New York. Spiritualism commended itself to
several elite American abolitionists such as Lydia Maria Child (1802-
1880), Harriet Beecher Stowe (1811-1896), George Ripley (1802-
1880), William Lloyd Garrison (1805-1879), William Cullen Bryant
(1794-1878) and Sarah (1792-1873) and Angelina Grimke (1805-
1879). Possibly due to mediums, often women, articulating messages
that resonated with women’s rights, several early feminists, such as
Susan B. Anthony (1820-1906), Elizabeth Cady Stanton (1815-
1902), and Frances Willard (1839-1898), found common cause with
the movement.25
     Spiritualism was a popular phenomenon rather than elite, that
began to spread thanks to P. T. Barnum (1810-1891), most known for
the Barnum & Bailey Circus, who took the Fox sisters on tour. Although
historian Jon Butler (born in 1940) previously restricted the clientele of
spiritualism to be mostly white, middle-upper class former Protestants,
contemporary researcher, Ann D. Braude, current director of the Harvard
Divinity School, uncovered significant diversity in support of spiritual­
ism. Among them were people of color, women, rural and urban laborers,
southerners, and Catholics. The accumulation of diversity in individu­
als who supported the movement and a large fascination with mediums
as seen in letters of ordinary people immediately before the Civil War,
demonstrates that spiritualism was an immensely popular movement in
nineteenth-century America.26
    The appeal of spiritualism to ordinary people in nineteenth-century
America lay in its democratic nature. A believer from the mid-century
affirmed, “Spiritualism is democratic; it speaks to the common people,
and we are all common people.” In addition to more established reli­
gious groups like Methodists and Baptists, spiritualists challenged the


## PDF page 68
62                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


authority of educated clergy and valued the innate wisdom of ordinary
individuals. They rejected the need for external authorities to connect
them with God or Truth, allowing each person, regardless of social sta­
tus or gender, to follow their conscience freely. Often, this conscience
led spiritualists to embrace ideals of freedom, equality, and democracy.
Consequently, spiritualism emerged as part of a broader trend of popu­
list religious movements reshaping the religious landscape of mid-nine­
teenth-century America.27
   Among the people living in that democratic society was a young
man who later became a prominent figure in Theosophical Society, Henry
Steel Olcott (1832-1907). Born to middle-class Presbyterian parents in
Orange, New Jersey, Olcott left the University of the City of New York
during his freshman year. He traveled westward, eventually arriving in
northern Ohio, where he encountered different spiritual beliefs prevalent
in the “burned-over district.” By the age of twenty, Olcott had embraced
spiritualism. In 1853, he returned to New York where he continued to
devote a majority of his energy to the spiritualist cause.28
    From 1853 to 1856, under the pseudonym of “Amherst,” he
authored more than a dozen articles in the New York-based newspaper,
Spiritual Telegraph. One of his most systematic pieces, The Spiritualist’s
Faith, Olcott, presented a sweeping historical and moral defense of the
movement. He started by proposing an evolutionary view of the universe,
where everything progresses “from lowest to highest, from bad to good,
from small to great ... upward, onward, to something more perfect, more
Divine!” He then organized all religious traditions according to this evo­
lutionary and hierarchical principle. His historical narrative began with
fetishism (immersed in barbarism, low and debased in human scale),
progressed to Islam (considered better than fetishism but still rooted in
satisfaction of desires and desire for dominion, and reached its pinnacle
with spiritualism grounded in reasoning, optimism, joyfulness, kindness,
spirituality, reverence, and universal acceptance.29
    Democratic and elitist impulses alike played major roles in the evo­
lutionary meta-narrative of Olcott. While some individuals or religions
were more advanced than others, the hierarchy was neither granted nor


## PDF page 69
Contextual Influences: Early Twentieth-Century Changes In Science, Religion, and European Art 63


static, since all were equally capable of being uplifted by the unstoppable
force of what was optimal. Therefore, not from a state of unchanging
equality, but from a perspective where everything was being elevated.
In this universal drive for improvement and societal change, spiritual­
ism played a crucial role in promoting upright, dignified, conscientious,
joyful, and intellectually engaged lives. Spiritualism contributed to the
“progression and elevation” of individuals, leading to “a contented house­
hold,” and ultimately to a harmonious community.30 As Olcott states in
The Spiritualist’s Faith:
      [Spiritualism] cultivates the sentiment of love, both to God
     and to man; it fosters true manhood; it makes demonstrably
      certain the fact of immortality; it extinguishes all forms of
      tyrannical governments, and thus is most democratic in its
      tendencies; it reconciles opposing factions, uniting North
     and South in common interests and a common destiny; it
     sweeps away all false religious organizations, retaining only
     what is true; it drives the money-changers from the temple.31
Two decades preceding his turn to spiritualism in the 1850s, Olcott dis­
tinguished himself as a New York Tribune reporter, an experimental agri­
culturist, an antislavery advocate, a government investigator of Lincoln’s
assassination, a civil service reformer, and an insurance lawyer. Thus, he
earned notoriety as a social reformer in addition to admission into the
Lotos Club, an esteemed gentlemen’s social group in Manhattan’s lit­
erary and artistic elite. Olcott firmly established himself in the social
stratum of New York City. Unlike an earlier intellectual class that looked
to English figures such as David Hume (1711-1776), this new gentry
admired Matthew Arnold’s (1822-1888) ideals. Members of this social
class, including Olcott, were convinced of their cultural superiority and
cultivated their literary interests through magazines such as Harper’s
Magazine, Atlantic Monthly, and North American Review, as well as
newspapers such as the New York Tribune.32
     Situated between the patrician gentry and the emerging middle
class, the metropolitan gentry struggled with a core problem: balancing


## PDF page 70
64                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


democracy with standards. Many, including Olcott, identified as radical
Republicans, yet their social identities were heavily influenced by a desire
for order and hierarchy, which they believed were under threat from
urbanization and immigration. They sought to maintain social stability
through initiatives aimed at educating and guiding the masses. Members
of the metropolitan gentry inhabited a complex space between populism
and elitism. Two main assumptions informed their worldview: the belief
that the masses could aspire to improve themselves. Secondly, it was the
duty of the elites to lift the masses. These beliefs formed the foundation
of their genteel reform.33
    Theosophy began with the  collision of spiritualism from two
social-class preoccupations that were antithetical to the democratic
inclinations of spiritualists: Olcott and the aristocratic occultist Helena
Petrovna Blavatsky (1831-1891). Blavatsky, recognized by her contem­
poraries for her great spirit, was born Helena Hahn von Rottenstern in
Ekaterinoslav, Ukraine. Her father served as an officer in the Russian
army, and her mother was a renowned novelist. At a young age, she
married Nikifor Blavatsky, a provincial governor appointed by the czar.
However, she abandoned her husband shortly after their marriage and
fled to Constantinople. A host of biographies on Blavatsky fails to estab­
lish the next twenty-five years of Blavatsky’s life. Although Blavatsky
made several attempts to organize spiritual circles, her lack of organiza­
tion could never meet her charismatic character which attracted people
in the first place. While in New York, she came across a series of let­
ters penned by Olcott in the New York Daily Graphic concerning alleged
spirit materializations at the Eddy family farmhouse in Chittenden,
Vermont. It is suspected she sought a publicist to promote her esoteri­
cism and a like-minded individual with spiritual gifts that she lacked. In
Colonel Olcott, she found both.34
    The meeting of “H.S.O” and “H.P.B.”—as they referred to them­
selves—has become legendary. Olcott offered Blavatsky a light for her ciga­
rette, marking the beginning of a partnership fueled by mutual admiration
and commitment to reform. Blavatsky’s unconventional attire, including a
bright red Garibaldian shirt, intrigued Olcott, and their initial encounter


## PDF page 71
Contextual Influences: Early Twentieth-Century Changes In Science, Religion, and European Art 65


quickly evolved into a shared critique of spiritualism’s moral and philo­
sophical shortcomings. Their reform efforts commenced with a series of
articles inspired by Blavatsky’s teachings, challenging spiritualism’s moral
integrity and philosophical depth. Previously, Olcott had chastised spiritu­
alists for their blind faith, but now, guided by Blavatsky’s wisdom, he pro­
posed a revolutionary theory on spiritual origins, influenced by her esoteric
knowledge. Their reform efforts with Olcott began in 1875 with a series
of Blavatsky-inspired and Olcott-authored articles published in Spiritual
Scientist, a critique that amounted to a two-fold argument that spiritual­
ism was both philosophically naive and morally backward.35
    From the outset, there existed a contrast between Olcott’s refined
demeanor and Blavatsky’s aristocratic background regarding the struc­
ture and goals of the organization. However, as the society’s spokesper­
son and primary author, Olcott’s influence was prominent in shaping
its image and objectives. During a significant gathering of spiritual­
ists, Olcott enthusiastically introduced the concept of a “Theosophical
Society,” focused on exploring the scientific principles underlying spiri­
tual phenomena, such as psychometry, magnetism, and occultism.36 The
overarching ambition of the newly formed society was ambitious: “to
seek a knowledge of God and higher spirits.” Its goal—“a new epoch for
Science and Religion,” was even more magnificent:
      This Theosophical Society offers the only neutral ground upon
     which [science and religion] can meet and compound their
       struggle. To the church it offers proof that the soul is immor­
        tal, at the once final and irresistible; to science, those mathe­
      matical demonstrations of new forces and an unseen universe
      the lack of which has hitherto sent its votaries adrift in that
      current whose vortex sucks them into Infidelity, Darkness,
     and Despair. To the Universe, Occultism restores an appre­
       ciable First Cause, and it floods the sky of the philosophical
      student with the dawn of an Eternal Day of Knowledge.37
Olcott had a deep understanding of the Theosophical movement,
which aimed to disseminate Eastern philosophies and practices. It


## PDF page 72
66                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


was an organization dedicated to enhancing spiritualism both mor­
ally and intellectually. Functioning as both a scientific institution and
a platform for social reform, it bore a resemblance to organizations
like the American Anti-Slavery Society and the American Academy
of Arts and Sciences rather than the Brahmo Samaj. Olcott depicted
Theosophists as diligent investigators committed to studying and veri­
fying all aspects of spirituality through rigorous inquiry and experi­
mentation. Their approach involved studying existing phenomena,
conducting their own experiments, and drawing conclusions based on
empirical evidence. Their ultimate goal was to uncover the fundamental
laws governing spiritual mechanics. Through this process, they sought
to reconcile naturalistic scientific principles with religious beliefs, lead­
ing to individual moral growth and the advent of a new era character­
ized by universal truth.38
    Given the Theosophical Society’s growing focus on Asia, it’s note­
worthy that Olcott’s narrative, which embodies modernity, didn’t include
any allusions to Eastern wisdom. In his initial presidential speech, he
mentioned figures like Albertus Magnus (1200-1280), Alfarabi, Roger
Bacon (1214-1292), and others, along with various mystical traditions
such as Chaldeans, Kabbalists, and Rosicrucians. However, his references
didn’t extend beyond the Near East, where Blavatsky’s “masters” hailed
from, a clandestine occult group entrusted with preserving ancient
knowledge throughout history.39
    The society aimed to explore mysterious occurrences and acquire
supernatural abilities but lacked a mentor. This prompted Olcott and
Blavatsky to seek an  alternative  instructor, while also claiming to
communicate with mystical experts. In 1877, Blavatsky authored Isis
Unveiled, which examined the intersection of Western beliefs with occult
practices and spiritual phenomena. Notably, she sought inspiration from
Egyptian traditions rather than Tibetan masters, who later influenced
Theosophical principles. Blavatsky’s prior residence in Egypt led her
to claim encounters with occult masters there. Accepting Blavatsky’s
accounts, Olcott sent a society member to Egypt and Tunis in search of
one of the numerous “African wizards” mentioned by Blavatsky. Egypt

Contextual Influences: Early Twentieth-Century Changes In Science, Religion, and European Art 67


was perceived as a land of magic, while the Middle East remained enig­
matic in the Theosophical imagination.40
                         Spiritual Science
The clear parallel of science and spirituality can be seen through Eastern
beliefs. According to Hindu philosophy, the fundamental operational
principles of the universe are sattva, rajas, and tamas. In terms of material
substance, later discovered, these three gunas correspond to the neutral
neutron (sattva), the positively charged proton (rajas), and the negatively
charged electron (tamas) of the physical atom. Modern quantum physics
has demonstrated that these three functional components of the atom
consist of elementary particles. These elementary particles include twelve
fermions (six quarks and six leptons) and four bosons (virtual force-car­
rying particles). As of 2012, the addition of a fifth boson, known as the
Higgs particle responsible for mass generation, has finalized the con­
temporary standard model of quantum physics, explaining everything in
the material universe except for the force of gravitation. There is a pos­
sibility that sub-quark particles exist, although current physical instru­
ments cannot detect them.41

observed or visualized using clairvoyant abilities before the discoveries

    Edwin D. Babbitt (1828-1905), physician, Doctor of Law, found­
ing member of the Theosophical Society, and father of early color theory
wrote The Principles of Light and Color published in 1878. This text is
an extensive exploration of the foundational concepts surrounding light
and color. Babbitt delves into the physics of light, the intricacies of color
perception, and the principles governing color mixing and harmony. The
book not only examines the theoretical aspects but also explores their
practical applications in various fields such as art, design, and photogra­
phy.43 Interestingly, the full title is Principles of Light and Color: Including
among Other Things the Harmonic Laws of the Universe, the Etherio -
Atomic Philosophy of Force, Chromo Chemistry, Chromo Therapeutics and the
General Philosophy of the Fine Forces, Together with Numerous Discoveries
and Practical Applications.
     Before scientists such as Planck made profound scientific discover­
ies about atoms, Babbitt discussed atoms philosophically:
    One of the most unfortunate things for the scientific world
        is that the law of atomic action has never been ascertained.
      All science both of the visible and the invisible must be defi­
      cient in its fundamental principles until it is known how
     atoms combine and work. Atoms have amazing activities
     and chemical potencies, but how can we get into the real
     knowledge of light, color, electricity, magnetism and force of
      every kind as connected with matter, unless we know how
      matter itself is constituted?44
He later stated:
     Atoms are the primary and indivisible particles of things. To
     understand them fully would be almost to understand infin­
        ity. In fact, we cannot understand the exact nature of the
      simplest object without apprehending its atomic constitu­
       tion. We shall not be real philosophers until we can reach far

     back toward primates and thence onward toward ultimates.
    We shall be but poor chemists so long as we cannot tell the
     law of the atomic action in any substance whatever, or the
      basic principles of chemical affinity.45
Babbitt’s diagram of “the general Form of the Atom” was published in
The Principles of Light and Color (Figure 6), in which he describes the
form of the atom after extensive research into the atomic structure to
align with scientific discoveries and natural principles, the conclusion is
drawn that this depiction outlines a typical atom capable of manifesting
all colors. According to Babbitt, this understanding suggests the pres­
ence of seven intra-spirals on the atom’s exterior for warm or thermal
colors, termed the thermolumino group. These spirals contribute to the
generation of both warm and electrical colors, with their size determin­
ing the heat intensity. Positive thermospirals at positions 3, 3, exhibit
larger sizes and higher heat compared to negative thermospirals at 5, 5.46
Even though Babbitt’s diagram of the atom is incorrect, it is a fascinat­
ing interpretation of the time and reflects imagery made by Hilma af
Klint, as will be discussed in Chapter Four.
                       Rudolf Steiner
The Theosophical Society had become a worldwide phenomenon by the
turn of the twentieth century, boasting a membership of one hundred
thousand. Rudolf Steiner (1861-1925) was appointed as the inaugural
Secretary General of the German Section of the Theosophical Society
on October 19, 1902. Joining the Theosophical Society provided Steiner
with a platform, an established audience, organizational infrastructure,
and invaluable exposure to the leading New Age spiritual community.
The popularity of the Theosophical Society indicated a widespread inter­
est in concepts such as reincarnation, karma, maya, and kamaloca, among
other Eastern and alternative spiritual ideas. Steiner benefited from the
support of Marie von Sivers (1867-1948), a capable and multilingual
personal assistant provided by the Theosophical Society.47 Sivers would
later become Steiner’s second wife.


## PDF page 76
70                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


    For Steiner, the Theosophical Society served as an ideal training
ground for his future endeavors, laying the groundwork for the establish­
ment of the Anthroposophical Society a decade later. Under Steiner’s
leadership, the German Section of the Theosophical Society experienced
significant growth, increasing its membership from three hundred and
seventy-seven in 1905 to three thousand, seven hundred two in 1913.
Steiner also generated income through ticket sales for his lectures and
Mystery Plays, as well as from book sales through his personal pub­
lishing house, Philosophisch-Theosophischer Verlag. Additionally, the
Johannes-Bau-Verein (Johannes Building Association) was founded in
1911, independent of the Theosophical Society, to construct a theatre in
Munich to showcase Steiner’s plays. However, the municipal authori­
ties in Munich rejected the building application in 1912, prompting a
shift towards constructing the Goetheanum in Dornach, Switzerland.
The Anthroposophical Society was officially established on December
28, 1912, in Cologne, Germany, attracting most of the members of the
German section of the Theosophical Society. Subsequently, Steiner was
expelled from the Theosophical Society on March 7, 1913. The foun­
dation stone for the Goetheanum, initially known as the Johannesbau,
was laid on September 20, 1913. The Theosophical Society served as
a model and launching pad for the establishment and growth of the
Anthroposophical Society.48
    As described, in 1905, under the stewardship of Steiner, the
Theosophical Society grew from three-hundred seventy-seven mem­
bers to nearly tenfold by 1913. By the time Steiner formed the
Anthroposophical Society, the memberships were mostly composed of
personal followers, likely due to high theatricality, charisma, and prolix­
ity, often mesmerizing the audiences of his lectures.49 Many of his loyal
devotees were women as was professed by Annie Besant (1847-1933),
“Let us add that Steiner exercised an increasing influence on his dis­
ciples, and they, who in 1914 already numbered some four thousand and
among who there were many women had for his admiration and ven­
eration equal to what the ‘orthodox’ Theosophists—if one may use this
word here.”50

      It is uncertain when af Klint met Rudolf Steiner. By 1908, af Klint
had records of sending Steiner letters—at this point, he had already
soared to stardom in the Theosophical world. He worked long hours
daily, giving one or more lectures per day. Opening all his mail and
replying to his senders would have been a full-time job in itself. Before
his great popularity, he studied science and mathematics and worked as
a private tutor in Vienna. He then went to work in the Goethe-Schiller
archive  for over a decade, meticulously editing Johann Wolfgang
Goethe’s (1749-1832) nature drawings.51
     Steiner is first seen in af Klint’s notes in early 1908, indicating
that she attended one of his lectures in Stockholm while she was work­
ing on the series Evolution and The Seven-Pointed Star. On the evening
of March 30, 1908, she arrived at the Swedish Society of Medicine’s
elegant auditorium to hear Steiner speak in German on “Goethe’s

Theosophical Answer to the Riddle of Humanity.” The two lectures
followed during this week called, “Guiding Themes in the Ring of the
Nibelung” and “The Initiation.” Af Klint approached Steiner after one
of his lectures asking several questions regarding where he could put
her in touch with the Rosicrucians. She also wanted to know if he
thought her work was influenced by the secret society. The meaning
and the history of the Rosicrucians was an area of expertise for Steiner.
He gave lectures in Munich on them titled, “The Theosophy of the
Rosicrucians.” His answers to her questions were not documented in
her notebooks.52
    Hilma af Klint created one of her darkest series called The Swan
(Figure 7), in 1914 when war was exploding throughout Europe.
Migrating birds had long epitomized Sweden since the 1906 publica­
tion of Nobel Prize-winner Selma Lagerlöf ’s (1858-1940) book, The
Wonderful Adventures of Nils Holgersson, a story of a young protagonist
led on a journey by wild geese through Sweden. At this time though,
the deadly war was beginning on the ground in the east, stretching
from the Baltic to the Dead Sea, and in the west, German troops occu­
pied Luxemburg and Belgium, advancing to northern France. Only
the Netherlands, Denmark, Norway, and Sweden remained neutral.
When an article on the Malmö Exhibition was published in the wom­
en’s magazine Idun, highlighting their contribution to the Women’s
Association, it was overshadowed by reports of the escalating war,
featuring images of a German Zeppelin bombing the cathedral in
Antwerp, and scenes depicting the German cavalry and the devasta­
tion of the town hall in Leuven, Belgium.53
     Steiner broke away from the Theosophical Society and founded the
Anthroposophical Society right before the outbreak of war. After World
War I, members of the society began applying Steiner’s ideas to spark
a cultural transformation such as in medicine, farming, and traditional
and special education.54 While Theosophy emphasized obtaining wis­
dom from the divine, Anthroposophy internalized this wisdom, focusing
on human wisdom that integrated natural sciences and was influenced
by Christian Gnosticism, while also incorporating polytheistic beliefs
