Contextual Influences: Early Twentieth-Century Changes In Science, Religion, and European Art 73


and the concept of reincarnation.55 According to the Hilma af Klint
Foundation, Hilma did not become a member of the Anthroposophical
Society until 1920.56
                    European Art
The late nineteenth century was a time of discoveries, introducing a
new foundation of abstraction in the arts. Scientists across both natu­
ral and social sciences uncovered patterns of abstraction in both the
human experience and within nature. New technologies in communi­
cation made it possible to send messages over great distances through
space or wires, harnessing new forces, invisible to the human eye. With
limited success, Modernist writers experimented with ways to use
abstraction in poems and novels. Additionally, with the emergence of
nihilism, Hilma af Klint’s world encountered significant challenges to
the Judeo-Christian underpinnings of history, morality, and knowl­
edge. She, along with other artists, sought to restore religious beliefs
through alternative systems of thought such as spiritualism, Hinduism,
Buddhism, Theosophy, Rosicrucionism, and  later, Anthroposophy,
influencing the art world.57 Hence, the cultural shifts, resulted in a
massive rewriting of traditional artistic production, an unprecedented
shift that had not been seen since the Renaissance. A transformation
that fundamentally shaped the creative practices of artists in the cen­
tury that followed.58
     Since the time of ancient cave paintings, the main goal of picto­
rial art was to portray recognizable objects. In the Modernist period,
abstraction was a revolutionary stylistic development that explicitly
rejected realism’s goal of accurately capturing identifiable objects in
the natural observable world.59 In several American and European cit­
ies, at the turn of the twentieth century, artists slowly began present­
ing work that differed drastically from the long history of almost all
work that had preceded them in the Western tradition: they aban­
doned the idea of painting a picture of something, totally shunning
depictions of objects in the world and displayed works devoid of dis­
cernible subject matter.60


## PDF page 80
74                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

                         Simultaneity
New inventions transformed communication in the nineteenth cen­
tury, and with  it, how one experienced space and time. Electronic
communication began in the 1830s with the telegraph, morse code
was then introduced in 1846, the telephone was invented in 1876, and
wireless in 1894: these all worked by detaching communication from
transportation. Previously, one would have to travel to communicate
with a person in a distant place, however, these technologies allowed
communication through wires or messages to be sent over airwaves
without physically seeing the individual. This combined with the new
cinema invented in the late 1880s with its quick editing and montages,
these technologies engendered a new sense of simultaneity, enabling
multiple events from distant locations to be experienced concurrently
in a single place. At the time, commentators noted that these new
technologies had “annihilated time and space.”61 Swedish historian,
Pär Bergmann, argued in 1970 that on the eve of World War I, known
before World War II as the “Great War,” in many realms, “succession
gave way to simultaneity”, characterizing this entire period as “an age
of simultaneity.”62
    These technologies increased the pathways and speed of com­
munication, thereby enriching and complicating possibilities. Marcel
Proust (1871-1922) wrote in Remembrance of Things Past (1913-1927)
wrote about telephone conversations in language that evoked the con­
ditions of a séance. Proust wrote that telephonic conversation is an
“admirable sorcery.” which brings before us, “invisible but present, the
person to whom we have been wishing to speak, and who, while still
sitting at his table … finds himself suddenly transported hundreds of
miles.” Telephone operators are “priestesses of the Invisible” who bring
us the sound of “distance overcome.”63
      The Consequence of Modernity on European Painting
Writing on the evolution of art in becoming totally non-objective, is
a very long and arduous history. A simple snapshot will be discussed


## PDF page 81
Contextual Influences: Early Twentieth-Century Changes In Science, Religion, and European Art 75


in this section for this research and how it relates directly to societal
changes and its effects on the arts will be discussed more extensively in
Chapters Four and Five.
    Throughout history, artists inevitably respond to intellectual and
material changes that are occurring around them by proposing new
notions on the value and meaning of art while cultivating radically novel
styles.64 With the emergence of economic infrastructure and industrial­
ization, new ideas about trade, and economic markets, combined with
copious fossil fuel resources, then in the form of coal, the invention of
more efficient energy-harnessing machinery and a relatively tolerant
government, developed the modern world and transformed humanity’s
relationship with the world. Eventually, industrialization restructured
Western Europe and North America, moving society towards technol­
ogy and away from agriculture, increasing urbanization, factory labor,
and ultimately a rise in Western economic power through global trade
dominance and military conquest.65
     Collectively, these developments  in Europe  are now termed
“modernity”: a vibrant societal structure characterized by ongoing mate­
rial expansion, technology-fueled acceleration, and cultural ingenuity,
in hopes of creating a society full of potential and ambition. Science
was finally successfully analyzing the laws of the world, separating emo­
tions and values from facts, however, resulted in believing imaginative,
personal, and spiritual responses to the world were merely subjective or
immature. Additionally, this rational “new” worldview focused on better
manipulation and control over the world itself. Nature was perceived as
a neutral resource, existing outside the realm of human society, ripe for
lucrative and boundless exploitation.66
    Economic, social, and political forces pushed artists towards local
margins or unaccustomed roles. As society became more secular, the
Roman Catholic Church, a traditional patron in much of Europe, slowly
began losing its power and ceased its support of the arts. Nobility, another
traditional customer of the arts, was also relinquishing its position to
the emerging commercial middle class, which propelled the Industrial
Revolution forward. Instead of the traditional patrons, the newly formed


## PDF page 82
76                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


nation-states assumed prominent roles as patrons, aiming to shape cul­
ture in alignment with the values they professed to uphold. As a result,
new styles and subjects were in demand.67
     Traditionally, for Académie, the measure by which art was judged
was by its comparisons to Classical Greece, Rome, and artists of the
Renaissance.68 These works of art made centuries earlier, were devoted
to honoring principles of Classical tradition. This foundation rests upon
mimesis or optical realism, with paintings striving to achieve a convinc­
ing illusion of three-dimensional space on a two-dimensional surface by
employing two core principles: perspective and the portrayal of forms
through the modeling of light and shadow.69
   A major shift in the traditional art aesthetic occurred towards the
end of the nineteenth century with the emergence of Symbolism. These
works celebrated the unrepresentable, the mysterious, and the impos­
sible and willfully avoided familiar or clear meanings. The goal of these
images was to free the imagination by painting a space for intangible
moods and expressing the “spiritual,” all of which were in opposition
with the materialism in everyday life. Many Symbolists appropriated the
visual language of optical realism to depict an inner psychological land­
scape, crafting works that exuded a timeless, introspective atmosphere
resonant with restless desires, foreboding, and melancholy.70 French
Symbolist, Odilon Redon (1840-1916), described works like The Cyclops
(1914) as “dreaming accompanied also by thought,” in which “the logic
of the visible” was put “in the service of the invisible”.71
    Another contributing factor was the effects war had on many art­
ists who were beginning to explore abstract imagery. While af Klint
continued to create abstract work before, during, and after World War
I, leading British writer, painter, and artillery officer, Wyndham Lewis
(1882-1957) witnessed quasi-abstracted material form from blasted
tree stumps, skeletons, and shell-craters.72 The trauma of the war killed
Lewis’s inclination to produce art as well as abstracted literature, he
wrote, “Writing—Literature—dragged me out of the abstract cul-de-sac
… The war was a sleep … Upon waking I found an altered world: and I
had changed too, very much. The geometrics which had interested me so


## PDF page 83
Contextual Influences: Early Twentieth-Century Changes In Science, Religion, and European Art 77


exclusively before, I now felt were bleak and empty.”73 For other artists,
such as Dadaists, the realities of mechanized warfare compelled them
to push even further away from convention by embracing randomness,
absurdity, and spontaneous expression—the antitheses of all that society
esteemed and valued.74
                    The Unconscious Mind
Emanuel Swedenborg (1688-1772), a Swedish scientist and religious
thinker, ahead of his time, anticipated many central ideas of the modern
age. Before his spiritual crisis in 1745, Swedenborg was a prominent
scientist, involved in founding Sweden’s first scientific journal, Daedalus
Hyperboreus. He was also recognized for his work as an inventor, engineer,
and as the Swedish assessor of salt mines. While he is now best known
for his esoteric interpretations of scripture and alleged psychic pow­
ers, his earlier scientific contributions remain noteworthy. Swedenborg
investigated the human mind and consciousness. He also explored the
unconscious in dreams as he recorded in his Journal of Dreams, a prelude
to that of Sigmund Freud (1856-1939), nearly a century before the well-
known neurologist. Remarkably, Swedenborg also anticipated many
similar ideas to that of Carl Gustav (C. G.) Jung (1875-1961), recog­
nized that rather than being meaningless, haphazard nonsense—dreams
were purposeful and aimed to tell us something. Both men believed in a
lifelong, gradual growth of the ego that developed with the wider Self.
Jung called this “individuation,” Swedenborg referred to it as “regenera­
tion,” both viewing dreams as a catalyst to insights and experiences nec­
essary for psychic development. Swedenborg also investigated “altered
states of consciousness,” as described in his Spiritual Diary.75
    Swedenborg was the first scientist to recognize the importance of
the brain’s frontal lobes as well as the existence of neurons. Furthermore,
he identified that optic lobes relate to our sense of sight, and “split-brain”
psychology with intellectual, rational character on the left side of the
brain and emotions on the right side. He also recognized the significance
of the cerebellum, which is located at the back of the skull, and believed
the divine reaches us through the cerebellum. Interestingly, some


## PDF page 84
78                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


psychologists view this as “the seat of paranormal experiences.”76 Beyond
science, Swedenborg influenced French literary figures such as Charles
Baudelaire (1821-1867), who was moved by his ideas of correspondence
between our sensory world, the physical, and the spirit. Baudelaire took
the ideas of Swedenborg and invented symbolism, as mentioned previ­
ously, the most influential aesthetic philosophy of the modern era.77
                       Automatism
In the early twentieth century, surrealists were fascinated by the uncon­
scious. Artists such as André Breton (1896-1966) and Louis Aragon
(1897-1982) studied psychiatry and medicine. They were familiar with
the handbooks of psychiatrists such as Pierre Janet (1859-1947) and
Freud and their medical research in psychoanalysis, hypnosis, mental ill­
ness, and states of consciousness. By the medical hypothesis of the time,
surrealists believed the unconscious was not easily accessible and only
expressed itself in dreams or automatism.78 Surrealists also experimented
with séances while practicing automatic writing. It was not until 1924
that surrealism was officially established with Breton’s publication of the
Manifesto of Surrealism.79 Even though this process is often explored as
revolutionary in the creative realm, artists such as Georgiana Houghton
(1814-1884) were doing this more than sixty years before. Hilma af
Klint and The Five were also practicing automatism approximately thirty
years earlier.80 The difference was that surrealists were exploring inter­
nal, unexplored places of the mind, while spiritualists were attempting
to connect externally with the spirit world. Automatic writing quickly
became automatic drawings, as also seen with previous artists.
    Automatism, viewed as a channel and the individual as a record­
ing instrument, af Klint deployed automatism in the same manner as
the surrealists, “to move beyond the confines of her original artistic
training.”81 This technique, above all, was considered liberating as it frees
one from authorship, from intentionally and consciously envisioning a
work of art from one’s mind and then illustrating it actively. Instead, one
can rely upon unconscious associations and chance in creating work in a
way that always puts thoughts of the psyche to come through. Therefore,


## PDF page 85
Contextual Influences: Early Twentieth-Century Changes In Science, Religion, and European Art 79


automatism liberates the artist from the constraints of morality, rational­
ity, and artistic training. It is difficult to unlearn things, meaning trained
painters and poets often struggle to authentically create from the uncon­
scious rather than their work being mediated by techniques they have
been taught.82
                     Beyond Wor(l)ds
Many artists that are recognized in our history books today as the found­
ers of modern abstract art, along with Hilma af Klint, were members of
the Theosophical Society, such as Wassily Kandinsky (1886-1944), Piet
Mondrian (1872-1944) and Frantisek Kupka (1871-1957). At the dawn
of the materialistic age, Theosophy gave artists a fresh vantage point
from a spiritualist perspective, allowing them, they believed, to see into
otherworldly realms beyond our natural world. They “stood in this door­
way between worlds as messengers, and communicating this knowledge
became the objective of their art.”83 One of the most fundamental teach­
ings in Theosophy is that everyone has the same physical and spiritual
origin because they are “essentially of one—infinite, uncreated and eter­
nal, whether we call it God or Nature.”84 These tenets and philosophies
of Theosophy were appealing to free-spirited souls with an artistic tem­
perament who were seekers of a higher cosmic truth and found this way
of thinking to be liberating.85
    German physicist Heinrich Hertz (1857-1894) demonstrated
the existence of electromagnetic waves in 1886 and achieved wire­
less transmission over a distance in 1888, pioneering the develop­
ment of wireless telegraphy and radio communication. Hertz’s experi­
ments revealed the presence of previously undetectable waves in space,
challenging Western human perception of reality. In 1895, Wilhelm
Conrad Röntgen (1845-1923), introduced a groundbreaking method
of visualizing the invisible: the X-rays he discovered enabled the visu­
alization of internal structures within the human body, earning him the
inaugural Nobel Prize in Physics.
     In 1897, Henri Becquerel (1852-1908) along with his student
Marie Curie (1867-1934) as part of his team, discovered radioactivity,


## PDF page 86
80                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


which explained the disintegration of uranium atoms and generally dem­
onstrated that atoms were neither stable nor a solid.86 From these discov­
eries, it was understood reality as people knew it, was much broader than
what was previously accepted as actuality.87
   Many artists in these years, turned away from the visible as they
became interested in connecting with this greater perspective of reality
or even, multiple realities. In a lecture in 1911, futurist artist, Umberto
Boccioni (1882-1916), declared, “It is not the visible that should be
painted but that which until now was regarded as invisible, namely, what
the clairvoyant painter sees.”88 Af Klint’s abstract work that predates the
dawn of abstract art in Europe and Russia is not concerned with the pure
abstraction of shapes and color for its own sake but rather, an attempt to
capture what is not visible. Originally an Expressionist, Kandinsky too,
gradually left visible reality in his work. Mondrian moved away from
figurative portrayals and reduced his compositions to a play of horizontal
and vertical lines with primary colors. “As a Theosophist, he was striv­
ing for a purely spiritual expression of eternal ideas beyond the visible
world.”89 Thus, the spiritual search became a fundamental element for
many modernists moving towards abstracted imagery.90

       Analysis of the Tree of Knowledge

                        Introduction
      umanity’s relationship to the world drastically transformed during
      the Age of the Enlightenment (1685-1815): a complex, political,Hintellectual, and social movement that fundamentally altered the Western
perception of their place in the world, instilling them with a fresh sense
of assurance and optimism about advancement. Enlightenment philoso­
phers contended that without challenging and updating traditional ide­
ologies and customs, subsequent generations would be burdened with
the superstitions and biases of their forebears. They unveiled the irratio­
nality, brutality, and inequity perpetuated by religious institutions that
wielded authority in European societies, sanctioning violent crusades and
censorship of scientific inquiry, stifling innovation across all domains.
Furthermore, advocated for a more humanistic and secular society based
on progress and reason and was universally independent from tradi­
tion. During this time, industrialization developed in the modern world,
making way for advancing technology and scientific advancements.1
                      Theory of Evolution
Charles Darwin’s (1809-1882)  theory  of  evolution, published  his
renowned book, On the Origin of Species by Means of Natural Selection, or
the Preservation of Favored Races in the Struggle for Life in 1859, is noted
as a triumph of scientific investigation and appreciated by many for his
work in entomology and botany.2 More specifically, Darwin’s theory of

                            87


## PDF page 94
88                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

natural selection is considered one of the most revolutionary scientific
advancements of the nineteenth century. This scientific breakthrough
significantly influenced the cultural backdrop of af Klint’s art.3
    Though it was an intriguing theory among scientists, Darwin’s stud­
ies significantly weakened the theological foundation on which human
life had long been established.4 Darwin’s theory connected humans with
the animal kingdom, introducing a ruthless struggle for survival as the
mechanism of evolution and establishing simple adaptation as the pri­
mary basis for selection and survival. This theory was often cited to jus­
tify war, imperialism, and racism. It challenged the creation narrative
in Genesis by suggesting that humans were not created in the image of
God but instead evolved from primitive matter through random and
unconscious processes driven by chance mutations, a concept Darwin
himself struggled to fully comprehend.5
    Nihilism: Philosophical Consideration of Scientific Progress
German philosopher  Friedrich Nietzsche (1844-1900), studied by
Rudolf Steiner before becoming involved in Theosophy, argued that
the outcome of the Enlightenment was not a society characterized by
increased freedom and equality, but rather nihilism—meaning, “nothing-
ism”, the belief in life’s inherent meaninglessness, where all choices hold
equal emptiness. Nietzsche attributed nihilism to the absence of God
in society. Despite this perceived loss, everyday life continued as if no
significant changes had occurred. He explained that although so much
could now be explained without God, individuals continued to live as if
the traditional meanings associated with God’s existence still provided
purpose to their lives. While the common populace may have found sol­
ace in these comforting illusions, those pursuing truth were challenged
to confront the stark reality. Nietzsche famously declared, “God is dead,
and we killed him,” emphasizing the profound shift in worldview and
the necessity for individuals to confront this existential truth.6
     Nietzsche viewed nihilism as a chance to break free from Christianity’s
dehumanizing aspects, which centered around humanity’s inherent sinful­
ness and dependence on Jesus’s crucifixion for redemption.7 While he


## PDF page 95
Analysis of the Tree of Knowledge and the Atom Series            89

rejected the Christ, he celebrated Jesus’s strong willpower and spiritual
strength which helped him craft his philosophy of the Overman in Thus
Spoke Zarathustra (1883) with speeches that resembled those of biblical
figures.8 In the creative realm and through the lens of confronting existen­
tial truths, the role of the artist shifted and viewed as having a dual pur­
pose: either to reveal the illusions and disconnect of modern life or to use
their art to combat contemporary nihilism by fostering a deeper connec­
tion and harmony with the world.9





           Figure 8. Hilma af Klint, Evolution, No. 4 (1908). Oil on canvas, 104 x 133.5 cm
                           Courtesy of The Hilma af Klint Foundation
      It is tempting to link the rise of abstract art to nihilism as some
writers and critics have done, suggesting that abstraction emerged as a
response to or representation of nihilism. While nihilism and abstract
art both surfaced around the same period, and certain abstract artists
directly acknowledge these connections, the relationship between them
is complex. Nihilism encompasses both positive and negative aspects: it
dismantled the foundations of mortality, knowledge, and significance,

**Embedded images:**

_Page render: `page_renders/page-0095.png`_


## PDF page 96
90                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

leaving individuals feeling intellectually, ethically, and spiritually adrift,
however, it also presented an opportunity, even a challenge, to rebuild
these foundations and redefine morals, knowledge, and meaning.10 Thus,
if humans have eradicated the concept of God, they must assume a god­
like role, thereby embracing the creative task of establishing their own
foundation for knowledge, morality, and purposeful existence.11
     Art Meets Science: Art Forms in Nature in Art Nouveau
Darwin’s theory of evolution was revolutionary and potentially impacted
the art world, yet not everyone grasped its significance in science and
religion. Nevertheless, it allowed for the exploration of diverse possibili­
ties in human existence. Af Klint, for example, glossed over intricate and
unsettling aspects of Darwinian theory. In 1908, she embarked on a
series depicting evolution, incorporating the word “Evolutionen” in sev­
eral pieces, such as Evolution, No. 4 (Figure 8). This artwork portrays a
snail with progressively larger segments, symbolizing evolution as a
methodical progression of superior forms where a larger size indicates
superiority. It culminates in a female human figure bathed in bright light
on the right, contrasted with a male figure in darkness on the left. This
spiral-shaped  snail embodies Darwin’s  initial  belief  that evolution
resulted from slight, successive, advantageous variations; a concept that
later proved inaccurate. Darwin himself did not fully comprehend the
mechanism of mutation, referred to as variation.12 Snails being her­
maphrodites, reproduced by fertilizing each other. In her depiction, af
Klint  presents copulating  snails, highlighting an  artificial  division
between male and female, contrasting with their hermaphroditic nature.
Both snails should be sexless and of the same color; however, af Klint
unites them in copulation, blending the colors associated with male (yel­
low) and female (blue) with the spiraling growth.13
    The scholarly journals and books that Darwin kept in his library
and read, contained several illustrations. The number of images in his
books on evolution grew over time from volume to volume with just
one image in Origin of Species in 1859 to seventy-eight pictures in his
publication of Descent of Man (1871). His visual collection continued to


## PDF page 97
Analysis of the Tree of Knowledge and the Atom Series            91

grow over his lifetime.14 Darwin created his illustrations using basic tools
such as pencil, paper, and the unaided eye. Typically, they were replicated
through woodcutting, a prevalent method during the nineteenth cen­
tury. However, in his book, Darwin utilized advancements in technology





                  Figure 9. Ernest Haeckel, Discomedusae.—Schweibenquallen from
                                    Art Forms in Nature (1904).

## PDF page 98
92                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

like the printing press and the heliotype process.15 In a classical sense,
Darwin was a virtuoso artist; his geological illustrations, cross-sectional
diagrams, sketches of embryos, and the development of organisms cre­
ated a new systematic way of studying nature that helped conceptualize
his theory of evolution.16
     In May 1860, six months after the first publication of Darwin’s On
the Origin of Species, a young biologist named Ernst Haeckel (1834-1919)
in Berlin read this work in its German translation.17 Haeckel studied and
translated Darwin’s theory of evolution into beautiful forms and color,
publishing his Art Forms in Nature series between 1899 and 1904.18 His
detailed biological illustrations are a visual encyclopedia for his ground­
breaking research and anatomical discoveries that were highly influential
and helped in propagating natural history studies. Using halftone prints
and lithographic prints for his publications, his work was revolutionary in
uniting scientific purpose and aesthetic beauty.19 He elevated our marine
predecessors with ornate elegance. Jellyfish adorned themselves with
extravagant skirts and veils that gracefully undulated through the water;
single-celled radiolarians resembled fairy-tale citadels with myriad towers
and coves (an example as seen in Figure 9). Those concerned that Darwin’s
theories might diminish humanity’s dignity and rob it of its illustrious ori­
gins could find reassurance to the contrary in the exquisite beauty depicted
in Haeckel’s illustrations.20
    Hilma af Klint would have been familiar with Haeckel’s work as she
encountered biological literature at the Veterinary Institute. Additionally,
Theosophists were interested in Haeckel’s work: though some saw the
work as useful, others did not. Blavatsky states in The Secret Doctrine, “No
occultist would deny that man—no less than the elephant and the microbe,
the crocodile—is, in his physical formation, the simple product of the evo­
lutionary forces of nature.”21 What he was missing, Blavatsky believed, was
the idea of the soul and its evolution; as a scientist, Haeckel told the first
part of the story, however, the homo sapien (wise human) evolution, was the
responsibility inherent in occult research.22
    Although Haeckel was deeply fascinated with the scientific prin­
ciples of evolution and nature from an early age, he began his academic


## PDF page 99
Analysis of the Tree of Knowledge and the Atom Series            93

career studying medicine, earning his doctorate in 1857. Unfulfilled work­
ing as a physician, he soon returned to biology earning his habilitation
in comparative anatomy in 1861— the highest university degree available
in most European countries. He then traveled to Italy, seeking a project
that engaged his interest in scientific research as well as his passion for
art. Becoming engrossed in the art and culture and making friends with
artists, he desired to dedicate himself fully to creative practices, however,
with no support from his father, he was prevented from pursuing a full-
time career as an artist. While in the Italian city of Messina, he studied
organisms under a microscope and became fascinated by the beauty of
the sea creatures’ geometric bodies. With dedicated precision, he created
intrinsic details of their bodies and skeletons, allowing him to combine his
two passions.23
    The innovative designs in Art Forms in Nature-inspired many artists
of the time. During the early twentieth century, the Art Nouveau move­
ment flourished, drawing inspiration from the untamed essence of nature.
This ornamental style embraced the beauty inherent in natural forms,
incorporating organic, flowing shapes reminiscent of flora and fauna.
This aesthetic style found widespread adaptation across various forms of
applied arts. In Germany, it was termed Gesamtkunstwerk, meaning com­
plete or indicating an all-encompassing work of art.24
    The influential German art dealer and promoter of Expressionism,
Karl Nierendorf (1889-1947) later wrote of Art Nouveau:
     The fickle delicacy of a Rococo ornament, as well as the heroic
       severity of a Renaissance chandelier, the mystically entan­
      gled tendrils of the Gothic flamboyant style, noble shafts of
      columns, cupolas, and towers of exotic architecture, gilded
      episcopal crosiers, wrought-iron railings, precious scepters,
        all these shapes and forms trace their original design to the
      plant world.25
Art Nouveau began in the 1880s and lasted until the First World War and
sought to escape from historical styles previously popular, prevailing artis­
tic traditions. The movement aimed to eliminate the conventional ranking


## PDF page 100
94                     Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

of the arts, which typically placed sculpture and painting above craft-
oriented decorative arts. Artists focused on modernizing design; drawing
inspiration from geometric and organic forms, and developing sophisti­
cated compositions that united natural, flowing forms, resembling blos­
soms and stems of plants. Other characteristics of Art Nouveau include





         Figure 10. Alphonse Mucha, Rêverie (1898) (detail). Color lithograph, 72.7 x 55.2 cm
                              Art Gallery of New South Wales
the dynamic movement of flowing lines, ornamental motifs, and the use
of symbolic images that brought intrigue into everyday life and beauty to
ordinary objects, enhancing people’s lives.26 The movement quickly became
popular as it spread in journals, magazines, posters, and advertising.
   A leader in this style, Czech painter, Alphonse Mucha (1860-1939)
was once viewed as “the greatest decorative artist in the world,” creat­
ing distinctive movie posters, stylized illustrations, decorative panels, and
advertisements as seen in the example of Rêverie (1898) (Figure 10).27
Interestingly, Mucha was drawn to spiritualism as he was involved in a
Theosophical circle in Paris and joined the Paris Lodge of Freemasons. He
believed three main attributes of humans must balance to achieve peace
and progress—Reason, Wisdom, and Love.28 There are many famous art­
ists of this style, including Gustav Klimt (1862-1918) as well as an early

## PDF page 101
Analysis of the Tree of Knowledge and the Atom Series            95

pioneer of the movement, Eugène Grasset (1845-1917) who developed
Plants and their Applications to Ornament (1897) as an Art Nouveau source­
book, that illustrate abstract stylization.
    Af Klint was developing into an artist that coincided with the peak
of Art Nouveau surely would have been exposed to the movement.29
The difference, however, between af Klint and male “pioneers,” is Hilma
embraced the connection of her botanical motifs with decoration and
abstraction while the men of abstraction predominately disendowed any
such connection.30
    Although Haeckel firmly believed that every living organism could
only be comprehended as a product of an all-encompassing evolutionary
process, af Klint was intrigued by the possibility that things could have
unfolded differently. Her artwork explores the futuristic potential for
transformation, a theme particularly evident in the abstract paintings she
started creating in 1906 for Drawings to the Temple. As an example, in
The Ten Largest, No.9, Old Age (Figure 11), set against a flesh-toned back­
drop, we witness a genesis unfolding across five stages, depicting growth
through cell division and separation, culminating in the emergence of two
figures resembling colossal Ferris wheels in motion.31 This group repre­
sents human development in four stages: childhood, youth, adulthood, and
old age. Af Klint’s botanical references perhaps inspired by Haeckel, con­
vey a message of humanity’s connection to the natural world as well as the
evolution of the soul. The groundbreaking works of her contemporaries
in science, psychology, philosophy, and the arts, suggest the spiritual had
never been so closely interlinked to that of nature and the self (example
in Figure 12). As af Klint stated, “The more we discover the wonders of
nature, the more we become aware of ourselves.”32
     In the forthcoming analysis, the focus shifts towards a detailed
examination of Hilma af Klint’s series, the Tree of Knowledge and Atom.
Building upon the contextual groundwork laid out thus far, this chapter
delves into the intricate symbolism, artistic techniques, and thematic ele­
ments present in these two series. Through this multidimensional explora­
tion, the aim is to explore deeper meanings embedded within af Klint’s
artworks, particularly about the themes of knowledge, spirituality, and the

between science, art, and spirituality.

At the age of fifty, in June 1913, af Klint began her Tree of Knowledge
series. Seven years prior, she had abandoned the academic painting style
she learned at the Royal Academy of Fine Arts in Stockholm. Instead,
she channeled her intuition, knowledge, and skill into the creation of
the extensive cycle of works, Drawings to the Temple of which the Tree of
Knowledge is a significant group.33
     In the series,34 comprising of eight works on paper: seven paintings
and one detail painting; forms dynamic movement as viewed together
(Figure 13). Small particles resembling sparks cascade downward like
fireworks, while a pair of birds gracefully ascend along the central axis
in sweeping arcs. Beams of light pierce through the darkened backdrop

like spotlights traversing the nocturnal expanse. Every element appears
to pulsate with life, vibrating and growing. In No. 5 (Figure 20), one of
the two shapes appears to unfurl, reminiscent of the canopy of a jelly­
fish. A sense of tranquility permeates the scene as the movement gradu­
ally diminishes and comes to a halt. In No. 7a (Figure 22), two radiant
forms hover within the confines of a darkened circle. A child is depicted
ascending along the central axis, emitting rays of light as it journeys
upward (Figure 23, detail of 22). Although the images do not resemble
traditional trees, they do hold a resemblance to Yggdrasil from Norse
Mythology which will be discussed in greater detail throughout this
chapter. As with many of af Klint’s paintings, the viewer is transported
into other worlds where we could be viewing something as small as bac­
teria or as large as the universe.35
    In April 1913, af Klint was invited to lecture at the Spiritistiska
Litteraturföreningen, meaning Spiritualist Literature Association, where
she spoke excitedly about the “elemental force” that she believed would
guide individuals, emphasizing the untapped potential within each
individual. Encouraging her audience to embrace spiritual wisdom,
she emphasized the rejuvenating power that could breathe life into
stagnant aspects of existence, metaphorically likening it to revitaliz­
ing these “withered branches” of the World Tree also known in Norse
Mythology as Yggdrasil36 (Figure 27).37 She urged individuals to fully
immerse themselves in this wisdom, extending from the depths of their
being to the tips of their fingers—the very tools she employed to wield
her paintbrush.38
     In May of the same year, after several of her friends visited Munsö,
where af Klint’s summer home was located, they uncovered a deeper
significance to the location. Contrary to historical records, which erro­
neously attributed the first preaching of Christianity to the island of
Björkö, they discovered that it had occurred on Munsö. This revela­
tion prompted the relocation of their creative endeavors to the island.
“The messages were received by Anna Cassel, Emilia Giertta, Gusten
Andersson, and Hilma. They were told that they were now part of a
tradition that included Saint Ansgar, the Apostle of the North, who was

notes onto the back of the sheets which were later transferred to a notebook
dedicated to her creative documentation. These notes primarily consist of
dialogues with voices that guided her artistic process, offering insights and
affirmations. Notably,

in her typescript, she highlighted the word brain, where Steiner speaks
on the “cosmic tree.” According to Steiner, the brain serves as the por­
tal in accessing the cosmic tree; by ascending into the ether body and
observing the spinal cord to the brain, one can see the body from the
inside out and through the top of the world tree.45 Voss observes:
    One then looks upwards and sees how the nerves, which
     go through all the organs, are collected together up there in
      the brain. That produces the feeling: “That is a tree of which

        all the members.” That in reality is not felt as being of the
     same small size as we are inside our skin. The roots stretch
       far out into the distances of space and the branches extend
     downwards.46
Steiner explains the tree as having “branches going downwards” and
“roots going upwards” and its leaves as the “leaves of the Veda book,
which put together, yield the Veda knowledge,” the holy teachings of
Hinduism.47 As per art historian, Julia Voss’s, assessment, “Steiner’s dis­
course stretches from India to Europe, from Hinduism to Christianity,
from the Book of Revelation to medicine, from the etheric body to the
spinal cord. His thought is a sediment formed of religion and science:
compressed, drained, and dense.”48
     Associate Professor and Director of the Copenhagen Centre for the
Study of Theosophy and Esotericism at the University of Copenhagen,
Denmark, Tom Rudbøg, argues that af Klint incorporates aspects of
Theosophical ideology into her work and is particularly evident in Tree
of Knowledge. According to Rudbøg, beyond the physical form, each
individual possesses an energy body, known as the etheric body, which
envelops and interpenetrates the physical form. At a deeper level, exists
the astral body, closely tied to emotions and feelings; during dreaming,
individuals traverse the astral realm within this body. Additionally, there
is the mental body, associated with the realm of thoughts of the mental
plane. This realm holds greater spiritual significance than the physical,
etheric, and astral planes, housing the soul’s essence within its higher

Analysis of the Tree of Knowledge and the Atom Series           109

domains. Although the spiritual realms extend beyond the mental, the
discussion can become rather intricate. Both individuals and entire cos­
moses possess seven bodies, each varying in spiritual intensity. Notably,
only the lowermost portion of af Klint’s Tree of Knowledge, No. 3 (Figure
17), represents the material realm; the remainder portrays the higher
planes of existence within the unseen universe, often depicted solely by
artists of clairvoyants.49
      It is important to pause for a moment and reflect on af Klint’s cre­
ative process. Notably, af Klint created Drawings to the Temple, between
1906 and 1915 with a four-year hiatus from 1908 to 1912. The whole
series comprises one-hundred and ninety-three paintings in various
groups, subgroups, and series. Between November 1906 and April 1908,
the first one-hundred-and-eleven paintings were produced. Famously,
one of the few things widely known about the artist was her descrip­
tion of where the shapes came to her spirituality and how she painted
the images, “The pictures were painted directly through me, without
any preliminary drawings and with great force. I had no idea what the
paintings were supposed to depict; nevertheless, I worked swiftly and
surely, without changing a single brushstroke.”50 As Voss points out, this
is only half-true, as she could not have served simply as a passive instru­
ment. Af Klint went into more detail about this process as collaboration,
“It was not that I should blindly obey the lords of the Mysteries, but
that I should imagine them always standing next to me.”51 After her
four-year pause, she executed the last eighty-two paintings from 1912 to
1915. During this time, she received information through her medium­
ship, however, she had more control in influencing the aesthetics than
she did previously. By this time, she had become adept at entering and
exiting heightened states of consciousness. Rather than being guided
by her hand, she found inspiration from the images and words she saw
within herself.52
    As previously stated, af Klint began Tree of Knowledge in 1913, then
paused to create the grimmest of her oeuvre, The Swan series, in response
to the First World War. The first two canvases show two swans in the
foreground; a white swan and a black swan, that appear to be fighting

Analysis of the Tree of Knowledge and the Atom Series           111

(Figures 14 and 15). Interestingly, in Tree of Knowledge, there are addi­
tional pairs of swans; a pair in the center of the top of “the tree,” and a
third pair that looks to be off in the distance, becoming abstracted (Figure
15). Above No. 1 (Figure 14), No. 2 (Figure 15), and No. 5 (Figure 20),
is a shape resembling a chalice, historically representing the Holy Grail,
with an infinity symbol ∞ inside. The swans go through a transforma­
tion that leads to unification. In Hinduism, the swan is regarded as a
divine messenger. Af Klint also incorporated two Christian messengers
who intervene in the process of transformation (Figure 21); Gabriel, the
bearer of divine wisdom, and Michael, the archangel who fights against
dark forces. Af Klint did not see any contradiction in Christian symbol­
ism alongside motifs from other religions: dogmatism and monotheism
were far from her.53
     In the first painting of the series, the image is reminiscent of the
story of creation from Genesis. The tree is a spiritual symbol represent­
ing harmony, fertility, and creation. Her images show a balance of polar­
ity in the roots and the crown.54 The fall of man, however, is absent. As
Voss eloquently states, “She turned the biblical Fall of Man on its head:
Man and Woman, Adam and Eve, are not thrown out of paradise by the
archangels. Instead, yellow and blue, male and female, united in green,
at the crown of the tree of knowledge (as seen in Figures 20 and 21).”55
Similarly to the traditional depictions of expulsion as seen in George
Thompson’s (1793-1839) image of the Garden of Eden, with the inscrip­
tion “And when the woman saw that the tree was good for food, and
that it was pleasant to the eyes, and a tree to be desired to make one
wise, she took of the fruit thereof, and did eat, and gave also unto her
husband with her, and he did eat” (Figure 24), there are appears to be
a snake (Figures 17 and 19). In af Klint’s depictions, however, it more
closely resembles the serpent eating its tail, also known as the ouroboros,
representing the infinite order of life, the cycles of nature, eternity, and
knowledge and wisdom; profoundly different from Christianity’s ser­
pent that symbolizes the devil, temptation, and deceit.56
    Through the lens of Swedenborg’s theology, af Klint may have also
created the map of the human spirit rather than a more obvious translation

of good and evil in the Garden of Eden from the reference to the Tree of
Knowledge.57 Including Swedenborg’s perspective from True Christianity
(1771) makes the work even more multifaceted, “In the word the Garden
of Eden does not mean some garden but a state of understanding, and a
tree does not mean some tree but a human being”.58 The Tree of Knowledge
also has a clear upper and lower circle in the first six images, creating two
distinct environments. Swedenborg also says, “The inner levels of our
minds are formed to be receptive to all things of heaven and the outer
levels are formed to be receptive to all things of this world.”59 Furthermore,
through a Swedenborgian perspective, the roots and the crown of the tree,
with overlapping spheres, connected by the trunk, convey a sense how
one’s spirit exists in both heaven and earth. The crown of the tree is the
spiritual self; embedded in the lower sphere, the trunk and roots, represent
our earthly self. These two parts of ourselves, as Swedenborg describes our
spiritual essence intricately intertwine without earthly existence, imbuing
our mortal self with vitality and purpose.60
     Interestingly, af Klint’s Tree of Knowledge No. 6 and No. 7 (Figures
21 and 22) has a center spiral like that of The Dove No. 1 (Figure 25).

This is especially evident in the detail painting, 7b (Figure 23). The

      It is possible that af Klint uses the dove and the swan to explore the

overcoming dualities. However, there is a dual meaning of the dove as a
symbol for the Holy Spirit in Christianity or more broadly, a messenger
of the divine and an emblem of peace.63

114                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

    As seen in The Dove, the Tree of Knowledge, includes heart symbol­
ism, which we know today represents love and affection. This icon, how­
ever, has a long multi-layered history linked to religion and nature. The
sacred heart, for example, as seen in José de Páez’s (1727-1790) painting,
Sacred Heart of Jesus with Saint Ignatius of Loyola and Saint Louis Gonzaga
(c.1770) (Figure 26), represents both the human and divine love of Jesus
Christ, developed during the Counter-Reformation emphasizing faith
in the Christianity.64
    The Immaculate Heart of Mary was another reciprocal doctrine
developed by the church, symbolizing the love of humanity for their
Savior. This visual iconography, however, was thwarted by a new use of
the symbol, the geometric cardioid, meaning “heart-shaped”, thanks to
the invention of the printing press and the commercial manufacturing
of playing cards throughout Europe in the late fifteenth century. The
cardioid, commonly found in nature, appears in flowers and leaves of
various plants, fruits, and vegetables, and is also formed by doves when
unfolding their wings and by swans when they touch beaks.65
    The cardioid shape can be seen throughout Tree of Knowledge: in
No. 7a (Figure 22), two large cardioids, one upside down, on top of one
turned on its side; No. 1—No. 6 (Figures 14, 15, 17, 19, 20, 21), shows one
large upside down cardioid, forming the top of the tree; in No. 3 (Figure
17) and No. 4 (Figure 19), there is a heart near the top, center of the tree;
lotus flowers, representing spiritual enlightenment are also seen in these
two paintings within the series.
    According to Hindu belief, within the heart of every individual lies
a lotus flower, which, upon blooming signifies the attainment of enlight­
enment. In No. 1 (Figure 14), No. 2 (Figure 15), and No. 5 (Figure 20),
a heart appears at the center in the base of the tree, with lines moving
through them like a river, spiraling upwards to the crown of the tree.
According to Swedenborg’s theology, will and intellect are vessels of love
and wisdom that, as he writes, “are that entity that we call soul.”66 Lastly,
the four flowing loops at the base of the trees, similar to the patterns of
fours in the Atom series, closely resemble two perpendicular infinity sym­
bols, each pointed in a different cardinal direction, possibly symbolizing

             ATOM SERIES
In January 1917, Hilma af Klint turned to an exploration of the invis­
ible realm of atoms and their energies, an area beyond human perception,
resulting in a new series of twenty-two watercolor paintings titled, Atom.
Aside from the first two paintings, the rest of the series consists of two
images of an atom: one larger in the bottom, right corner, and one smaller
in the top, left corner, each divided into four cells. Most of the works also
include handwritten annotations, as understood by af Klint of atoms and
their properties.
    The lower, bottom image represents a physical atom and its energy,
while the image at the top represents atoms on an ethereal plane. The con­
figuration within the squares changes slightly with each painting, occur­
ring on the etheric plane and physical plane. The concept of an ethereal

plane is likely to be inspired by the Theosophical ideas of planes of exis­
tence, however, as will be discussed further in the chapter, “the ether” is a
term that popped up in the scientific community on the atom in the early
twentieth century.70
    As the Curies proved, atoms are divisible and through decay could
be split. As in Tree of Knowledge, the goal is to transcend the constraints of
the physical realm and access the ethereal. Directly inscribed on the fifth
painting of the series, af Klint writes, “The body is capable of rising above
its earthly ties by listening to the supernatural energies” (Figure 34)71.

     In her notebook, she further elaborates by adding, “I am an atom
in the universe that has access to infinite possibilities I want, gradually,
to reveal.”73 As with the Tree of Knowledge, macrocosm and microcosm
reflect one another, with each element possessing a counterpart; nothing
exists in isolation.74
   When af Klint began producing paintings of atoms and their ener­
gies, the term “atom,” originating from ancient Greek philosophy, still
possessed an air of innocence even towards the end of World War II

(1945), shortly after af Klint’s passing in 1944. It was during this time that
humanity achieved the release of energy concealed within atoms, result­
ing in a radiant energy, a thousand times brighter than the sun and thus
the destruction of life. The notion of “Innocence” as written by af Klint in
Atom No. 1 (Figure 30), is evident as she begins the series observing inno­
cence constituting the core of the universe. This concept is further devel­
oped in the subsequent drawing (Figure 31) with the inscription, “Every
atom has its center, but every center is directly connected to the center of
the universe.”75
    From the artist’s viewpoint, atoms symbolize a sort of foundational
element within the intricate fabric of existence, aspiring from a realm
beyond the material one “to create ever more beautiful forms” as af Klint
says in Atom, No. 4 (Figure 33). German philosopher, Immanuel Kant
(1724-1804) referred to this as “disinterested pleasure” in his Critique of
Judgment (1790).76 Here, af Klint perceived the energy encapsulated within
atoms as constructive—a concept likely to resonate with scientists striving
to better understand the inherent mechanisms through which the world
shapes itself, whether through genetic or physical forces.77

## PDF page 126
120                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

      It is widely acknowledged that the notion of the atom as the fun­
damental, indivisible unit, along with the concept of energy, traces its
origins back to early European philosophy. Aristotle introduced the term
“atom” from atomos, meaning “indivisible” or “uncuttable,” as a dynamic
entity, aiming to understand how individuals shape their perceived real­
ity from the myriad possibilities presented by the world, utilizing an
active force known as energy.78
     Despite the enduring influence of both “energy” and “atom,” and
the profound implications associated with these concepts, ancient Greek
philosophers tended to approach them more speculatively. They were not
accorded the same level of seriousness as modern scientists have since
the nineteenth century when explanations of the visible world began to
be framed in terms of invisible atoms. Simultaneously, this period wit­
nessed the emergence of descriptions of machine functionality in terms
of energy, an invisible force akin to atoms.79
    Following the confirmation of one of the postulates of the 1915
general theory of relativity by an eclipse expedition, “science” under­
went a significant transformation. It evolved into an abstract math­
ematical domain, where the fourth dimension was redefined as time
rather than space, within a four-dimensional “space-time continuum.”
     Concurrently, the concept of the ether, which had held a central
place in the cultural imagination for decades, was largely disregarded
as irrelevant physics. This shift, initiated in the 1920s by the domi­
nance of relativity theory over ether physics, had profound implica­
tions. It not only posed a challenge for artists and individuals rooted
in the previous paradigm but also presented a dilemma for subsequent
art historians and cultural historians on a broader scale. Instead of
Einstein, the backdrop for the emergence of modern art, including af
Klint, was the succession of scientific discoveries and developments
of the new century, previously discussed in this research including
but not limited to X-rays, wireless communication, and electromag­
netic waves. By the end of the nineteenth century, the impalpable
ether was attributed with numerous functions, occupying all space,
and penetrating all matter.80

    During this era, the notion that all matter could be radioactive
gained traction, presenting a novel perspective of objects continually
emitting particles into the surrounding ether. This concept was popular­
ized by Gustave Le Bon’s (1841-1931) bestselling book of the period,
L’Evolution de la Matiére (The Evolution of Matter) in 1905. At this time,
physicist, Sir Oliver Lodge (1851-1940), added upon mathematical Lord
Kelvin’s (1824-1907) theory of the “vortex atom,” and hypothesized the
“electric theory of matter,” in which matter is thought to be born through
interaction of the ether and the electron. Lodge was best known to the
public before World War I as he wrote comprehensively in popular jour­
nals and because of his openness to psychical research and spiritualism;
he was followed closely by both Theosophists and spiritualists.81
    As scientific knowledge increases, the thought about things in
the  invisible  world  begins  undergoing  considerable  modification.
Unsurprisingly, before painting the Atom series, af Klint produced the
Parsifal series in 1916, a part of this series, af Klint entitled one of the
parts, Ether Convolute, in which convolute suggests the creation of mat­
ter from amorphous fields of color. After this, af Klint began working
on her Atom series, exploring the juxtaposition of images on the physical
plane and etheric plane with added annotations such as, “The atom pos­
sesses both limitation and the ability to change. When the atom expands
on the etheric plane, the psychical part of the earthly atom begins to
glow,” as is transcribed on No. 7 (Figure 36).
    Af Klint’s ideas on the ether plane suggest that she was not only
referencing physics but also Theosophy, where the etheric was considered
the space between the physical, astral, and mental planes. Blavatsky dis­
cussed ether as well as its long history in both Iris Unveiled (1877) and a
book we know af Klint owned, The Secret Doctrine (1888).82
    The scientific journey toward understanding the atom originated
from the realm of chemistry. During the Romantic Era, chemists dem­
onstrated that these fundamental components were not just abstract
concepts but tangible entities capable of forming predictable molecu­
lar structures through what they termed elective affinities, a concept
also explored by Wolfgang von Goethe (1749-1832) in his novel, of

the same name, Wahlverwandtschaften (1809). By the early twenti­
eth century, physicists had developed methods to quantify atoms. In
1905, Albert Einstein’s (1879-1955) groundbreaking formula E=MC²,
revealed the equivalence of energy and mass, suggesting that the core
of the universe harbored immense energy. According to Einstein’s for­
mula, which denotes that energy (E) is equivalent to mass (M) times
the speed of light squared (C²), the innermost core of the world is pre­
sumed to contain a significant amount of energy. This concept, illus­
trated by the speed of light measured at about three hundred thousand
kilometers per second, is echoed in Hilma af Klint’s work, where she
speaks of the energy emanating from an atom, “When the atom is at
rest on the ether plane, energy is drawn into its centre and stored there.
When the body is harmonious, energy emanates from the centre of
atoms, radiating outward from them” (Figure 35).
     Physicists and chemists did not manage to detect this energy within
an atomic nucleus until 1938, approximately two decades after af Klint
created her atom drawings. Although physicists and chemists had not yet

## PDF page 132
126                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

detected this energy within atomic nuclei, physicist Lise Meitner (1878-
1968), an Austrian of Jewish faith, who was exiled in Sweden at the time
in 1938, grasped the concept’s significance just before World War II. Her
insight laid the groundwork for subsequent experiments in nuclear energy,
culminating in the politically driven Manhattan Project of the 1940s. The
connection between atoms and energy, particularly in the form of radi­
ation, had been anticipated earlier by H. G. Wells (1866-1946) during
World War I when he coined the term “atomic bomb.” This suggests that
af Klint’s depictions of atoms may have followed similar trajectories.83
     In relation to af Klint’s Atom series, it is crucial to examine the
scientific understanding of atoms that emerged during the twentieth
century, particularly in Copenhagen, which is geographically close to
Malmö. It was in Copenhagen where physicist Niels Bohr (1885-1962)
introduced the first sustainable models of the atom in 1913. Bohr’s
models conceptualized the atom’s “core” as its central feature, containing
most of its mass, while electrons orbited this core like planets orbiting
the sun. However, by the onset of World War I, Bohr’s model began to
face scrutiny, as it became apparent that it couldn’t fully account for the
behavior of electrons within the atom. Despite Bohr’s attempts to refine
his model with increasingly intricate electron orbits, the fundamental
concept of electrons circling a core with an electrical field proved unten­
able (Figure 38).84
     Bohr’s model of the atom, introduced in the early twentieth cen­
tury, transformed our comprehension of atomic structure through the
notion of quantized electron orbits. However, as physicists probed deeper
into subatomic behavior, challenges emerged. In 1925, Wolfgang Pauli’s
(1900-1958) Exclusion Principle refined Bohr’s model by asserting that
no two electrons within an atom could occupy identical quantum states
simultaneously. This principle addressed inconsistencies within atomic
orbitals, enhancing our understanding of atomic structure.
     Concurrently, Werner Heisenberg’s (1901-1976) 1927 Uncertainty
Principle further reshaped atomic physics by establishing fundamen­
tal limits on our ability to precisely measure both particle position and
momentum. These contributions prompted a reevaluation of Bohr’s

Analysis of the Tree of Knowledge and the Atom Series           129

model, emphasizing the probabilistic nature of quantum mechanics and
challenging  classical determinism. Together, Pauli and Heisenberg’s
insights refined and expanded Bohr’s foundational model, culminating
in the development of modern quantum theory.85
     Furthermore, Heisenberg’s Uncertainty Principle fundamentally
altered our understanding of atomic behavior and ushered in the era of
quantum mechanics, where particles were no longer bound by classical
notions of determinism. This shift introduced the notion of probability
waves and the existence of multiple potential states for particles, sug­
gesting the need to consider dimensions beyond the three spatial dimen­
sions familiar in classical physics.
    The Uncertainty Principle implied that particles existed in a realm
where traditional concepts of position and momentum became probabi­
listic, hinting at the existence of additional dimensions beyond our con­
ventional perception. Thus, Heisenberg’s work not only revolutionized
atomic physics but also opened new avenues for exploring the nature of
reality, including the possibility of higher-dimensional spaces.86
     In addition to his contributions to physics, Pauli delved into mysti­
cism and Carl Jung’s psychological theories, particularly those involv­
ing archetypes.87 He observed that as physics transitioned from classical
fields to the quantum realm, Jung’s concept of the shift from the number
three to the number four resonated; three represented time-dominated
consciousness, and four represented eternity and contemplation. Hence,
Jung believed that humanity’s collective consciousness  is devoted to
evolving from three to four.88 As af Klint says in No. 17, “The atom has
found the fourth characteristic that takes mankind onward. Burning
desire for action and stubborn persistence” (Figure 47).
     This transition, comparable to the move from classic physics to
atomic theory with its quantum leaps, introduced a holistic dimension.
Much like the interconnected web depicted by af Klint, where each atom
possesses a center linked to the universe’s center, the understanding of
atoms transcends mere mathematical theories. The shift from three to
four, symbolized by af Klint’s depiction of square atoms instead of the
traditional circular representations, may offer insights into mystical

## PDF page 138
132                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

and philosophical realms, including the significance of the sacred tet­
rad. Pythagoras’s tetraktys, play a significant part in the Atom series. For
example, in No. 9-No. 20 (Figures 39-50), af Klint encases her atomic
squares within circles, creating a mandala-like configuration, emblem­
atic of a state devoid of conflict, physicists achieved this harmony within
their atomic theory following Pauli’s introduction of a fourth quantum
number known as spin. This spin, resembling a twirling motion in space-
time, enabled electrons to maintain their spatial separation, facilitat­
ing the expansion of matter despite the vast empty spaces surrounding
atomic nuclei. This miraculous spin imparts internal energy to atoms,
enabling them to expand and maintain their form without dispersing
outward, preserving their innocence, as noted by af Klint (Figure 50).89
     In Annie Besant (1847-1933) and  Charles Weber  (C. W.)
Leadbeater’s (1854-1934)  Occult Chemistry, originally published  in
1908, uses Edwin Babbitt’s (1828-1905) 1878 illustration of the atom
(Chapter Three, Figure 6), to describe its shape and energy:
       It will be seen that the atom is a sphere, slightly flattened,
     and there is a depression at the point where the force flows
       in, causing a heart-like form. Each atom is surrounded by a
       field, formed of the atoms of the four higher planes, which
     surround and interpenetrate it. The atom can scarcely be
      said to be a ‘thing’ though it is the material out of which all
      things physical are composed. It is formed by the flow of the
       life-force and vanishes with its ebb. When this force arises
      in ‘space’— the apparent void which must be filled with
      the substance of some kind, of concealable tenuity—atoms
      appear; if this be artificially stopped for a single atom, the
     atom disappears; there is nothing left. Presumably, were that
      flow checked but for an instant, the whole physical world
     would vanish, as a cloud melts away in the empyrean. It is
      only the persistence of that flow which maintains the physi­
       cal basis of the universe.90

## PDF page 140
134                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

The fourth dimension was a concept where science, mathematics, and
Theosophy intersect. To be noted, this description not only refers to
the Atom series but also the Tree of Knowledge, as previously discussed,
depicting heart-shaped forms and a spiral that moves through the cen­
ter, giving it life (see Figure 22 as an example). The fourth dimension
became a significant component in the discussions of both Leadbeater
and Steiner, using it to explore the astral plane and astral vision, and
Steiner employing it extensively in his lectures to introduce the idea of
higher consciousness.91
    The idea of a transcendent fourth dimension of space grew in the
mid-nineteenth century from the development of n-dimensional geom­
etries or hyperspace philosophy; whereas three-dimensional is how we see
ordinary geometry in our Euclidean plane, n-dimensional shape is gen­
erated by projecting the shape of dimension, n minus 1 which is essen­
tially hyperspace.92 If space did possess additional dimensions, that would
make our familiar three-dimensional world a shadow of higher, indis­
cernible reality—a concept supported by Plato’s “Allegory of the Cave.”93
    American writer, architect, and active member of the Theosophical
Society, Claude Bragdon (1866-1946), illustrated plates from his book,
Primer of Higher Space: The Fourth Dimension (1913) further clarify the
relationship of higher dimensions and how they can be understood as
lower-dimensional folding (Figures 54 and 55). Today, the concept of
additional spatial dimensions is in the realm of physics, prominently in
string theory. Philosopher, Immanuel Kant (1724-1804), suggested that
space and time are not objective, self-subsisting realities; rather, they
are subjective necessities of our human sensory-cognitive faculties, to
which all things must adhere; they are transcendentally ideal and mere
“forms of intuition” that depend upon the “subjective constitution of the
mind.”94 Concerning four-dimensional space, Leadbeater highlighted
its capacity to elucidate not only the phenomenon of seeing-through in
etheric vision but also the elevated level of understanding linked with
the astral plane. In astral vision, objects would be perceived simultane­
ously from all angles and “every particle of the inside—not through the
other, but all flattened out.”95

## PDF page 144
138                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

    Bragdon also believed in the fundamental idea that all forms are
inevitably involved in a systematic process of dynamic shifting in which
all visual elements of point, line, surface, and cube evolve from a singu­
lar dimension towards two-dimension, then three, four and then multi-
dimensional space; meaning the line is a result of a moving point, a surface
from a moving line, a cube from a moving surface until finally the tetra-
hypercube evolves from cubes moving within a four-dimensional space.
Examples of hypercubes can be seen in af Klint’s The Swan series, No. 8.,
No. 9., and No. 23 (Figures 51-53). Bragdon also had visions of the “genesis
of form and space” as an “evolutionary process” and even illustrated images
of the helix. This is another motif as seen in Tree of Knowledge and The
Dove. Lastly, Bragdon theorized that three-dimensional space gives a “lack
of perspective power,” in which one cannot communicate an appropri­
ate or accurate perception of an object, again alluding to planes and their
effect on true perception.96
     Astral planes and the astral world were predominant concepts for af
Klint as she refers to them consistently in her writings and notes. In her
series, Parsifal (1916), includes four sub-series: Physical Plane Convolute,
Astral Plane Convolute, Ether Plane Convolute, and Mental Plane Convolute.
Along with the Atom series, her added notations to the monochromatic
squares suggest Leadbeater’s multidimensional idea of astral vision. The
concept of these higher dimensions gave Theosophists such as Leadbeater
and af Klint a way to better understand interconnections of multiple planes
of existence—the physical plane with its ether aspect, mental and astral are
not all separate from each other but rather, interpenetrating.97
   Two of af Klint’s paintings from the Tree of Knowledge, No. 3 and
No. 4 (Figures 17 and 19), represent higher planes of existence: in her
second version of the paintings, she labeled the horizontal levels as
“ether plane”, “astral plane”, and “mental plane”. Leadbetter explains in
An Outline of Theosophy (1902):
    To these inner worlds of different levels of nature, we
      usually give the name of planes. We speak of the visible
     world as “the physical plane”, through under that name we


## PDF page 145
Analysis of the Tree of Knowledge and the Atom Series           139

      include also the gases and various grades of ether. To the
      next stage of materiality, the name “the astral plane” was
      given by the medieval alchemists … and we have adopted
      their title. Within this exists yet another world of still finer
      matter, of which we speak as “the mental plane”, because
        its matter is composed of what is commonly called “the
     mind in man.”98
Furthermore, Leadbeater’s Man Visible and Invisible (1902), included a
diagram called Planes of Nature (Figure 56), that represents the various
“realms of nature” and their associated colors. He discusses that the
different realms of nature are not separated but rather exist simultane­
ously existing all around us at once.
    In Tree of Knowledge and Atom, clear sequential changes are occur­
ring, however, thinking of something as multidimensional is difficult
to perceive when viewing them on a flat two-dimensional surface.99
About these various co-existing planes and multi-dimensional spaces,
Leadbeater says, “It is universally recognized that ether penetrates all
known substances, the densest solid as well as the most rarefied gas;
and just as it moves with perfect freedom between particles of the
denser matter, so does astral matter interpenetrate it, in turn, and move
with perfect freedom among its particles.”100
    Upon deeper observation, it must be noted that the very small,
subtle changes occurring in the Atom series, create movement like that
of a flip book. When played sequentially, af Klint’s atoms display a type
of astral vision: moving up and down, inward and outward like that
expansion of growing molecules. Much like the Tree of Knowledge, these
changes are related to planes of physical and etheric. Af Klint’s goal
in both series is to transcend the constraints of the physical form to
access the etheric realm.101 (see the animation developed solely for the pur­
pose of this research and book at:: https://www.rachelvillari.com/research)
    During the period 1917-1918, she prolifically wrote comprehen­
sive work spanning two-thousand and fifty-eight pages, titled Studies
of the Life of the Soul. Within this work, she examined the theme of

human division into genders and advocated for transcending this dual­
ity on a spiritual plane. She introduced concepts like “womanman” and
“manwoman”, challenging the superficial distinctions between sexes.
Female contemporaries were viewed as “mannish” if they claimed free­
doms that were thought at that time as the privilege of men.
    Although she did not dwell on society’s narrow-mindedness, she
continued to explore this duality along with multidimensional spaces
throughout many works as early as Primordial Chaos No. 8 (1906)
(Figure 57) as she illustrated symbolism of both man and woman
emerging from a snail shell; a hermaphroditic animal that unifies both
genders. This can also be seen in Tree of Knowledge as well as Atom; af
Klint unapologetically referred to her work as the “Fifth Gospel.”102
    In the annals of art history, the narratives of female artists like
Hilma af Klint and their contri­
butions to the abstract art move­
ment have often been overshad­
owed or marginalized. The societal
constraints and gender  biases  of
their time relegated them to the
peripheries, where their innovative
visions and radical expressions were
frequently dismissed or misunder­
stood. Despite facing obstacles and
enduring  societal  judgments,  af
Klint persisted in her exploration of
multidimensional spaces and spiri­
tual symbolism, leaving behind a
legacy that challenges conventional         Figure 57. Hilma af Klint, Primordial                                                         Chaos No. 8 (1906). Oil on canvas,notions of art and spirituality.                         53 x 37 cm
    Her pioneering spirit, coupled     Courtesy of The Hilma af Klint Foundation
with her unyielding commitment to her artistic vision, serves as a tes­
tament to the resilience and creativity of women in the face of adver­
sity. As we reflect on the rich tapestry of abstract art history, it becomes
imperative to reevaluate and acknowledge the significant contributions
