        Contemporary Abstract Elite:
       Upheld by the Art Establishment
                        Introduction
    ime and time again, people hear the constructed art history narra­
      tive, “the pioneer of abstract art, Russian Wassily Kandinsky …”, asTis traditionally taught, researched, and discussed within the art world.1
Many art historians across the course of decades, have dedicated a great
deal of research to better understanding the life and work of Kandinsky
(1866-1944) while holding him in high regard and praising his artistic
genius as completely new and unique compared to any other art ever
made over human history. Although the complete oeuvre of Hilma af
Klint was not discovered until the 1980s, the progress of acknowledg­
ing her work as serious fine art has progressed exceedingly slowly. Well-
intentioned individuals call af Klint a “contemporary” of abstract art­
ists such as František Kupka (1871-1957), Piet Mondrian (1872-1944),
and Kandinsky (1866-1944), however, she did not work alongside them.
This research uses the term “contemporary”, purely to describe the same
period, however, these artists were not peers of af Klint: she was not a
part of the art scene, she did not proclaim herself an artistic genius and
she did not promote her work to elevate her status as a creator.
    The work and life of af Klint are primarily incorporated into the pre-
existing art-historical context, compared and studied to other protago­
nists such as Kandinsky even though af Klint did not intend to partake
in the extroverted social movement of modernists that were inspired by
Theosophical and spiritualistic currents of the time and often bypassed by

                           149


## PDF page 156
150                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


historians.2 Af Klint was a trained, educated artist, primarily recognized as
a figurative artist, her later abstracted works were largely kept in secret per
her specific instructions. Much of her work was shown to very few, care­
fully selected, people; a stark contrast to the socially oriented early male
modernists, who favored breadth in their approach. This fact in addition
to her absence of social assertiveness, has only added to the debate about
“if she was an artist at all”, in doing so it prioritizes the idea that a piece of
art is only considered legitimate under specific social circumstances: being
aligned with a particular movement, having artworks showcased to the
public through galleries, reviews, museums, and exhibitions, thus being
integrated into an established art historical canon.3
    According to this ontological social definition, the extensive exhi­
bition of a significant portion of her paintings to diverse audiences,
along with critical reviews in prominent newspapers and magazines, and
acknowledgment by scholars and critics as an artist, establishes her as an
internationally acclaimed artist.4 Professor in the Theory and History of
Ideas of Visual Art at Malmö Art Academy, Gertrud Sandvist advocates
for regarding Hilma af Klint as a contemporary artist, stating:
     Almost all the abstract pioneers moved in Theosophical cir­
       cles similar to the one to which Hilma af Klint belonged. The
      ideas are reminiscent of each other. Af Klint, too, describes
      auras and astral planes, and talks about spiritual evolution, in
    common with Helena Blavatsky, Annie Besant, and Charles
      Leadbetter. But she never allowed her artistic work to ossify
      into a dogma. When she describes evolution, she does not
     mean some “development” in  art, but a labor of cosmic
      renewal … When Kandinsky became fascinated by Besant-
      Leadbeater’s findings about thought-forms, it was because
     he was intrigued by an expansion of what we call reality, he
     expanded his painting and pushed the boundaries of what and
    how artists can paint. This was a primary concern for him and
      other abstract pioneers … Abstract pioneers show what this
     new language of form looks like and how it is to be applied.5


## PDF page 157
Contemporary Abstract Elite: Upheld by the Art Establishment         151


Other historians such as Simon Morley attempt to bring acknowledg­
ment to af Klint, highlighting that she was a woman in a field where
most spirit mediums were also women, in a marginalized social group
leading the quest for trans-rational and spiritual alternatives to domi­
nant materialism. He points out that up to the 1980s, she remained “a
very marginal, even cranky figure” and a largely obscure figure in art
circles, however, is recognized as one of the foremost pioneers of abstrac­
tion.6 Even though it is refreshing to see af Klint included in art history
texts, she is often labeled as making nothing more than mediumistic
works of art. As Morley later writes:
       It should be noted that when Kandinsky used the German
     word geistige or ‘spiritual’, he did not mean it in the same
      sense as af Klint, who was referring to an existence beyond
      the grave. Rather Kandinsky’s goal was a refined, internal
        ‘reality’ lying beyond mere appearances and ‘impressions’.7
This limited and common perspective fails to offer a thorough com­
prehension of what af Klint was exploring through her artwork. How
art is defined and the explanation of “what an artist is” has long been
a source of controversy; definitions have varied over time, with dif­
ferent interpretations prevailing in various eras. For example, in the
twelfth century, an artist was a craftsman who developed skill from
knowledge and practice while later, in the Enlightenment, this was
an individual who created something beautiful, dominated by classi­
cism. Avant-garde, on the other hand, redefined art, and artistic pow­
ers, where the artist becomes a visionary of not yet developed new
cultural ideas. For Modernists, the ideas aimed at revolution and social
change, and its representatives were proponents of different forms of
utopianism.8 Af Klint was aware her paintings did not quite belong to
any contemporary art movements but were rather for future audiences,
as she believed. Paradoxically, her work diverges from the established
canon that art history has constructed around the rise of abstract art in
the early twentieth century.9


## PDF page 158
152                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

                  Abstract with a Capital A
When artists and critics use the word abstract, they typically refer to the
process of abstraction inherent in painting, where complex visual elements
are simplified or extracted to create a coherent and personal artistic state­
ment on a flat surface. In this broad sense, abstraction involves prioritiz­
ing exclusion over inclusion, making all paintings inherently abstract.
Although the roots of twentieth-century abstract art stem from this gen­
eral concept, its implications evolved to encompass three main aspects:
firstly, the removal of historical influences from painting to achieve
purity and essentialism, making it more modern; secondly, a shift away
from narrative storytelling towards painting as an autonomous entity;
and thirdly, a complete rejection of traditional optical realism and rep­
resentational pictorial languages. This transformation redefined painting
as a means of expressing invisible thoughts, ideas, emotions, moods, and
spiritual realities. Often, an artist’s pursuit of abstraction combines these
various intentions, necessitating an iconoclastic approach to achieve the
latter two forms of abstraction.10
     In the early twentieth century, there were initial endeavors to inte­
grate traditional non-Western religious philosophical concepts with the
latest advancements in Western philosophy and science. Westerners rec­
ognized that the term enlightenment could carry two distinct cultural
connotations. On one hand, it denoted a mode of thinking that priori­
tized evidence-based reasoning, advocated for a scientific worldview, and
emphasized the pursuit of social and individual well-being and justice
however, from a global standpoint, another interpretation of enlighten­
ment emerged, one that diverged from its western counterpart. This per­
spective centered on the belief in humanity’s capacity to attain profound
intuitive insights into the entirety of reality, valuing mystical experiences
and the potential for spiritual awakening.11
    The alternative, non-western concept of enlightenment laid the
groundwork for an impulse towards abstraction aimed at visualizing an
invisible spiritual dimension. It held appeal to Westerners amidst the
systematic disenchantment and rationalization of society, where modern


## PDF page 159
Contemporary Abstract Elite: Upheld by the Art Establishment         153


scientific and technological advancements appeared to confine reality
solely to the visible world encompassing more than conventional painting
depicted was reinforced by scientific discoveries revealing the intricacies of
the microscopic and telescopic realms. For some, the limitations of the vis­
ible world illuminated by science suggested that art should address realms
beyond scientific exploration: the transcendent domains of spirituality, reli­
gious experiences, altered states of consciousness, and the supernatural.12
    The longstanding inquiry into identifying the “first” abstract artist
has long intrigued historians, again, with figures like Kupka, Malevich,
and Kandinsky often cited as the most prominent contenders. However,
the emergence of Hilma af Klint onto the global stage has added a new
dimension to this discussion. Despite her relatively recent recognition
through the traveling international exhibition, Hilma af Klint—a Pioneer
of Abstraction, questions persist regarding her precedence in the abstract
art movement. It is crucial to note that af Klint (nor any of the above
figures) was not the first to produce non-figurative canvases; artists have
been creating abstract images for centuries, albeit without recognizing
them as “works of art.” Before the twentieth century, the term “ami­
metic” may be more appropriate to describe such works, as they devi­
ate from the classical doctrine of mimesis, or imitation of nature. Since
the turn of the twentieth century, Abstract Art—with a capital A—has
evoked unsuitable connotations and generalizations about the object in
an image as well as the artist behind the work.13 This chapter explores the
incorporation of abstract art into art history narratives, acknowledging
the broad spectrum of movements existing under the abstract umbrella,
including Expressionism, Cubism, De Stijl, and others. Even though the
term “abstract” is used loosely in this study, it is defined contextually for
clarity and ease of comprehension.
     Since the late nineteenth century, various spiritualist teachings have
been reshaping religious perspectives worldwide, such as Theosophy,
which aimed to harmonize spiritual beliefs with scientific understand­
ing. Many celebrated abstract artists, including Kandinsky, Mondrian,
and Kupka, explored Theosophy, yet none openly claimed that their
artworks were expressions of anything beyond  their consciousness.


## PDF page 160
154                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


Recognizing that her creations and their underlying motivations were
ahead of their time, af Klint made a significant decision in 1932: she
stipulated that none of her paintings or drawings should be exhibited
until twenty years after her death, as previously discussed.14 In doing so,
she propelled her life’s work into the future, beyond the first half of the
twentieth century and into the second, shielding it from the contempo­
rary critique of her peers. It is remarkably fortunate that af Klint’s work
has been almost entirely preserved, despite storing it away for decades.
Artists who achieve only modest recognition during their lifetimes often
fade into obscurity after death, making their traces exceedingly difficult
to recover. Af Klint took proactive measures to prevent this fate, but her
efforts would have been futile without the diligent preservation of her
nephew Erik af Klint (1901-1981). The preservation efforts began with
the artist herself, who acted as an archivist and editor of her writings.
Prioritizing her work over her personal life, Hilma allowed many details
of her private life to vanish.15
    The scarcity of information regarding her life presents a signifi­
cant obstacle to the recognition of her work within the framework of
art history. Additionally, af Klint is up against a multitude of stereotypes
and prejudiced historical assumptions about women. She is not the sole
female artist to have disregarded the notion of documenting her own
life. Even today, many argue that it is the artwork itself that holds signifi­
cance, and the formalistic approaches of art shivery tend to reinforce this
perspective. Consequently, there is a risk of undervaluing both the indi­
vidual and their work. For af Klint, prioritizing the space for her visions
and the accompanying voices, which she believed granted her access to
higher spiritual realms, took precedence over personal documentation.
As a result, her archive presents a somewhat impersonal exterior. The
prevailing notion that only the artwork holds significance, perpetuated
by formalist approaches in art history, often leads to the underestimation
of both the artist and their work, particularly if the reception during the
artist’s lifetime was minimal. Without contextual information, an art­
ist’s body of work may appear diminished, eventually being perceived
as narrow and inconsequential. The result is that af Klint’s early abstract


## PDF page 161
Contemporary Abstract Elite: Upheld by the Art Establishment         155


paintings are frequently regarded as fortuitous, accidental, and lacking in
planning. Conversely, the creations of her male counterparts are typically
attributed to exceptional talent and intellect. Af Klint has frequently been
dismissed as a “madwoman” by those who have not bothered to study her
work deeper. As Julia Voss states, artist, Rebecca H. Quarryman (born
1961) observed that this was the simplest method to discredit her; his­
torically deeming her artworks “valueless” in a literal sense as they were
not bought and sold on the art market.16
              From Mimesis to Fine Art
Various methods exist in categorizing and studying images. Raphael
Rosenburg, a distinguished professor of art history, dedicated fifteen
years to studying the diversity in material and form within abstracted
images. His research culminated in the  classification of mimetic
images into three distinct groups: accidental blotches, images associ­
ated with discussions on the aesthetics of effects, and depictions of
invisible things.17
    The initial category comprises images that appear to have emerged
by chance, or at least give that impression—blots perceived as intrigu­
ing, aesthetically pleasing, or emotionally expressive. Dating back to the
fourth millennium BC, from ancient Egypt to Neolithic Scandinavia,
artisans crafting vases or flint axes sought out stones with distinct pat­
terns once polished. Consequently, they selected exceptional minerals,
even if they were more challenging to work with. From very early on,
artisans managed to incorporate accidental patterns into the center of
the objects they were crafting. By around one hundred AD, the Romans
refined the technique of book-matching stone panels, resulting in ami­
metic, irregular yet symmetrical patterns with slightly unpredictable
variations. The enduring fascination with such decorations is attrib­
uted in part to the rarity of the materials, but more significantly to their
amimetic (abstract) aesthetic qualities. This is evidenced by the myriad
techniques employed since the second millennium BC, to replicate arti­
ficial marbling in glass, painting, ceramics, paper, stucco, and textiles by
diverse cultures worldwide.18


## PDF page 162
156                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge





              Figure 58. Arthur Wesley Dow, Four Variations of Irregular Spacing (1899)
    The second category of abstracted imagery, predating the nine­
teenth century, which we classify as amimetic, is intricately linked to
the aesthetics of effect. These aesthetics revolve around art theoretical
discussions concerning the impact of various elements such as lines,
colors, and composition. These discussions originated in the eighteenth
century and remain pivotal in both artistic education and contemporary
art historical analyses. For example, artists often use amimetic images for
instructional purposes in their written works. A prime illustration of this
phenomenon can be found in William Hogarth’s (1697-1764) Analysis
of Beauty, published in 1753, where he emphasizes the significance of
the serpentine “line of beauty” and includes several abstracted images in
the accompanying plates. Similarly, Mary Gartside’s (1755-1819) Essay
on Light and Shade, on Colours, and on Composition in General, published
in 1805, features eight amimetic watercolors “blots” intended to demon­
strate color arrangement for painting flower still lifes. Another instance
is Arthur Wesley Dow’s (1857-1922) Composition: A Series of Exercises in
Art Structure for the Use of Students and Teachers, first published in 1899
(Figure 58), which includes illustrations reminiscent of later works by
Mondrian (Figures 59 and 60). Despite Dow’s focus on figurative art,
the striking resemblance between his illustrations and Mondrian’s later
canvases is noteworthy.
    Mondrian was a part of the artistic collective known as De Stijl,
comprising of other figures such as Georges Vantongerloo (1886-1965)
and Theo van Doesburg (1883-1931), who exerted significant influ­
ence on the European avant-garde from its inception in 1917-1931.
Aligned with the Constructivist movement, De Stijl aimed to dissolve

barriers separating different art forms to integrate them seamlessly into
daily life. They advocated for a new form of art termed constructive or
concrete, emphasizing the intrinsic element and principles of paint­
ing rather than borrowing from external sources, as seen in Cubism.
Mondrian, in particular, developed his own painting system called by
historians Neoplasticism, as exemplified in works as seen in Figure
60, where traditional linear perspective and modeling were rejected in
favor of geometric shapes and colors. Mondrian viewed his art not
merely as an aesthetic pursuit but to transcend rational boundaries.
By blending influences from Cubism, esoteric philosophy, scientific
inquiry, and potential compositions from Dow, he sought to elevate art
beyond mere visual representation or emotional expression, aiming for
a higher absolute reality.19
     This research primarily examines the final category of abstracted
images from the pre-nineteenth century before their integration into the
realm of fine art. These images represent something invisible, making
them representational but not imitative of nature. An exemplary instance
is a black square crafted by Swiss engraver Matthäus Merian (1593-
1650) in 1617, featured in physician, mathematician, and Rosicrucian,
Robert Fludd’s (1574-1637) extensive work The Natural History of Both
Worlds, Macrocosm and Microcosm (Figure 61). Fludd’s comprehensive
text aims to systematically summarize all existing human knowledge,

## PDF page 164
158                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


accompanied by approximately a thousand images etched by Merian.
In contemplating the universe’s origins, Fludd explores whether it arose
from a primal substance (Greek hyle, Latin prima materia), as suggested
by biblical accounts. Drawing from conflicting textual evidence, Fludd
concludes that the primal substance is an existing, infinite, and formless
entity devoid of measurable qualities, rendering it imperceptible. Fludd
illustrates this concept as an image of formless material, described as
black as smoke, steam, or a dreadful shadow, or depicted as a rough,
disorganized mass.20
     Ukrainian artist, Kazimir Malevich (1879-1935), explored vari­
ous  styles—Impressionism,  Post-Impressionism,  Symbolism,  and
Cubo-Futurism—before pioneering his own style of Suprematism. In

Suprematist paintings, geometric shapes are arranged diagonally in clus­
ters of flat colors that dominate the composition against a typically white
backdrop. However, it was his iconic Black Square (1915) (Figure 62),
reminiscent of Fludd’s 1617 Hyle image (Figure 61), that epitomized
Malevich’s radical vision as the emptiest Western painting to date. The
starry minimal artwork represents a non-visual yet palpably real world
accessible only through intuition—a heightened state of consciousness
characterized by pure feeling. Malevich likened his paintings to icons

of Russian Orthodox Christianity, emphasizing their role in making the
ineffable tangible. As he remarked, his exposure to icon painting revealed
that the essence of art lies not in anatomical precision or perspective, but
in emotional connection, illustrating the truth of nature and the percep­
tion of artistic reality.21 Both Malevich and Fludd challenge conventional
perceptions of art by suggesting that its essence transcends mere visual
representation, serving as a conduit for accessing deeper, non-objective
realms of reality through intuition and emotional resonance.
    Although  the  visual  comparison  between  Fludd’s  Hyle and
Malevich’s Black Square is apparent, there is no evidence suggesting that
Malevich was aware of Fludd’s work, let alone influenced by it. This
distinction holds true for the examples of Mondrian and Dow as well.
However, the significance lies not in the creation of novel forms, but in
the assertion that amimetic images are indeed art and that contemporary
Art must be Abstract. Fludd and Malevich’s works may appear simi­
lar formally, but the crucial difference lies in their classification: Merian
etched an amimetic image, whereas Malevich created an Abstract work
of art. This disparity extends to the semiotic level, where the question
of “what does it mean?” elicits varied responses and modes of interpre­
tation. Although Merian’s Hyle has a specific, defined meaning within
Fludd’s text, Malevich’s Black Square, comparable to Umberto Eco’s con­
cept of an “open work,” encompasses a spectrum of meanings rather than
a singular interpretation.22
    Some historians use this rationale to discredit af Klint as the pio­
neering Abstract Artist (capital A’s). However, this argument is flawed:
while Kandinsky, historically lauded as “the father of abstraction,” was
immersed in Theosophy, influenced by Rudolf Steiner, Buddhism, the
Bhagavad Gita, C. W. Leadbeater’s Thought-Forms and Man Visible and
Invisible and Edwin D. Babbitt’s studies of the heart-shaped atom, as
too was Hilma af Klint. Yet, Kandinsky is celebrated as a visionary artist,
and af Klint’s contributions to Abstract Art are often minimized. The
critical disparity may lie in af Klint’s altruistic documentation of her
creative process compared to Kandinsky’s more self-focused narratives.


## PDF page 166
160                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


    Hilma af Klint left her mark on her era, despite acknowledging
that she couldn’t unveil her art to the public and steadfastly kept her
paintings hidden for years. By preserving her artwork and wisdom, and
believing that future generations would appreciate it, she demonstrated
vision, bravery, and conviction. She embodied confidence and courage,
fearlessly forging her own path and firmly standing by her convictions.
Remarkably, she navigated her journey without facing significant opposi­
tion or criticism during her lifetime. As an unwavering artist, she refused
to conform to contemporary trends or please her audience, instead focus­
ing on authentically expressing her inner vision.23 As a spiritual leader,
Anne Sophie Jørgensen (1954) observes:
     At a time when many assumed that women artists were
      incapable of creating anything original, that they could only
      reproduce what men had already done better, she created art
      that was original and innovative. Even a century later, her
       art has something new to contribute. In her notebooks, she
     wrote that she had to set aside her ego to let her paintings
      take whatever form they wanted … Truly relinquishing her
     need for recognition was necessary to deposit her life’s work
      in an uncertain future, with no guarantee that, years later,
     anyone would appreciate her paintings and the aesthetics
     and messages they hold.24

             Gendered Roles in Spiritual Art:
         Female Mediums vs. Male “Genius” Artists
The traditional position allowed to women in the art sphere was often
confined to roles as muses, partners, or occasionally, supportive benefac­
tors. Numerous institutional barriers hindered women from engaging
in professional competition with men within the art domain. Even if
female artists possessed talent and received similar education to their
male counterparts, they were not automatically included in established
male-dominated networks. During the late nineteenth and early twen­
tieth centuries, prevailing masculine norms confined women to the


## PDF page 167
Contemporary Abstract Elite: Upheld by the Art Establishment         161


domestic sphere, primarily as mothers and wives. If a woman pursued
art, it was often viewed as a hobby until marriage, rather than a serious
endeavor. This perception included the belief that women lacked cre­
ativity beyond childbirth and were incapable of original genius, merely
capable of replicating what men had already achieved. Consequently, it is
unsurprising that throughout her career, af Klint preferred to collaborate
with women and engage in artistic dialogue within female networks.25
    Between the barriers faced by women in the art world, is another
pioneering female figure who challenged conventional norms, Georgiana
Houghton (1814-1884). In 1871, amidst widespread protests challeng­
ing traditional authorities across Europe, a dual upheaval unfolded.
In France, the Paris Commune erupted, precipitating the overthrow

a female medium claiming guidance from supernatural entities. This dis­

    Houghton also participated in séances, a common practice at the
time as we know, through which she received messages. However, unlike
many others, she sought inspiration from within herself for her visions,
which she meticulously depicted in increasingly intricate watercolors.
Her focus was not on external appearances but rather on the phenomena
that emerged when she relinquished control of her consciousness, giving
form to an unseen inner world. Artistically, she drew from two primary
sources: an artist friend, identified only as Mr. L in her autobiography
Evenings at Home (1881), and secondly, spiritual entities.27 These spirits
not only guided her paintings but also offered advice on practical matters
such as social visits and wardrobe choices.28
    Mr. L proposed the idea of a public exhibition to Houghton, know­
ing that her submissions to the Royal Academy had been rejected twice.
He suggested that instead of facing potential rejection again, she should
consider organizing her own exhibition. This suggestion initially bewil­
dered Houghton as she perceived herself a timid woman incapable of
such an undertaking. However, Mr. L assured her of his full support,
pledging to assist in every aspect as a mentor, admirer, and friend. This is
when she began promoting her London exhibition in the “New British
Gallery”, which was a well-known part of the art scene.29
    She displayed a total of one hundred and fifty-one artworks,
none of them particularly large, with few exceeding thirty-two by

portraiture. Reviews criticized Houghton and dismissed her paintings as

emotions and intellect. While she associated each painting with a per­
son and initially dismissed them as allegorical, later she described them
as “real objects” from the spiritual realm, resisting definitive interpretation
and maintaining their enigmatic significance.31
     Overall, the exhibition encountered significant hostility and skepti­
cism, unable to effectively communicate Houghton’s visionary perspective
to an audience largely unreceptive to her abstract style. Despite her efforts
to guide viewers through the spiritual journey depicted in her paintings,
their true significance remained elusive. Critics dismissed them as lacking
authenticity and failed to recognize their innovative approach to abstract
representation. Additionally, Houghton’s interpretations of her work may
have added to the confusion, calling her work “Sacred Symbolism,” seem­
ingly contradicting her earlier stance. Sometimes, she provided explana­
tions for individual forms on the back of her pictures, for example calling
them “fruits” and “flowers” (Figure 64), only to reject any attempt at inter­
pretation later on. She ultimately declared her paintings to be a “spiritual
cipher,” suggesting a deeper, mysterious meaning beyond human language,
further adding to their enigmatic nature.32
    Houghton believed her artworks were unparalleled in the world,
standing alone without comparison among her contemporaries or pre­
decessors. Like the great artists who came before her, she possessed
the skill and determination to dedicate years of practice to achieve
artistic excellence. She emphasized that the ability to capture the invis­
ible realm required rigorous training, not just innate talent, or inspira­
tion, but also patience and hard work. According to Houghton, artistic
mastery cannot be achieved overnight; it requires gradual progress,
much like an acorn growing into a mighty oak over many seasons. She
recounted being present for some of the most memorable moments of


## PDF page 171
Contemporary Abstract Elite: Upheld by the Art Establishment         165


her exhibition, where visitors expressed joy at encountering the “new
beauty” revealed in her artworks.33
    During this period, many artists were intrigued by the idea of con­
necting with a broader reality or multiple realities beyond the visible realm.
They turned their focus away from tangible subjects. In a lecture in 1911,
the Futurist artist Umberto Boccioni (1882-1916) expressed this senti­
ment, stating, “It is not the visible that should be painted but that which
until now was regarded as invisible, namely, what the clairvoyant painter
sees,” as noted by Iris Müller-Westermann.34
    Hilma af Klint’s pioneering artworks emerged in the early twenti­
eth century, preceding the advent of abstract art in Russia and Europe.
Her creations transcend mere abstraction of color and form for aesthetic
purposes; instead, they aim to depict the invisible. Similarly, artists like
Kandinsky, Malevich, and Mondrian also explored spiritual dimensions.
Kandinsky departed from Expressionism, gradually abandoning visible
reality. He delved into the occult and published On the Spiritual in Art
in December 1911, stating in his book, “This spirit in painting stands
organically in direct relation to the new construction of the spiritual realm,
which has already begun, for this spirit is the soul of the epoch of great
spirituality.”35 Malevich’s journey led him to Suprematism, producing
abstract and highly spiritual imagery after experimenting with Cubism
and Futurism. Mondrian, influenced by Theosophy, eschewed figurative
representations, simplifying his compositions to basic vertical and hori­
zontal lines and primary colors. He sought to express eternal ideas beyond
the visible world through his art.36
     In December 1913, Kandinsky embarked on his inaugural ven­
ture into fully abstract painting with significant works like Light Picture
(Figure 66) and Black Lines (Figure 67), liberating art, as museums like
the Guggenheim say, from representational constraints by tapping into
his inner spiritual world. These professional successes were a testament to
the achievements he made in his quest towards abstraction.37 Much like
af Klint, Kandinsky “considered nonrepresentational abstraction to be
the natural manifestation of the spirit—and its opposite, representational
naturalism, to testify to a materialist view.”38 Kandinsky’s unwavering

    Although he faced some initial skepticism from the art world, his
abstract compositions were relatively quickly accepted compared to
the struggles faced by female artists such as af Klint and Houghton.
Despite his deep-rooted spiritual intentions and aesthetic convictions,
Kandinsky’s journey toward abstraction was met with different initial
reactions. He sought to capture the unseen and convey inner emotions
through his abstract compositions, but his artistic endeavors were often
misunderstood and met with skepticism. While Kandinsky quickly
gained recognition through his own self-promotion and the backing
of influential individuals like Rudolf Steiner and the Guggenheims, he
subsequently received ample support from the art establishment and is
celebrated in history. In contrast, female abstract artists continue to con­
front persistent challenges in securing their rightful place in the narra­
tive, shedding light on the privilege entrenched within the male-domi­
nated art historical discourse.40
    The connection between Steiner and Hilma af Klint is rooted in shared
ideas and contexts, as well as intersecting life paths. When Steiner visited
Sweden in 1910, af Klint welcomed him to her studio, but his response was
lukewarm, criticizing her “mediumistic” paintings.41 This reaction aligns
with Theosophical principles, which aimed to replace externally controlled
spiritual contacts with autonomous higher knowledge. While af Klint’s
evolving relationship with Steiner remains unclear, she visited Dornach in
1920 after her mother’s passing, where she encountered Steiner again. This
visit marked the beginning of a close association with Anthroposophy, as
evidenced by her prolonged stays in Dornach until 1930 and her decision
to bequeath part of her work to the Goetheanum in 1927.42
    However, af Klint’s affiliation with Anthroposophy was not without
complications and she left it by 1930. In 1922, she transitioned from her
abstract geometric style to one characterized by flowing colors, influenced
by Steiner’s doctrine advocating for translucent surfaces over graphic
lines in spiritual painting. Steiner’s opposition to abstract art and prefer­
ence for allegorical symbolism led him to critique geometric painting,


## PDF page 174
168                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


possibly prompting af Klint’s shift towards an “Anthroposophical style.”
Even though this adaptation may have brought her inner peace within
Anthroposophy, it also potentially constrained her creativity, as she navi­
gated the strict painting rules dictated by “higher insight.”43
   On July 25, 1928, an event organized by the Anthroposophical
Society in England marked a significant milestone. Despite the pro­
longed effort required to establish the society and promote Steiner’s
teachings in the region, the occasion was a testament to their perse­
verance. In a gesture of support for af Klint, the World Conference on
Spiritual Science announced the opening of an exhibition scheduled for
four in the afternoon. Alongside the exhibition, an address by the artist
was planned. Preferring a separate space from the larger group exhibition
organized by the Anthroposophists, af Klint was granted a room with a
capacity for fifty to a hundred people.44
    The conference commenced on a Sunday, and three days later,
Hilma af Klint inaugurated her exhibition. During her address to the
attendees, she elucidated the connections between her artworks and the
Rosicrucians45. Althoughthe exact paintings displayed remain unspeci­
fied, references to their “enormous size: 2-3 m” in correspondence regard­
ing the conference suggest they may have been selected from the series
Drawings to the Temple. Additionally, mentions of small works were also
noted in some letters. Presumably, af Klint’s lecture was translated in
real-time, yet the precise content of her speech remains undisclosed.
Undoubtedly, articulating the essence of her creations posed a consider­
able challenge, given that much of it was enigmatic even to the artist her­
self. Numerous innovative concepts were introduced at the conference
and met with great enthusiasm. However, Hilma’s artistic contributions
were once again overlooked, leaving her on the fringes of recognition.
    Both Georgiana Houghton and Hilma af Klint, alongside male
counterparts like Kandinsky, ventured into the realm of spirituality
through their art during a time when women faced significant obsta­
cles in the art world; these are only examples. Despite societal barriers,
they pursued their spiritual visions with conviction, creating artworks
that transcended conventional representations. Houghton and af Klint


## PDF page 175
Contemporary Abstract Elite: Upheld by the Art Establishment         169


challenged traditional artistic norms by exploring abstract and non-rep­
resentational forms to convey the unseen spiritual realm. Meanwhile,
male artists like Kandinsky often received more recognition and vali­
dation for their spiritual explorations, reflecting the gender disparities
prevalent in the art world.
   New York City: Temple Visions Amidst Degenerate Art
Hilma af Klint’s Drawings to the Temple series was intended for a lit­
eral temple that she envisioned and documented in her notebooks. Even
thoughshe held firm her belief in her work, she harbored doubts about
her contemporaries. Consequently, she took decisive action. A signifi­
cant entry dated “1932”, found on the initial page of one of her note­
books, reads “+x” in pencil, accompanied by an explanatory note, “All
works which are to be opened twenty years after my death bear the above
sign.”46 The “+x” was consistently added to her notebooks from 1905
onwards, including those containing Drawings to the Temple indicating
her desire for preservation. Enclosed within a square frame, the symbol
served as a distinctive marker, similar to a control button, designating
these works for preservation while signaling the disposal of others. This
notation functioned as a means for the artist to encapsulate her creations
within a temporal vault, destined for unveiling in the future.47
    As af Klint was conceptualizing her temple project, an unexpected
shift occurred in the art world across the Atlantic, thousands of miles
away. Among the prominent figures in this emerging movement was
Hilla von Rebay (1890-1967), an artist with a vision to establish a
museum in New York City, a concept deemed radical by many of her
contemporaries. However, von Rebay did not need to persuade others, as
she garnered significant support from Solomon R. Guggenheim (1861-
1949), born into the renowned Guggenheim family for their success in
the mining industry.48
    At the age of forty, Rebay had grand ambitions. In 1930, she corre­
sponded with the artist Rudolf Bauer (1889-1953), expressing her vision
for a museum that transcended traditional notions. She envisioned a
space designed with meticulous care, where artworks would be displayed


## PDF page 176
170                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


sparingly and rotated regularly to ensure each received equal attention.
Rebay described her aspiration as creating “a temple of non-represen­
tation and reverence,” preferring the term “temple” over “church” for its
connotations. Presumably, this would have resonated with af Klint’s own
ideals, indicative of their shared perspectives. Rebay’s characterization of
the museum as a temple reflected her belief in art’s capacity to transcend
mere representation and elevate viewers to a higher spiritual plane. Her
inspiration drew from the writings of figures familiar to af Klint, such
as Annie Besant, Helena P. Blavatsky, and Rudolf Steiner. Rebay’s early
exposure to Steiner’s lectures in her adolescence further shaped her phil­
osophical outlook, aligning with af Klint’s spiritual influences.49
     After immersing herself in esoterica studies like af Klint, Rebay
pursued formal training in classical art, attending private academies
in Paris and Munich. She championed a style she termed “non-objec­
tive” art, which she saw as heralding a revolutionary era like that of
the earth orbiting the sun. Documented in the first book published
by Solomon R. Guggenheim Collection of Non-Objective Paintings,
Rebay wrote, “For thousands of years astronomers, as well as laymen,
believed that the earth was the center of the universe, around which
all other planets evolved …. For an even longer period, there was
a belief that the object in waiting was the center around which art
must move. Artists of the twentieth century have discovered that the
object is just as far from being the center of art as the earth is from
being the focal point of the universe.”50
    Documenting the evolution of her museum concept can be traced
through photographs in the Guggenheim Museum archive. Early images
from 1930 capture Rebay’s visit to the Bauhaus, where she laughs alongside
Guggenheim and Kandinsky, celebrating their purchase of Kandinsky’s
Composition VIII (1923). The culmination of their vision came with the
commissioning of Frank Lloyd Wright (1867-1959) to design a permanent
museum. A 1945 photo captures Rebay with a model of the Guggenheim,
showcasing Wright’s innovative design with a spiraling ramp instead of
conventional stairs. This architectural choice symbolized Rebay’s belief
that nonobjective art could elevate consciousness, guiding visitors on a


## PDF page 177
Contemporary Abstract Elite: Upheld by the Art Establishment         171


metaphysical journey toward spiritual enlightenment. Reflecting on her
vision, Rebay expressed a lifelong desire for her museum to embody a spi­
ral form, eschewing stairs entirely.51
    Af Klint was unaware of the museum project, but her notebooks
outlined designs for a spiral temple housing nonrepresentational paint­
ings, conceived as a conduit to celestial realms. She envisioned the spiral
as a metaphor for spiritual growth, facilitating the viewer’s transcendence
from material concerns to high understanding. Describing her temple as
a repository of metaphysical truths, she expressed a desire for it to unveil
the mysteries beyond tangible reality In contrast to Rebay, af Klint hesi­
tated to pursue her plans, sensing the inopportune timing amidst politi­
cal unrest, particularly in Germany. With apprehension over looming
turmoil in Europe, she deferred her ambitions, recognizing the impend­
ing era of destruction. Aware of the tumultuous times ahead, af Klint
postponed her plans for her temple.52
    Not long after this in Germany, the era of tolerance for artistic ambi­
guity came to an abrupt end. The German campaign against “degenerate
art” triggered a seismic shift in both art creation and its historical trajec­
tory across New York and other U.S. cities. As German authorities seized
and confiscated thousands of artworks from museums and private collec­
tions, many pieces were exhibited in a propaganda display orchestrated by
the German Reich in Munich. This exhibition aimed to vilify the works
as manifestations of “Jewish Bolshevism” or the products of “mad incom­
petents.” With the escalation of artist persecution in Europe, an outpour­
ing of support emerged from across the Atlantic. Notable German artists
and intellectuals such as Anni (1899-1994) and Josef Albers (1888-1976),
George Grosz (1893-1959), and Erwin Panofsky (1892-1968) sought ref­
uge in the United States. American newspapers extensively covered Hitler’s
regime’s totalitarian cultural policies, with headlines like “Modernism is
No Verboten” appearing in the New York Times in July 1937.53
    The reports sparked concern among collectors and emerging muse­
ums in New York specializing in modern European art, recognizing a
significant threat to an important artistic tradition. Abstract art gained
unprecedented recognition, with many  critics, including Hilla von


## PDF page 178
172                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge


Rebay, asserting that art freed from representational constraints reached
the pinnacle of artistic expression. In March 1936, while Rebay show­
cased over a hundred nonrepresentational pieces from the Solomon R.
Guggenheim collection, the Museum of Modern Art launched a sig­
nificant exhibition titled From Cubism to Abstract Art. However, the
proximity of these institutions fostered competition among passionate
advocates of abstraction, who struggled to reach a unified critical under­
standing of “non-representation.”54
     Alfred H. Barr Jr. (1902-1981), the inaugural director of the Museum
of Modern Art, diverged significantly from Rebay’s perspective. He pri­
oritized form and medium potential over spiritual connotations or soul­
ful evolution in art. Barr categorized abstract art into two streams: one
grounded in reason and logic, comparable to mathematics and architecture
(termed “Apollonian”), and the other rooted in emotion and irrational­
ity, resembling music (“Dionysian”). While acknowledging the mystical
undertones in the latter, Barr steered clear of such discussions, empha­
sizing instead the surface evolution of abstraction from Impressionism
to Cubism and beyond. His modernist framework excluded factors like
Theosophy, Anthroposophy, politics, or gender equality movements from
the narrative of artistic progress. Notably, the “painting and sculpture” sec­
tion of From Cubism to Abstract Art featured no works by female artists,
and Barr omitted any mention of Rebay in the exhibition catalog.55
   Among the abstract artists still residing in Europe, Kandinsky stood
out as one of the earliest to recognize the significance of the developments
unfolding in New York. At the time, Kandinsky was living in a Parisian
suburb, having faced setbacks such as the closure of the Bauhaus by the
Germans in both Weimar and Dessau. His art sales had slowed, espe­
cially in France where collectors leaned towards cubism and surrealism,
failing to appreciate the spiritual and nonobjective nature of his work.
Despite his efforts, Kandinsky found little appreciation in his new envi­
ronment. In 1935, he wrote his New York gallerist, Israel Ber Neumann
(1887-1961), to Moscow to retrieve artworks from his former studio,
including a pivotal piece dating back to 1911, which he believed to be
stashed away without proper documentation.56 He sought photographic


## PDF page 179
Contemporary Abstract Elite: Upheld by the Art Establishment         173


evidence of its existence for a specific purpose, as Kandinsky proclaimed,
“It is truly the very first abstract painting in the world, as at that point
not a single other painter was making abstract work.”57
     Kandinsky’s perception of being the sole pioneer of abstraction was
not accurate, as he was cognizant of the broader context surrounding
abstract art. His involvement in the Blaue Reiter Exhibition in Munich
in 1911 occurred amidst existing discussions on non-representation,
with publications like Thought-Forms and works by artists like Katharine
Schäffner (1884-c.1931) already exploring similar themes. Furthermore,
Kandinsky had observed mediums in Munich producing non-natural­
istic artworks, indicating that the field of non-representation was not
entirely novel. However, formal documentation of abstraction’s history
was still in its early stages, gaining more attention in the mid-thirties,
particularly in New York.58
   Who could dispute the changing landscape of the art world? Back
in 1911, Munich was abuzz with the term “spiritual” as a cornerstone
of avant-garde art, leading to the emergence of “non-representation” as
a significant artistic outcome—a departure from the mere depiction of
nature’s material form. Meanwhile, across the Atlantic in New York,
“abstraction” was a hot topic closely linked to “non-representation,” albeit
with a focus on visible forms, as per Barr’s interpretation. Recognizing
the shift, Kandinsky acknowledged the impossibility of spearheading a
spiritual movement similar to his and Franz Marc’s (1880-1916) vision
in 1912. Now, a quarter-century later, he sought to assert his leadership
in a movement being shaped in New York.
     This shift in the definition of modern art, according to Barr, would
later influence the reception of af Klint’s work as well. Meanwhile, in
Munich, the National Socialists were actively seeking to discredit his
legacy, branding some of his work with derogatory labels like “Crazy at
All Costs” in the Degenerate Art Exhibition.59 However, it was Barr’s
perspectives and those shared by similar scholars that predominantly
molded the canon. Over the ensuing decades, abstraction was primarily
characterized as a formalist endeavor, with its pioneers recognized as
Kandinsky, Mondrian, Malevich, and Kupka.60

6
                 Conclusion
     xploring Hilma af Klint’s artistic trajectory and the intricate inter­
     play between science and spirituality within her oeuvre becameEapparent. From the early manifestations of visionary work in Drawings
to the Temple, of which the Tree of Knowledge is a part, to later evolution
seen in the Atom series, her art transcends conventional dichotomies,
offering a profound synthesis of disparate realms.
    Throughout this examination of her transformative body of work,
the life and work of af Klint extends far beyond the confines of tra­
ditional artistic categorization, serving as a testament to the enduring
power of creative expression to move beyond the limitations of the time
and inspire new areas of thought. Further research could further exam­
ine af Klint’s art in contemporary contexts, exploring its impact on cur­
rent artistic practices and philosophical discourse, as well as investigating
previously unexplored and undocumented areas of her life.
     Additional exploration into the contributions of The Five and Anna
Cassel, along with any potential involvement of other artists in creating
earlier images of Drawings to the Temple is warranted. Although af Klint
emerged as the predominant artist, it is conceivable that others may have
contributed to the paintings. Exploring archival materials, correspon­
dences, af Klint’s documentation in her notebooks as well as other histori­
cal records could help unveil insights into the roles and contributions of
the women associated with The Five, providing a more nuanced under­
standing of their collective creative process. Furthermore, while af Klint
was not a part of the art scene, additional comparative analysis with con­
temporaneous artists and movements could elucidate the unique aspects
of af Klint’s visionary practice and its broader cultural and artistic context.

                           177


## PDF page 184
178                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

     Cassel in particular, one of the women who was a member of The
Five and studied at the Royal Academy of Fine Arts in Stockholm with
af Klint requires further study of her work. In her notebooks, af Klint
documented the collaborative nature of the group but did not go into
great detail about how exactly the group functioned as a collective. Kurt
Almqvist and Daniel Birnbaum bring forth a starting point for inves­
tigating Cassel’s involvement in the images, discovering her notebooks
within the archives of the Anthroposophical Society, as they discuss in
Anna Cassel: The Saga of the Rose (2023). However, it is acknowledged
that along with bringing recognition to Cassel as an artist in her own
right, further investigation is needed in studying the paintings of af Klint
and whether collaboration occurred in making the images.1
     Additionally, the parallels between Hilma af Klint and Wassily
Kandinsky are intriguing. Although there is no evidence to suggest that
af Klint and Kandinsky ever met in person, they shared common inter­
ests and connections, particularly through their relationship with Rudolf
Steiner. Both artists were influenced by Steiner’s spiritual teachings of
Theosophy and Anthroposophy, which emphasized the connections
between spiritual and material worlds. Kandinsky had a direct connec­
tion to Steiner, also attending his lectures and incorporating his ideas
into his artistic practice, much like af Klint. Similarly, af Klint corre­
sponded with Steiner and shared images of her work with him, indicat­
ing a mutual interest in exploring spiritual themes through art.
    Although Kandinsky visited Sweden, there is no documentation
they crossed paths. Nonetheless their shared interest in spirituality, eso­
tericism, and exploring unseen realms that informed their artistic jour­
neys is worth examining further as there are questions from many schol­
ars about whether Kandinsky was influenced by af Klint, having perhaps
seen her paintings through Steiner.
    Another area of interest is af Klint’s meditation practices, suggest­
ing a form of transcendental meditation ahead of its time. Even though
af Klint left behind extensive notebooks documenting her spiritual jour­
ney and artistic process, the specific techniques, and rituals she employed
during meditation remain relatively unexplored.


## PDF page 185
Conclusion                       179

    Studying af Klint’s writings could offer valuable insights into
the nature of her meditative experiences, processes such as becoming
vegetarian, and other practices and how they informed her creations.
Additionally, comparative studies with contemporary spiritual practices,
including those rooted in Hinduism and Buddhism, and meditation
techniques may shed light on the unique aspects of her approach and its
significance within the broader context of spiritualism and esotericism
in the early twentieth century. Studying the potential benefits of medita­
tion for artistic inspiration and creativity, along with underlying beliefs
and philosophical foundations that could provide valuable perspectives
on her work. Research on meditative practices and their effects on con­
sciousness, cognitive function, emotional well-being, and heightened
awareness and how these influences could provide more insight into af
Klint’s inner exploration and forms of expression.
    Theosophy  and  Anthroposophy  were  primarily  focused  on
throughout this research as this is what much of the information avail­
able is based on, however, af Klint was also very much influenced by the
Rosicrucian Order. Although this is discussed briefly in this study, more
research is needed into both af Klint’s involvement in Rosicrucianism
as well as the extent her images reflect their teachings. It is said that af
Klint wore a necklace with a cross and rose like the symbolism of the
Rosicrucians that represents the resurrection and redemption of Christ,
claiming esoteric powers.
    Af Klint was excited by Steiner’s idea of Rosicrucian Theosophy
and sat in on his lectures discussing these ideas, however, the informa­
tion documented by af Klint is vague. Furthermore, in af Klint’s Tree of
Knowledge, there are parallels as seen in the Rosicrucian secret symbol­
ism such as Figura Divina (seventeenth century), which consists of tri­
angles and circles with varying layers moving downward from the divine
to the spirit, to the soul and then the lower part representing the body.
These levels of spiritual to material are reminiscent of the visual language
and notes from both the Tree of Knowledge and the Atom series.
    Af Klint’s Tree of Knowledge series serves as a powerful symbol
of spiritual enlightenment and cosmic interconnectedness. Drawing


## PDF page 186
180                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

inspiration from esoteric traditions and mystical teachings, af Klint
imbues these paintings with layers of symbolic meaning, evoking ancient
concepts such as the tree of life as found in various spiritual and reli­
gious traditions. In her interpretation as studied in this research, the
tree represents not only the pursuit of knowledge and wisdom but also
the interconnectedness of all existence, with its roots emerging into the
unseen realms of the consciousness and its branches reaching towards
the heavens. This symbolism is like that of the Tree of Life and its uni­
versal symbolism of creation, growth, and renewal that reflects af Klint’s
spiritual insights and her belief in the fundamental unity of all existence;
parallels that should be further explored.
     Additionally, a more in-depth understanding of the  scientific
aspect of this research must be further investigated, particularly the
realm of quantum physics and the quantum field may provide more par­
allels to af Klint’s work, especially in her Atom series. As discussed in this
research, quantum theory resonates with af Klint’s exploration of the
unseen, interconnected dimensions of reality. Just as quantum mechan­
ics challenges traditional Newtonian physics by revealing the complex
and interconnected nature of the universe, af Klint’s art transcends con­
ventional artistic boundaries by looking deeper into the spiritual and
metaphysical realms.2
    The Atom series contains intricate geometric forms that evoke the
underlying concepts of higher consciousness. By integrating insights
from scientific research into her artistic practice, af Klint created a unique
visual language that merges science and spirituality, offering ideas about
the deeper mysteries of existence. Further research is needed, primarily
from physicists to explore the intersection between af Klint’s art and
scientific principles, particularly in the context of quantum mechanics,
to uncover more about the meaning behind her images.
     Finally, the discovery of animation-like qualities in af Klint’s Atom
series, as revealed through this research, raises intriguing questions about
the intentionality behind her work and its relationship to contemporary
scientific and cultural developments. Whether deliberate, subconscious,
or influenced by exposure to early cinema and the study of moving


## PDF page 187
Conclusion                       181

images, the dynamic interplay of shapes in the Atom, suggests the con­
cept of movement and transformation. This resonates with the evolving
understanding of the atom in the early twentieth century, where scientific
discoveries of the time hinted at the dynamic nature of atomic particles
and their behavior. Moreover, the exploration of quantum physics and
concepts of the four-dimensional plane adds another layer of complex­
ity to af Klint’s artistic exploration, echoing Plato’s “The Allegory of the
Cave” and the quest for deeper truths beyond the confines of conven­
tional perception. Further investigation into these themes could provide
a more comprehensive understanding of af Klint’s visionary practices
and their resonance with contemporary scientific paradigms.
    As this research concludes an examination of Hilma af Klint’s
groundbreaking work, it becomes evident that there is still much more
to uncover and understand about her artistic vision and spiritual insights.
Significant progress has been made in recent years to shed light on af
Klint’s contributions to Abstract Art and her pioneering journey of
intersecting science and spirituality, yet, there remain numerous unan­
swered questions and unexplored avenues for further research.
    The complexity of af Klint’s oeuvre, with its intricate symbolism
and esoteric references, calls for an interdisciplinary collaboration and
innovative approaches to interpretation. In years to come, more audi­
ences of the future may find themselves increasingly drawn to af Klint’s
enigmatic diagrams and visual language. In this, there is an opportunity
to engage with af Klint’s work in new ways, leveraging twenty-first-
century advances in technology, science, scholarship, and consciousness
studies that can help reveal the mysteries she left behind. Embracing af
Klint’s vision of a harmonious confluence of science and spirituality as
seen in the examples of the Tree of Knowledge and the Atom series, future
research can potentially contribute to a deeper understanding of what
she believed are the fundamental truths that unite humanity and all of
existence.

## PDF page 189
Afterword

Rachel Villari’s book Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge is a
beautiful dialogue between art, natural science and spirituality. She pres­
ents how natural science and various forms of spirituality are intertwined
in Hilma af Klint’s symbolic works. The book presents ardent research
of the literature that has been written about af Klint and her symbolic
works. In addition, Villari has also presented to us how the various mat­
ters are treated and referred to in af Klint’s notebooks. And all this in
a book with a language that flows lightly and increases the curiosity to
learn more.
      Villari presents Hilma af Klint as a strong-willed person with a
good education in conventional painting and drawing at the Academy of
Fine Art in Stockholm. She became spiritually developed during her ten-
year session with The Five, in order to be ready in 1906 to grasp the brush
and to start her non-figurative painting odyssey. In this endeavor, she
painted her symbolic works in series. She planned to show the Drawings
to the Temple to the public in a Temple building erected in circles in ever
higher planes. The visitors should go from one hall to another—being
exposed to different developments until they eventually enter he highest
plane and reach the tower in the center.
      Villari presents af Klint’s symbolic works of the period 1906–1920
to be based on three main aspects—i.e., cartography, natural science,
and philosophy & religions. The latter were mainly Rosicrucianism,
Theosophy, and an esoteric form of Christianity. In addition, Villari
states that Hilma af Klint had basically completed all her symbolic
works by the end of 1920, when she went to Dornach and joined the
Anthroposophical Society. This means that her symbolic works are not
based on Anthroposophy. In 1921, she studied Goethe’s Color Theory,
which became the base for her watercolor paintings in the “wet-on-wet”
technique from 1922. So, af Klint was extraordinary in the sense that
she painted in three different styles during her lifetime—conventional

                           183


## PDF page 190
184                   Hilma af Klint’s Atom & the Tree of Knowledge

paintings until 1906, non-figurative paintings during 1906–1920 and
“wet-on-wet” watercolor paintings during 1922–1941.
    Rachel  Villari emphasizes that  af  Klint’s symbolic works are
“sacred” and contain a “spiritual message to mankind from higher dimen­
sions”. Hilma af Klint even called them the Fifth Gospel. She presented
philosophical ideas and spiritual concepts in physical form on canvas for
us to contemplate and reflect upon. By going back and forth to the Astral
plane, she made the Invisible, Visible.
     In presenting this, af Klint embraced the connection of her botani­
cal motifs with decoration and abstraction. Villari presents this by means
of the series the Tree of Knowledge, which could be regarded as the
World Tree of the North Mythology—Yggdrasil. The Tree of Knowledge
encompasses the seven bodies of the cosmos. On the trunk of the Tree
of Knowledge, in its branches and in its root system, we may observe the
various phases of Evolution. All this takes place under the Holy Grail
with the infinity symbol “ꝏ” inside on the top of the Tree of Knowledge.
The Tree of Knowledge series connects the two portions of the Drawings
to the Temple painted 1906–1908 and 1912–1915, respectively.
    Hilma af Klint’s interest in natural science was the basis for her cre­
ation of the Atom series in 1916. The Atoms are presented in two versions,
with the physical atom and its energy at the lower bottom right of the
picture, while the upper left square of the picture illustrates the atom on
the Ethereal plane. When the atom is at rest on the Ether plane, energy
is drawn into its center and stored there. When the body is harmonious,
energy emanates from the center of the atoms, radiating outward from
them. Af Klint’s atoms are presented as squares, encased within circles,
creating a mandala-like configuration, emblematic of a state devoid of
conflict. As mentioned in the Atom picture HaK 354 “Every atom has
its center, but every center is directly connected to the center of the
universe.”
      Villari’s book presents Hilma af Klint’s work as reflecting the inter­
connectedness of the universe—exploring the duality of material and
spiritual dimensions.


## PDF page 191
Afterword                       185

      Villari also mentions that recent academic research has arrived to
the fact that the “spiritual message to mankind from higher dimensions”
inherent in Hilma af Klint’s symbolic works of the Drawings to the
Temple could well mean that we are leaving Materialism to encompass
Mentalism, where everything is based on consciousness (= All is Mind).
   And now, the same idea has been presented in natural science
by means of Maria Strømme’s article of 13th November 2025 titled
“Universal consciousness as foundational field: A theoretical bridge between
quantum physics and non-dual philosophy”, which states that consciousness
is the very foundation of existence.
     This is interesting, as the Neoplatonism of Saccas and Plotinus from
the third century B.C. indicates that consciousness encompasses every­
thing and gives it life. In addition, the Chinese Buddhist school Yogācāra
Cittamātra from the fourth century A.D. states that reality does not exist
external from the mind and that everything is a product of the mind—i.e.
All is Mind.
    So now, over a hundred years after the creation of Hilma af Klint’s
symbolic paintings in the Drawings to the Temple, natural science proves
the same thing, i.e.,
            Everything is based on consciousness—All is Mind
                                         * * *
With these few remarks, I would like to emphasize how important
Rachel Villari’s book is. With its beautiful language and its professional
documentation, the various aspects simply “flow to” the reader and evoke
his interest. This book will be a source for future academic research about
Hilma af Klint, her symbolic works, and her time.
                                            Johan af Klint, PhD
